The candle cast shifting shadows across Ivy's face, softening the severity of her features. "You've been gathering this information for months. You said so yourself. Why risk everything to help me?"

Ivy was quiet for a long moment. When she spoke, her voice was barely audible.

"Because I know what it's like to lose someone and have everyone pretend they never existed."

The raw pain in her words cut through Drevik's suspicion. He waited, giving her space to continue.

"My brother. Three years older than me. He was a Warden—one of the youngest ever promoted to that rank. Brilliant, talented, everyone said he'd be an Elder someday." Ivy's hand dropped from his arm, and she turned away, adjusting her glasses in a gesture that seemed more about having something to do with her hands than any practical need. "He discovered something in the archives, too. Not about the breaches—I don't know exactly what. He never told me. But I watched him change over the course of six months. Growing more paranoid, more afraid. And then one night he walked into the Shadowlands and didn't walk out."

"Ivy..."

"They said it was suicide. That the pressure of his position broke him, that he simply gave up." She turned back to face him, her jaw set with determination that transformed her nervous energy into something fierce. "But I knew my brother. He didn't give up. He found something, just like your mother did, and it got him killed. And I've spent every day since then trying to figure out what it was."

The chamber felt smaller suddenly, the walls pressing in. Drevik was acutely aware of Ivy's proximity, the way the candlelight caught the curve of her cheek, the intensity in her eyes that had nothing to do with scholarly interest. She was sharing more than information—she was sharing a wound, one that matched his own.

"You think the same people are responsible," he said. "The ones who killed my mother."

"I think the breaches, your mother's death, my brother—it's all connected. The pattern she identified twenty-two years ago? It's happening again." Ivy moved back to the desk, pulling out a rolled parchment covered in dates and symbols. "I've been tracking breach reports for the past six months. The timing matches the lunar cycle exactly, but that's not the significant part. Look at the locations."

Drevik unrolled the parchment, his eyes scanning the marked positions on a hand-drawn map. Each breach was labeled with a date and a location—and every single one had occurred within a mile of the university campus.

"They're getting closer."

"They're converging. Whatever they're doing, whoever is creating these breaches, they're building toward something. And the center of the pattern—" Ivy pointed to a spot on the map, marked with a red circle. "—is right here. The compound beneath the university. The heart of the Order's power."

A chill ran through Drevik that had nothing to do with the cold air. His mother had been right. Someone in the Order was deliberately weakening the veil—and they were using the Order's own headquarters as the focal point.

"Why didn't anyone else notice this? The Elders, the Wardens—"

"Maybe they have." Ivy's voice dropped even lower. "Maybe some of them are in on it. Or maybe they've just been told not to look too closely. Either way, you need to be careful. You're an acolyte, which means you have access to parts of the compound that initiates don't. People talk around you. They assume you're too new, too unimportant to matter. If you keep your eyes open, you might see things that others miss."

Drevik looked at the map again, then at the stack of documents Ivy had assembled. His mother's notes. The breach pattern. The letter that had never been delivered. It was more than he'd learned in weeks of training, more than anyone had ever told him about the Order or his own family.

And Ivy had been collecting it all, piece by piece, for months. For him.

"Why me specifically?" he asked. "You could have taken this to someone with more authority. A Warden, an Elder—"

"And watched them bury it, like they buried everything else?" Ivy shook her head. "No. You're an unknown quantity. You're outside the power structure, outside the families that have controlled the Order for generations. And you have a personal stake in this. Your mother—" She hesitated, then stepped closer again. "You deserve to know the truth. And maybe... maybe you're the one who can finally do something about it."

The air between them thicken. Drevik was suddenly aware of how alone they were in this hidden chamber, surrounded by secrets that could get them both killed. Ivy's bravery, her months of careful research, her willingness to trust him with knowledge she'd risked everything to uncover—it was more than professional courtesy. More than shared interest.

He reached out, brushing a strand of hair from her face. The gesture was impulsive, driven by something he didn't fully understand, but Ivy didn't pull away. Her breath held, her eyes widening behind her glasses.

"Drevik..."

"I know." His voice was rough. "I know this isn't—I know we're in the middle of something dangerous. But I need you to know that I understand what you're risking. And I'm not going to let it be for nothing."

Ivy's hand came up to cover his, pressing his palm against her cheek. The contact sent a jolt through him—warmth and shadow mixing in his blood, his power responding to the closeness of another magical being. Her eyes fluttered closed for just a moment, and when she opened them again, there was something new in her expression. Something that looked like hope.

"I've been so alone with this," she said. "For three years, I've been gathering pieces of a puzzle I couldn't solve by myself. And then you walked into the initiation ceremony, and I saw your name, and I thought—maybe this is it. Maybe this is the moment everything changes."

"It will." Drevik's thumb traced the line of her jaw. "Whatever's happening in the Order, whatever killed my mother and your brother—we're going to find out. And we're going to stop it."

Ivy leaned into his touch for a moment longer, then reluctantly pulled back. The practical part of her reasserted itself, the nervous scholar returning to the surface.

"We should go through the rest of these notes quickly. I've already extended the ward bypass longer than I should have—"

A sound from above made them both freeze.

Footsteps. Heavy, deliberate, moving through the stacks above their heads.

Drevik's heart slammed against his ribs. "Did anyone follow you?"

"I don't think so. I was careful, I—" Ivy's face went pale. "The shadow alarm. I disabled the primary wards, but there's a secondary system that triggers if someone accesses the metal boxes. I didn't think anyone would—"

Another set of footsteps joined the first. Voices, muffled but audible through the stone.

"—definitely came this way. The wards lit up like a festival display."

"Who has access this time of night?"

"That's what we're about to find out."

Ivy moved quickly, shoving documents into Drevik's hands. "Take these. Your mother's notes, the breach map, the letter—everything that proves the pattern. Hide them in your room, somewhere the searches won't find them."

"What about you?"

"I'll stay. Cover our tracks, make it look like I was doing independent research. I have permission to be in the archives—I can explain away a late-night study session." Her hands trembled as she gathered the remaining papers. "You don't have that luxury. If they find you here, especially with these documents—"

"I'm not leaving you to face them alone."

"Drevik." Ivy grabbed his arm, her grip surprisingly strong. "You don't understand. If they find out what you know, what you're carrying, they'll kill you. Not officially, not publicly—but you'll disappear, just like your mother. Just like my brother. And then everything I've done, everything I've gathered, will be for nothing."

The footsteps were closer now, descending the spiral stairs. Drevik heard the creak of leather, the soft clink of metal—the sounds of Wardens in full gear.

"Ivy—"

"Go." She pushed him toward the back of the chamber, where the shadows pooled thickest. "Use your gift. Walk through the shadows. Get out of here."

"You said there were wards—"

"The wards are disabled, and the shadow alarm only tracks physical movement through the archives. If you walk through shadow, you'll bypass everything." Her eyes were bright with fear, but her voice held steady. "I'll find you tomorrow. We'll figure out the next step. Just go."

Drevik clutched the documents against his chest. He heard the Wardens' voices clearly now, calling out to each other as they reached the bottom of the stairs.

Ivy shoved him hard, her hands against his chest. "Go. Now."

He reached for the shadows, felt them respond to his will, wrapping around him like cold water. But even as the darkness swallow him, he saw Ivy turn toward the chamber entrance, straightening her spine, arranging her face into an expression of innocent confusion.

And then the shadows took him, and the world fell away.

He emerged in his dorm room, the documents still clutched against his chest, his heart pounding so hard he felt it in his throat. The familiar surroundings felt surreal after the hidden chamber, the candlelight, the weight of secrets revealed.

Drevik moved to his bed and began pulling up the loose floorboard he'd discovered his first week—a hiding spot he'd never thought he'd need. His hands shook as he placed the documents inside, covering them with a spare shirt, replacing the board, spreading his rug back over the spot.

Then he sat on his bed, his head in his hands, and tried to process everything he'd learned.

His mother had been murdered. Someone in the Order was creating breaches deliberately. The attacks were converging on the compound. And Ivy—quiet, nervous Ivy who worked in the archives and helped him with his translation exercises—had been carrying the weight of that knowledge alone, for months, waiting for someone she could trust.

He thought about the way she'd looked at him in the chamber. The way she'd leaned into his touch. The fear in her eyes when she'd heard the Wardens approaching—not for herself, but for him.

She's been alone with this for three years. Just like I've been alone since my grandmother died.

Drevik stood, pacing the small room. He wanted to go back, to make sure Ivy was safe, to stand beside her when she faced the Wardens. But he knew she was right. If they found him with the documents, everything would be lost. His only chance to help her was to stay hidden, to preserve the evidence, to wait for her to contact him tomorrow.

Assuming she could.

Assuming they didn't arrest her, or worse.

Someone in the Order is creating breaches deliberately.

His mother had discovered the same pattern twenty-two years ago. She'd tried to report it, and she'd vanished. Now it was happening again, and the people responsible were still in position, still pulling strings, still killing anyone who got too close to the truth.

Drevik thought about Lexi, about the way she'd looked at him during training—suspicious, challenging, but with something else beneath it. He thought about the other acolytes, the initiates, the Wardens and Elders who moved through the compound with their secrets and their power struggles.

How many of them knew? How many of them were complicit?

And how could he possibly know who to trust?

A knock at his door made him freeze.

He counted to three, forcing his expression into something neutral, before opening it.

Lexi stood in the hallway, her auburn hair loose around her shoulders, her green eyes sharp with something he couldn't read. She was still dressed in her training clothes—fitted pants, a sleeveless tunic that showed the defined muscles of her arms.

"Drevik." Her voice was carefully controlled. "You're awake late."

"So are you."

She didn't smile. "There's been a security incident in the archives. Someone triggered the shadow alarm. The Wardens are investigating."

Drevik kept his face still. "That sounds serious."

"It is." Lexi's eyes searched his face, looking for something. "They've detained one of the archive workers. A woman named Ivy. Do you know her?"

Detained.

The word hit him hard. He let a flicker of confusion cross his face—not hard to manage, given the circumstances.

"Ivy? She's helped me with some translations. I wouldn't say I know her well. Why?"

Lexi was quiet for a long moment. When she spoke again, her voice had softened slightly, though her expression remained guarded.

"She was found in the restricted section, past the disabled wards. The Wardens think she was stealing information." Another pause, weighted with meaning. "They're questioning her now. But I thought you should know. In case you have any... insight."

Drevik forced himself to hold Lexi's gaze. "I don't know why she would do something like that. She always seemed dedicated to the Order."

"People are not always what they seem." Lexi's voice carried an edge. "Sometimes they hide their true purposes. Sometimes they let others take risks for them while they stay safe in the shadows."

The accusation in her words was clear. But was she accusing him of being involved, or warning him that Ivy had taken a fall for someone else's benefit?

"I appreciate you telling me," he said. "If there's anything I can do to help—"

"There isn't." Lexi stepped back, her expression closing off. "This is Warden business now. The best thing you can do is stay out of it. Focus on your training. Don't draw attention."

She turned to leave, then paused, looking back over her shoulder.

"Whatever you're involved in, Drevik, be careful. The Order doesn't take kindly to acolytes who overstep. And I'd hate to see you make a mistake you can't come back from."

She walked away before he could respond, her footsteps fading down the corridor.

Drevik closed his door and leaned against it, his pulse quickening. Ivy was in custody. The Wardens were questioning her. And Lexi—whatever her motivations—had just given him a warning that felt far more significant than a casual concern for a fellow acolyte.

He thought about the documents hidden under his floor. About his mother's research, the pattern of breaches, the conspiracy that had killed her and countless others.

This is bigger than I understood. Bigger than I'm ready for.

But he couldn't walk away now. Ivy had risked everything to bring him this information, to trust him with secrets she'd carried alone for years. He wouldn't let her sacrifice be meaningless.

Tomorrow, he would find a way to help her. He would begin unraveling the truth about his mother's death. And he would discover who in the Order was worth trusting—and who wanted him dead.

Drevik climbed into bed, but he knew sleep wouldn't come. The shadows in his room seemed deeper than usual, and he couldn't stop thinking about Ivy's face as she'd pushed him toward safety—brave, terrified, and utterly alone.

He wouldn't let her stay that way.

Not if he could help it.

Chapter 5: Breach Point

Chapter 5: Breach Point

The scream cut through his sleep like a blade through silk.

Drevik's eyes snapped open, his body already moving before his mind caught up. The dorm room was dark—too dark. The shadows pooling in the corners weren't natural. They writhed and pulsed with a rhythm that made his stomach clench.

His phone buzzed on the nightstand. Then again. Then a third time in rapid succession.

Emergency. Science Building. Now.

The text glowed on his screen, sent from a number he'd memorized during his initiate days. Elder Thornwood's personal line.

Drevik was out of bed and pulling on his jeans before the fourth buzz came. His chest tightened against his ribs as he grabbed his jacket—the one with the concealed pockets holding his ritual components—and shoved his feet into boots without bothering with socks.

The hallway outside his door was chaos. Students streamed toward the exits, some crying, others shouting into phones. The alarm system hadn't triggered, but something had these people running. Animal instinct, maybe. The same instinct that made prey animals flee before the predator stepped into view.

He caught a glimpse of Mira's door as he passed. Already open. Already empty.

Good. She was smart. She'd have gotten out.

The night air hit him like a slap when he burst through the ground floor exit. Cold and wrong, carrying a smell that didn't belong on a college campus—rotting vegetation and something metallic underneath. Copper and decay.

The science building loomed three hundred yards away, its modern glass facade reflecting emergency lights that flickered in an irregular pattern. Blue and red strobes that created a strobing effect making his vision swim.

Drevik broke into a run.

He made it fifty yards before a hand grabbed his arm and yanked him sideways into the shadows between buildings.

"Wait."

Lexi's voice was low and controlled, but he heard the tension underneath. Her auburn hair was pulled back in a tight braid, and she wore tactical clothes that looked nothing like what a college student should own. Black cargo pants, fitted top, boots that had seen actual combat.

"What's happening?" He didn't fight her grip. She was stronger than she looked—Order training did that to people.

"Breach." Her green eyes met his, and for a moment he saw something beneath the professional mask. Fear, maybe. Or something else. "Science building. Multiple entities. The ward matrix collapsed about fifteen minutes ago."

"How many acolytes are responding?"

"Everyone within a five-mile radius. But Drevik—" She tightened her grip on his arm. "The breach isn't contained. It's growing."

He processed that. A breach that grew meant the barrier between realms was destabilizing. Meant whatever had punctured through was still actively tearing the wound wider.

"Ivy," he said suddenly. "She was being questioned. Where—"

"Safe. For now." Lexi released his arm and stepped back. "Elder Voss has her in a secure location. But that's not why I grabbed you."

The way she said it made cold settle in his stomach. "Then why?"

"Because I need you to trust me." Her jaw tightened. "And because I'm about to do something that might get us both in serious trouble."

Before he could ask what she meant, she grabbed his hand. Her fingers intertwined with his, palm to palm, and he felt the familiar tingle of shadow magic responding to contact with another practitioner.

"We're shadow-walking into the building," she said. "The main doors are being watched by entities that feed on fear. They'll sense any acolyte coming and scatter. But if we go through the shadows—"

"We can get past them." He finished her thought. "Lexi, that's risky. If the breach has corrupted the shadow paths—"

"It has." Her expression was grim. "That's why I need your help. Your talent with shadow-walking is stronger than mine. If we link, I can guide while you stabilize."

It was against protocol. Against everything they'd been taught about breach response. Acolytes weren't supposed to engage directly with uncontained entities. They were supposed to support, contain, and let the Wardens handle the actual threat.

But the Wardens weren't here yet. And every minute they waited, the breach grew.

"Show me the path," he said.

Lexi's smile was brief and fierce. She squeezed his hand once, then pulled him into the shadow of the building beside them.

The world inverted.

Walking through shadow was like being underwater without the pressure. Colors bled into grayscale, sounds became muffled echoes, and time moved strangely—stretching and compressing in alternating waves. Drevik had done this hundreds of times since his initiation, but never into an active breach zone.

The corruption hit him immediately.

It was like oil sliding across his skin. Dark and slick and wrong. The shadow paths that usually felt cool and welcoming now had an infection running through them, something that made his magical senses scream in protest.

Hold, Lexi's voice came through their linked hands. Not spoken aloud—impossible in the shadow realm—but projected through their connection. I can see the contamination. It's coming from the northeast corner of the building. Guide us around it.

He closed his eyes—not that it mattered in this place—and focused on the shadow paths. They spread out before his awareness like a network of veins, some clear and bright, others clouded with the same oily corruption. The breach was poisoning everything it touched.

Drevik pulled them left, then down. The shadow realm had its own geography, one that didn't always match the physical world. A straight line in reality might require a dozen turns through shadow.

There. Lexi's mental voice carried a note of triumph. I can see the exit point. Science building, second floor. The entity concentration is lower there.

They materialized in a hallway that should have been familiar but looked like a war zone. Lockers had been torn from walls, their contents scattered across blood-smeared tile. The fluorescent lights flickered overhead, casting the corridor in alternating pools of yellow and shadow.

Blood. Not a lot, but enough. Someone had been hurt.

"Stay close," Lexi said. Her hand found his again, but this time she was pulling a blade from the sheath at her hip. The metal was dark and drink the light around it—Order-forged steel, designed to cut through entities that weren't entirely physical.

They moved through the hallway in a crouch, checking corners with practiced efficiency. Drevik's shadow senses were stretched thin, reaching out for any sign of the entities that had come through the breach. He felt them—vague shapes of hunger and malice—but they seemed concentrated elsewhere. Drawn to something.

"The main lab," Lexi said. "That's where the breach is centered. And that's where the entities are feeding."

"Feeding on what?"

She didn't answer. Her expression told him she didn't want to guess.

They reached the stairwell without incident, but the moment Drevik's foot touched the first step, the temperature plummeted. His breath misted in front of his face, and the shadows in the corners of the stairwell move.

Not naturally. Not randomly.

Something was pulling them together, gathering darkness into a shape that shouldn't exist.

"Lexi."

"I see it." Her blade came up, the dark steel catching what little light remained. "Don't engage. We go around. Fast and quiet."

But the shadow-thing was already forming. A torso, elongated and wrong. Limbs that bent in too many places. A face that was barely a face—just a suggestion of features around a mouth that gaped too wide.

It pulled itself out of the corner like something climbing out of a pool, and when it saw them—saw them, though it had no eyes—its mouth stretched wider in what might have been a smile.

Drevik felt the fear hit him like a wave. Not his own fear—the entity's. It was projecting, pushing terror into his mind, trying to freeze him in place so it could feed.

Don't listen to it. Lexi's grip on his hand tightened painfully. This is what they do. They make you feel things that aren't yours.

He knew that. The Wardens had drilled it into every acolyte during training. But knowing and resisting were different things when the fear was clawing at the inside of his skull, when every instinct was screaming at him to run or freeze or—

Lexi moved.

She didn't run. She didn't freeze. She stepped forward and drove her blade through the thing's chest in a motion so fast Drevik barely tracked it.

The entity shrieked—a sound that existed somewhere between hearing and feeling—and dissolved into wisps of shadow that scattered like smoke.

"Move," Lexi ordered, already pulling him down the stairs. "That will have drawn attention."

They ran. The next floor was worse—more blood, more damage, and the shadows themselves press inward like a living thing. Drevik felt the breach now, a wound in reality that throbbed with wrongness. It was close.

They found the source in the main laboratory.

The room was a disaster—overturned tables, shattered equipment, and in the center, a tear in reality that looked like someone had cut a hole in the air itself. Through it, Drevik saw the Shadowlands. Twilight sky. Twisted trees. Movement in the distance that suggested things coming to investigate the opening.

Three Wardens stood in a triangle around the breach, hands raised, chanting in a language that predated human speech. Sweat beaded on their faces. One of them—the woman leading the ritual—had blood running from her nose.

"Containment team is on their way," one of the men said without breaking his chant. "We're holding it open but we can't close it. Something's anchoring it from the other side."

Drevik's stomach dropped. "Anchoring it?"

"Something came through." The lead Warden's voice was strained. "Several somethings. We can't close the breach while they're still in this realm. They're using the opening to feed, and every minute they're here, the tear gets wider."

That was why the entities had been drawn elsewhere. They weren't just randomly attacking—they were spreading out, hunting, feeding. And every person they touched made the breach worse.

"We need to find them," Lexi said. "All of them. And we need to do it before—"

"Before more students die," Drevik finished. "How many came through?"

The Warden's silence was answer enough.

They split up. Lexi took the east wing, Drevik took the west. It went against every protocol—acolytes weren't supposed to hunt alone—but there weren't enough Wardens to cover the building, and every second they waited, the situation deteriorated.

Drevik moved through the corridors with his shadow senses stretched to their limit. The fear-projecting entity in the stairwell had been a minor one. The things that came through a breach this size could be significantly more dangerous.

He found the first one in a bathroom.

It had cornered a student—a girl he vaguely recognized from his chemistry lecture—and was pressing her against the wall with tendrils of shadow that wrapped around her wrists and ankles. Her eyes were wide and vacant, her mouth open in a silent scream. The entity was feeding on her terror, and from the look of her, it had been feeding for a while.

Drevik didn't hesitate. He pulled the shadow knife from his jacket—the one every acolyte carried—and stepped into the room.

The entity turned. Its face was barely formed, just a suggestion of features, but he felt it assessing him. Deciding whether he was worth abandoning its current meal.

"Hey." His voice came out steadier than he felt. "You want something to eat? Try me."

The girl's eyes moved—just barely—but the entity didn't release her. It was smarter than the one in the stairwell. Cautious.

Drevik reached for his shadow-walking ability, the talent that had marked him as unusual since his initiation. He felt the shadows in the room, sensed the entity's connection to them. It was using shadow to maintain its form, shadow to bind its prey.

If he could disrupt that connection...

He reached out with his mind and pulled.

The entity shrieked as the shadows that composed its form unraveled. It wasn't destruction—the thing was still there, still dangerous—but it was distracted, trying to pull itself back together, and Drevik was already moving.

His knife found the center of its mass three times in rapid succession. Not Order-forged steel like Lexi's blade, but enough to disrupt the entity's cohesion. It dissolved into fragments that scattered across the ceiling and walls.

The girl collapsed. Drevik caught her before she hit the ground, lowering her gently to the tile. Her breathing was shallow, her pulse thready. Alive, but barely conscious.

He pulled out his phone and typed a quick message to the coordinating Warden: West bathroom, second floor. One student down, one entity dispersed. Medical needed.

The response came immediately: Confirmed. Three more entities detected. Continue sweep.

Three more. And that was just in this building. If the breach had been growing for fifteen minutes before anyone responded...

He left the girl in the bathroom and kept moving. The next entity was two corridors away—he felt its presence like a stain on his senses. This one was bigger. Stronger.

It had found another meal.

Drevik rounded the corner and stopped cold.

Mira stood in the center of the hallway, her back against the wall, her hands raised in a defensive posture. The entity facing her was massive—at least seven feet tall, with shadow-limbs that flow and reform constantly. It wasn't just feeding on fear. It was playing with her.

Toying with its food.

"Mira." Her name came out sharper than he intended. "Get down."

She didn't move. Her eyes were locked on the entity, and he saw the terror in them—real terror, not the projected kind. She'd seen something like this before. She knew what it could do.

The entity turned toward him, and Drevik felt the fear-wave hit him like a physical force. This one was stronger than the others. Older. More powerful.

Don't engage. We go around. Lexi's words echoed in his memory.

But there was no going around. Not with Mira in the thing's path.

He reached for his shadow-walking ability again, tried to pull at the entity's form like he had with the others. But this one resisted. It had better control of its shadows, more cohesion. It was anchored to the Shadowlands in a way the minor entities weren't.

Drevik's knife suddenly felt very small.

The entity lunged.

He moved on instinct—ducked under the first swipe, rolled away from the second. A tendril of shadow caught his shoulder and he felt cold spread through his arm like poison. His knife hand went numb.

"Your shadow-walking won't work on me, little acolyte." The entity's voice was a whisper that come from everywhere at once. "I've eaten a hundred of your kind."

It wasn't bluffing. He felt the truth in the words, sensed the weight of experience behind them. This thing had killed Order members before. Had fed on them.

Drevik's mind raced. His knife was useless. His shadow-walking was blocked. He was outmatched, outpaced, and running out of options.

But he wasn't alone.

Lexi's blade took the entity in the back.

She'd come out of nowhere—shadow-walked directly into the thing's shadow, which was apparently still possible even when direct manipulation failed. The Order-forged steel sank deep into the entity's mass, and it screamed. Actually screamed, a sound that rattled the windows and made Drevik's ears ring.

"Move!" Lexi shouted.

He didn't need to be told twice. He grabbed Mira's arm and pulled her toward the nearest exit while Lexi engaged the entity. The thing was thrashing, trying to reach the blade buried in its back, but Lexi was already moving—circling, striking, forcing it to split its attention.

Drevik glanced back once and saw her face in the flickering light. Focused. Fierce. Beautiful in a way that made his chest ache.

He pushed Mira through the exit door and turned back toward the fight.

Lexi was holding her own, but barely. The entity had recovered from the initial strike and was pressing hard. Its shadow-tendrils whipped through the air, forcing her to dodge and weave, and he saw her starting to slow. Starting to tire.

He reached for his shadow-walking ability again. Not to disrupt the entity—he'd already tried that—but to change the battlefield.

The shadows in the hallway responded to his pull. They deepened, spread, created pools of darkness where there hadn't been any before. Not much—just enough to give Lexi more weapons.

She understood immediately. Her next dodge ended in a shadow-walk, reappearing behind the entity for another strike. Then again. And again. Using the shadows he was creating, moving faster than the thing could track.

"End it!" Drevik called. "I can't hold this forever!"

Lexi drove her blade into the entity's core. The thing convulsed, its form destabilizing, and she twisted the blade before yanking it free. Shadow exploded outward, dissipating into nothing.

The hallway went quiet.

Lexi stood in the center of it, breathing hard, her blade dripping with something that wasn't quite blood. Her eyes found his across the space between them, and for a moment, neither of them moved.

Then she was walking toward him, and her hand was on his face, and she was checking him for injuries with a focus that made his heart stutter.

"You're hurt." Her fingers found the wound on his shoulder—the one the entity's tendril had touched. "This needs treatment. Shadow-taint. If it spreads—"

"I'm fine." He caught her wrist. "Lexi. I'm fine. Are you?"

She stared at him for a long moment. Her eyes were too bright, her jaw too tight. The fear she'd been suppressing was starting to show through the cracks.

"That was stupid," she said finally. "Trying to fight a greater shadow entity with a knife. What were you thinking?"

"I was thinking about Mira." He nodded toward the other woman, who was standing frozen by the exit door. "I couldn't leave her."

Lexi's expression flickered. He couldn't read it—there was too much happening behind her eyes. But her grip on his shoulder gentled.

"You need to be more careful." Her voice was quiet. "You're not expendable, Drevik."

Neither are you, he wanted to say. But the words stuck in his throat.

The Elder council convened at midnight.

Drevik stood in the center of the circular chamber, his shoulder bandaged and aching, and tried not to feel like he was on trial. The Elders sat in their elevated positions around him, faces half-shadowed, expressions unreadable.

Elder Thornwood—Lexi's father—spoke first. "The breach has been contained. Four entities eliminated, six students injured, no fatalities. We were fortunate."

"Fortunate?" Elder Voss's voice dripped with contempt. "Our defenses were compromised. Someone disabled the ward matrix. This wasn't a random breach—it was an attack."

"An attack that was detected and responded to with appropriate speed." Elder Thornwood's tone remained neutral. "The acolytes performed admirably."

"Acolytes should not have been in a position to perform at all." Voss leaned forward, and Drevik saw the cold calculation in his eyes. "Where were the Wardens? Why was the response left to untrained children?"

"The Wardens were dispatched to the wrong location." Another Elder spoke—Drevik didn't know her name. "The initial alert was... redirected."

The implication hung in the air. Someone had intentionally sent the Wardens away from the breach. Someone had wanted it to grow.

"We are investigating." Elder Thornwood's voice hardened. "But for now, we must address a more pressing concern."

His eyes moved to Drevik.

"The breach source remains open. We've contained it, but we cannot close it. Something is anchoring it from the Shadowlands side, and until that anchor is removed, the breach will continue to expand. Every hour it remains open, the risk of another incident increases."

"What are you proposing?" Voss asked.

"A mission." Thornwood's gaze didn't waver. "Someone needs to enter the Shadowlands and find the anchor. A small team, moving quickly. In and out before the local entities can respond."

Drevik's stomach tightened. Shadowlands missions were dangerous under the best circumstances. During an active breach, they were nearly suicidal.

"Who would lead this mission?" someone asked.

Thornwood's expression was unreadable. "The acolyte Drevik."

Silence. Then Voss laughed—a cold, sharp sound. "An acolyte? You want to send an untrained boy into the Shadowlands during a breach event?"

"His shadow-walking abilities are exceptional." Thornwood's voice carried a note of challenge. "And he has experience with Shadowlands navigation that many Wardens lack."

"He's expendable, you mean." Voss's smile didn't reach his eyes. "If he fails, we lose nothing of value."

Drevik kept his face neutral, but the words stung. He knew how the Order viewed him—the outsider, the scholarship student, the one who'd stumbled into membership rather than being born to it. To Elders like Voss, he was a resource to be spent, not a person to protect.

"I'll do it," he said. "I'll go."

"You don't have a choice in the matter." Voss waved a dismissive hand. "The council has decided—"

"I volunteer." Lexi's voice cut through the chamber like a blade.

She stepped out of the shadows at the edge of the room—Drevik hadn't even seen her arrive—and moved to stand beside him. Her jaw was set, her eyes bright with determination.

"Lexi." Her father's voice was carefully controlled. "This isn't a discussion."

"No, it's not." She crossed her arms. "Drevik isn't going into the Shadowlands alone. I'm going with him. That's not negotiable."

Voss's smile had returned, and Drevik didn't like the satisfaction in it. "How touching. The Thornwood heir volunteering to die alongside her—" He stopped, reconsidered his words. "Her partner."

"You need someone who knows breach dynamics." Lexi's voice was steady, but Drevik saw the tension in her shoulders. "You need someone who can fight while he looks for the anchor. I'm the best qualified acolyte for this mission."

"She's not wrong," another Elder said. "Her combat scores are exceptional."

"The mission is dangerous enough without sending two acolytes." Voss's tone was skeptical, but Drevik saw the calculation behind his eyes. The man wanted them both out of the way. Lexi's presence would make the mission more likely to succeed, but also more likely to result in casualties.

And Voss wanted casualties.

"Two acolytes can cover more ground than one." Elder Thornwood's voice was carefully neutral. "And Lexi's skills complement Drevik's. They work well together."

"Is that what we're calling it?" Voss said.

Lexi's hand found Drevik's. Not holding—just touching. A reminder that she was there. That she wasn't going to let him face this alone.

"The mission is approved." Thornwood's voice was final. "Drevik will lead. Lexi will provide support. They leave at dawn."

Dawn. Less than six hours away.

Drevik looked at Lexi, saw the determination in her eyes, the fear she was trying to hide. She knew what they were walking into. She knew the odds.

She was going anyway.

"I need to make arrangements," he said. "Preparations."

"Take what time you need." Thornwood's gaze was heavy on him. "But be ready. The Shadowlands won't wait for anyone."

They found Ivy in a secure room beneath the Order's compound.

She looked smaller than Drevik remembered. Fragile, somehow, despite the defiance in her eyes. Her interrogator had left bruises—visible ones, on her wrists and arms, and Drevik suspected there were others hidden under her clothes.

"They wanted to know about my research," she said when they were alone. Her voice was hoarse. "About the texts I found. About the things I've seen."

"What things?" Lexi asked from the doorway.

Ivy's eyes moved between them, calculating. Trusting, but only so far.

"Things that shouldn't exist." Her voice dropped. "Things the Order has hidden. Things about what's really happening to the barrier between our world and theirs."

Drevik thought about the breach. About the disabled wards. About Voss's cold smile and the way he'd looked at Lexi when she volunteered.

"Someone inside the Order is working against it," he said. "Someone wanted that breach to happen."

Ivy nodded slowly. "I know. And I think I know who."

She told them what she'd found in her research. The patterns. The money trails. The communications that shouldn't exist between Order members and entities that lived in shadow.

By the time she finished, Drevik's hands were shaking.

"We have to tell Thornwood," Lexi said. "This changes everything."

"Does it?" Ivy's smile was bitter. "Or does it just confirm what some of us have suspected all along? The Order is rotting from the inside. And the people in charge are too busy protecting their own positions to see it."

Drevik thought about the mission. About walking into the Shadowlands with enemies behind him and unknown dangers ahead.

"Whatever's happening," he said, "we need to survive this mission first. Find the anchor. Close the breach. Then we deal with the traitors."

Lexi nodded, but her expression was troubled. "And if the traitors are the ones sending us in?"

The question hung in the air, heavy and uncomfortable.

Drevik didn't have an answer. But as he looked at the two women—the one who'd volunteered to die beside him, and the one who'd risked everything to uncover the truth—he knew one thing for certain.

He wasn't going to let any of them die. Not if he could help it.

And if that meant walking into the Shadowlands and facing whatever waited there...

Then that's what he would do.

Dawn came too quickly.

Drevik stood at the breach point, watching the tear in reality pulse and breathe. In the twilight beyond, he saw shapes moving. Waiting.

Lexi appeared at his side, geared for combat. Her face was pale but determined.

"Last chance to back out," she said.

"Same to you."

She shook her head. "Not a chance. Where you go, I go."

It should have sounded like a threat. Instead, it sounded like a promise.

They stepped through together, and the Shadowlands swallowed them whole.

Chapter 6: Testing Limits

Chapter Six: Testing Limits

The first blow caught Drevik across the ribs before he even saw Warden Thane move.

Air exploded from his lungs as he crumpled to the training mat, the fluorescent lights above swimming in his vision. The underground training facility smelled of sweat and something metallic—maybe blood. Probably blood.

"Again." Thane's voice cut through the ringing in Drevik's ears. The senior Warden stood with his arms crossed, not even breathing hard. His shadow stretched across the mat like a living thing, tendrils of darkness still reaching for Drevik even after the strike had landed.

Drevik rolled to his feet, one hand pressed against his aching ribs. Three days of this. Three days of being beaten into the ground, of having his limits tested and exceeded, of pushing through pain that should have stopped him hours ago.

"Your shadow walking is sloppy," Thane continued, circling like a predator. "You think because you can slip between spaces, that makes you fast. But you leave traces. Residue. Any competent enemy can track your path."

"I'll work on it." Drevik's voice came out rougher than intended.

"You'll work on it now." Thane's shadow lunged.

This time, Drevik was ready. He dropped into his own darkness, feeling the familiar cold rush as the Shadowlands opened around him. For a heartbeat, he existed in both places—training mat and twilight realm—before reappearing behind Thane.

His strike was fast. Thane was faster.

The Warden caught Drevik's wrist and twisted, sending him sprawling again. But Thane's eyes held something new. Interest, maybe. Or calculation.

"Better," he said. "Your transition time is improving. But you're still thinking of the Shadowlands as separate. A door you open and close." He offered Drevik a hand up. "It's not separate. It's a part of you. A part of this world. The Veil isn't a barrier—it's a membrane."

Drevik took the hand, rising on unsteady legs. "That's not how it was taught in initiate training."

"Initiate training is designed to keep you alive long enough to learn the truth." Thane's expression hardened. "The Elders don't want acolytes thinking too deeply about what lives in the dark. It attracts attention."

The words sent a chill down Drevik's spine. He remembered the darkness he'd carried back from his last journey into the Shadowlands—the cold weight that still lingered in his bones, the whispers that came in his sleep.

"Speaking of attention," Thane continued, glancing toward the observation window above the training floor. "You have an audience today."

Drevik followed his gaze. The glass was tinted, but he felt the weight of multiple stares. Watching. Waiting.

"Political vultures," Thane muttered. "Half of them want to see you succeed. The other half want documentation of your failure. The smart ones haven't decided which side to take yet."

"Wonderful." Drevik rolled his shoulders, trying to loosen the tension building there. "Should I be worried about any of them?"

"You should be worried about all of them." Thane's shadow contracted, pulling tight against his body. "But that's not your concern right now. Right now, you need to focus on staying conscious."

The training resumed, and with it came the relentless pressure that had defined Drevik's week. Thane didn't just test his combat abilities—he probed every weakness, every gap in Drevik's defenses. Shadow manipulation. Veil Sight under stress. Physical endurance when magical reserves ran dry.

By the second hour, Drevik's vision had blur at the edges.

By the third, he was moving on instinct alone.

By the fourth, he stopped feeling the hits.

"That's enough for now." Thane's voice come from far away. "Your control is slipping. I can see the darkness you're carrying trying to surface."

Drevik blinked, realizing he'd been standing in place for several seconds without remembering how he got there. His hands were trembling—not from exhaustion, but from something else. Something cold and hungry coiling in his chest.

"Come back tomorrow. Same time." Thane was already walking toward the exit. "And Markinsoul? Whatever you're holding onto from your last crossing... you need to find a way to release it. Or it will release you."

The words followed Drevik into the locker room, echoing in the tile chamber as he stripped off his sweat-soaked training gear. The showers said to life, scalding water washing away the surface grime but doing nothing for the deeper chill.

He pressed his forehead against the tile wall, letting the heat sink into his muscles. The darkness inside him shifted, restless. In the week since his last journey into the Shadowlands, he'd felt it growing—a weight behind his sternum that hadn't been there before. It was like hunger. Like waiting.

"You look like someone tried to drown you and failed."

Drevik turned to find Lexi leaning against the locker room doorway, her arms crossed over her chest. She'd changed out of her own training gear into a simple black tank top and jeans, but the shadows under her eyes spoke to her own exhaustion.

"Thane doesn't believe in half measures." He grabbed his towel, wrapping it around his waist as he stepped out of the shower spray. "What are you doing here? I thought your training rotation was with the Seekers this week."

"It was." Her eyes tracked over his form—clinical, assessing, but with something warmer underneath. "I requested reassignment."

Drevik paused in the middle of reaching for his clothes. "You what?"

"I requested reassignment." Lexi pushed off from the doorframe, crossing to stand in front of him. The locker room was empty save for the two of them, the afternoon light filtering through the high windows casting her auburn hair in shades of copper. "Thane's training methods are brutal. I know what he put the last acolyte through, and she had twice your experience."

"I can handle it."

"That's not the point." Her voice sharpened. "You shouldn't have to handle it alone. The Order throws new recruits into the deep end and expects them to either swim or drown. It's stupid and wasteful and—"

"Lexi." Drevik caught her wrist, feeling the tension vibrating through her. "Why does this matter to you?"

The question hung between them. In the silence, Drevik became acutely aware of how close she was standing. Of the water still dripping from his shoulders. Of the way her breath had caught when he touched her.

"Because." Lexi's eyes met his, fierce and vulnerable at once. "You're mine to protect. Mine to train. Mine to—" She stopped, jaw tightening. "The Elder Council wants to see you fail. My mother wants to see you fail. I'm not going to give them the satisfaction."

"Your mother." Drevik released her wrist, but Lexi didn't step back. "I keep forgetting she's one of them."

"One of the most powerful, which means one of the most dangerous." Lexi's hand came up, fingers hovering near his bruised ribs without quite touching. "She's been asking questions about you. About your progress, your abilities, your... potential."

The cold in Drevik's chest spread. "What kind of questions?"

"The kind that end with people disappearing." Lexi's voice was flat, but her eyes betrayed fear. Not for herself—for him. "Drevik, I need you to be careful. The factions are circling. Some see you as an asset to be cultivated. Others see you as a threat to be eliminated. And a few—" She stopped, looking away.

"A few what?"

"Nothing. Just... be careful." Her hand finally touched his skin, feather-light over the bruise spreading across his ribs. "Does it hurt?"

"Yes." Drevik didn't lie. "But I've had worse."

"I know." Lexi's touch traced the edge of the bruise, clinical and intimate at once. "I read your mission report. The thing you faced in the Shadowlands—I've never seen anything like it. And the darkness you brought back..." Her eyes rose to meet his. "It's still there, isn't it? I can feel it from here."

Drevik nodded slowly. "Thane noticed it too. He said I need to release it before it releases me."

"Easier said than done." Lexi's hand flattened against his chest, directly over his heart. Her palm was warm against his damp skin, grounding him against the cold coiling inside. "The Shadowlands don't let go easily. Whatever you touched, whatever touched you—it wants to keep you."

"Then I'll have to convince it otherwise."

A smile ghosted across Lexi's lips. "That's either very brave or very stupid."

"Can it be both?"

"Usually, with you." Her hand pressed harder against his chest. "I want to help. Let me help. Whatever training Thane is putting you through, I can supplement. I know techniques he doesn't teach—shadow weaving, emotional anchoring, ways to channel excess darkness into something useful."

Drevik covered her hand with his own, feeling her heartbeat through her palm. Or maybe that was his own pulse, thundering against his ribs. "Why? You already have your own responsibilities, your own training. Why add mine to the pile?"

"Because—" Lexi started, then stopped. Her eyes searched his face, looking for something. "Because someone has to keep you alive long enough to become useful."

The words were light, but her tone wasn't. Drevik could read the truth underneath—the fear, the care, the stubborn refusal to let him face this alone.

"Okay." He nodded slowly. "Show me what you know."

The secondary training room was smaller than the main facility, tucked away in a corner of the underground complex that Drevik had never visited. The walls were lined with crystals that pulsed with soft light, and the air hummed with contained power.

"This is a meditation chamber," Lexi explained, moving to the center of the room. "The crystals help focus your energy. They're usually reserved for Seeker-level exercises, but my mother's influence has some benefits."

"Your mother arranged access for you?"

"My mother arranged the entire room." Lexi's smile was sharp. "She just doesn't know I'm using it for this."

Drevik followed her to the center, where two meditation cushions sat facing each other. The crystals' hum intensified as he approached, responding to the darkness still coiled in his chest.

"Sit." Lexi settled onto one cushion, crossing her legs beneath her. "Close your eyes. I want you to focus on the darkness inside you—not fighting it, just... acknowledging it."

Drevik obeyed, lowering himself onto the cushion. His muscles screamed from the day's training, but he forced himself to relax, to breathe through the pain.

"Good." Lexi's voice came closer. "Now I want you to imagine that darkness as a physical thing. A shape, a color, a texture. Give it form."

The exercise was harder than it sounded. The darkness inside him wasn't a single thing—it was layers upon layers, cold and hot at once, smooth and sharp. When he finally grasped its shape, the image that formed made his stomach clench.

"It's... a sphere," he said. "Black glass. With something moving inside."

"Something?" Lexi's voice was careful. "Or someone?"

Drevik's breath held. He hadn't told anyone about the whispers that followed him from the Shadowlands. The voices that called his name in the space between sleeping and waking. The sense of being watched by eyes he couldn't see.

"I don't know," he admitted. "It feels... aware. Like it's watching me."

"That's not uncommon after a deep crossing." Lexi's hand found his knee, warm and solid. "The Shadowlands leaves marks on everyone. Some become more sensitive to its presence. Others carry pieces back with them."

"And which am I?"

"Both, I think." Lexi's thumb traced circles on his knee, absently soothing. "Your abilities are unusual, Drevik. The Foresight alone would make you valuable. But your connection to the Shadowlands—it's deeper than it should be. Deeper than Thane wants to admit."

"Thane doesn't want to admit a lot of things."

"Thane is scared." The words were blunt. "He's seen what happens when someone's connection to the Shadowlands grows too strong. The hunger it creates. The things it makes them do."

Drevik's eyes opened. "What things?"

"Stories." Lexi's expression was unreadable. "From before my time. The kind the Order doesn't like to discuss."

The cold in Drevik's chest pulsed, and with it came a flash of image—the Shadowlands stretching before him, endless and dark. A figure standing at the edge of his vision, watching. Waiting.

"Your connection is growing." Lexi's hand tightened on his knee. "I can feel it from here. The darkness is responding to your attention, trying to pull you deeper."

"Is that bad?"

"Is that a serious question?" Lexi's laugh was short, humorless. "Yes, it's bad. The Shadowlands wants to consume. It doesn't negotiate, doesn't compromise. If you let it pull you too far—"

"I won't." Drevik covered her hand with his own. "I won't let it."

"You might not have a choice." Lexi's eyes met his, fierce and frightened. "That's what I'm trying to tell you. The darkness inside you isn't just residue from your last crossing. It's a seed. And it's growing."

The crystals around them pulsed faster, responding to Drevik's spiking heartbeat. The sphere in his chest throbbed with something that felt almost like anticipation.

"What do I do?"

"We anchor you." Lexi shifted closer, her knees touching his. "Heart bonds are rare, but emotional tethers are common. Most acolytes form them naturally with their mentors, their training partners. Someone who can pull them back when they start to drift."

"And you're offering?"

"I'm insisting." Lexi's free hand came up to cup his jaw. Her touch was warm, grounding, human against the cold spreading through his veins. "Close your eyes again. Focus on my voice. On my touch. Let me pull you back if the darkness tries to take you."

Drevik let his eyes fall closed. In the darkness behind his lids, the sphere pulsed and spun, its surface reflecting images he couldn't quite see. The whispers started almost immediately—soft, sibilant, speaking words just beyond hearing.

"I'm here." Lexi's voice cut through the sound. "Focus on me. On my hand. My breath."

She was close enough that he felt it now—the warmth of her exhale against his cheek, the brush of her hair falling forward. The whispers grew louder, more insistent, trying to pull him toward the sphere and whatever waited inside.

"Drevik." Lexi's forehead pressed against his. "Come back to me."

The intimacy of the contact surprised him. His eyes opened to find her face inches from his, her gaze steady and unafraid. The cold in his chest retreated, just slightly, pushed back by the warmth of her presence.

"Good." Her mouth curved. "That's what an anchor feels like. Remember it. When the darkness pulls, you reach for your anchor."

"How do you know so much about this?"

"Because I've needed anchors too." Lexi's thumb traced along his jaw. "My mother's bloodline carries a strong connection to the Shadowlands. It's why she rose so high in the Order—and why she's so afraid of losing control."

"And you? Are you afraid?"

"Every day." The admission was quiet. "But fear keeps us alive. It keeps us careful. The problems start when people stop being afraid."

Drevik's hand came up to cover hers against his cheek. In the dim light of the meditation chamber, with crystals humming around them and darkness coiling in his chest, Lexi looked both fierce and fragile.

"Thank you." The words felt inadequate for what she was offering. "For this. For everything."

"Thank me by staying alive." Lexi pulled back, but her hand lingered against his skin. "Now let's try again. And this time, I want you to try speaking to the darkness instead of just observing it."

"Speaking to it?"

"Ask it what it wants." Lexi's expression was serious. "Understanding your burden is the first step to controlling it."

Drevik closed his eyes once more. The sphere materialized in his mind's eye, spinning faster now, its surface cracked with lines of deep purple light. The whispers had become a chorus, voices overlapping in a language just beyond comprehension.

What do you want?

He pushed the question toward the sphere, unsure if he was doing it right. For a moment, nothing happened. Then the sphere stopped spinning, its surface going smooth and still.

A voice rose from inside—not the whispers, but something clearer. Something familiar.

You.

The word echoed through his mind, and Drevik's eyes snapped open.

"Your form is acceptable."

Mira's voice dripped with calculated boredom as she circled Drevik's prone body. He'd been on the training mat for the past hour, working through a series of shadow manipulation exercises while she critiqued every movement.

"Acceptable?" Drevik pushed himself upright, wiping sweat from his brow. "I hit every target."

"You hit every target with sloppy technique." Mira's smile was sharp, deliberately provocative. "Power without precision is wasted potential. Anyone can throw a punch. It takes skill to place it exactly where you want it."

"I'll keep that in mind."

"See that you do." Mira turned toward the observation window, where Lexi had been watching for the past thirty minutes. Her posture was rigid, her arms crossed, her expression carefully neutral. "Your shadow is getting restless, Thornwood. Is watching your pet struggle making you nervous?"

"He's not my pet." Lexi's voice could have frozen fire. "And I'm not nervous."

"Could have fooled me." Mira's smile widened. "You've been glaring at me like you want to tear my throat out. Possessive doesn't suit you, Lexi. It makes you look... desperate."

"Careful." The warning in Lexi's tone was unmistakable.

"Or what?" Mira took a step toward the window, toward Lexi. "You'll challenge me? In front of the observers?" Her gesture took in the tinted glass above them. "Think about what that would look like to the Council. An Elder's daughter fighting over an acolyte like a child with a toy."

Drevik rose to his feet, positioning himself between them. "That's enough. Both of you."

"Defending her honor?" Mira's eyes glittered. "How charming. Though I wonder if she'd do the same for you."

"Mira." Drevik kept his voice level. "What do you want?"

"Right now? To help you train." Mira circled him, predatory and graceful. "You have raw talent, Markinsoul. But raw talent isn't enough for what's coming. The Shadowlands mission will kill you if you're not prepared."

"That's what Thane is for."

"Thane is teaching you to survive." Mira stopped in front of him, close enough that he could smell her perfume—something dark and floral. "I can teach you to succeed. There's a difference."

"What kind of difference?"

"The kind that matters." Her hand came up, fingers trailing along his shoulder. The touch was deliberate, provocative, designed to provoke. "Thane wants to make you a good little soldier. I want to make you powerful. There's a reason your abilities are so strong, Drevik. You're not like other acolytes. You're not like anyone I've seen before."

"You say that like it's a compliment."

"It's an observation." Mira's fingers traced down his arm, feather-light. "Compliments are for people who need reassurance. You don't need reassurance—you need challenge. You need someone to push you past your limits."

"And that someone is you?"

"Unless you'd rather keep playing it safe." Her eyes flicked to Lexi, then back to him. "Some of us prefer safety. Others prefer power. The question is—which are you?"

Drevik caught her wrist before her touch could travel further. "I prefer results. Show me what you're offering, or step back."

Mira's smile turned genuine. "Good. That's the answer I was hoping for."

She pulled her wrist free, then moved to the center of the training mat. "Shadow manipulation isn't just about controlling darkness. It's about understanding the relationship between light and dark. Every shadow is defined by the light that creates it. To master one, you must understand both."

"Thane already covered the basics."

"Thane covered the mechanics." Mira raised her hand, and the shadows in the corner of the room stretch toward her. "I'm showing you the art. Watch."

The shadows pooled at her feet, then rose into the air. They twisted and reshaped, forming something that looked almost like a sculpture—a figure made of pure darkness, standing tall and reaching toward the ceiling.

"A shadow is alive," Mira said. "It responds to intent, to emotion, to desire. When you try to control it through force alone, you're fighting against its nature. But when you work with it..."

The shadow figure shifted, moving through a series of combat forms. It was beautiful—fluid and deadly and impossibly graceful.

"When you work with it," Mira continued, "it becomes an extension of yourself. Not a weapon. A partner."

The shadow figure dissolved, flowing back to its original position. Mira turned to face him, her expression serious for the first time.

"That's what I can teach you. Not just to use the Shadowlands, but to work with it. To understand it as a living thing rather than a resource to be exploited."

"And why would you teach me this? What do you get out of it?"

Mira's smile returned, but it was softer now. "I get to watch you become something extraordinary. For some of us, that's enough."

From the observation window, Lexi's voice cut through the moment. "Drevik, your training schedule is already full. You don't need her interference."

"Interference?" Mira raised an eyebrow. "Is that what we're calling expertise now?"

"We're calling it what it is—an attempt to insert yourself into his training for your own purposes."

"My purposes are transparent." Mira's voice hardened. "I want him to survive. You want him dependent."

"That's not—" Lexi started, then stopped herself. When she spoke again, her tone was controlled. "Fine. Show him what you know. But I'm staying to observe."

"Of course you are." Mira turned back to Drevik. "Shall we begin?"

The evening found Drevik in his dorm room, surrounded by training supplements and hand-written notes. Ivy's care package had arrived via courier—an unmarked envelope containing energy supplements, muscle relaxants, and a detailed breakdown of shadow manipulation theory that was clearer than anything he'd received in formal training.

For the long nights, her note had read. You're pushing too hard. Let yourself rest when you can.

The supplements were working. His muscles still ached from the day's training, but the sharp edge of exhaustion had dulled. He could think clearly for the first time in hours.

A knock at the door interrupted his study.

Lexi stood in the hallway, a container in her hands and an expression on her face that suggested she wasn't entirely happy about being there.

"I brought food." She held up the container. "Real food. Not the processed garbage they serve in the dining hall."

"You didn't have to do that."

"I know." She pushed past him into the room, setting the container on his desk. "But you've been training for twelve hours straight. Even your shadow needs fuel."

Drevik closed the door behind her, acutely aware of how small the room felt with her inside it. The bed was unmade from his early morning. Training clothes were scattered across the floor. His notes covered every available surface.

"You're living like a prisoner," Lexi observed, looking around. "When's the last time you saw sunlight?"

"I see plenty of sunlight."

"You see training rooms and meditation chambers." She pulled the container open, releasing the smell of home-cooked stew. "When's the last time you went above ground?"

Drevik hesitated. "Yesterday?"

"Yesterday was rain. You haven't been above ground in at least two days." Lexi's expression softened. "The darkness you're carrying—it feeds on isolation. The more you withdraw, the stronger it gets."

"I'm not withdrawing. I'm training."

"There's a difference between training and hiding." Lexi pulled out his desk chair, sinking into it. "I know because I've done both. After my first crossing into the Shadowlands, I spent three weeks barely leaving my room. I told myself I was recovering. Really, I was letting the darkness convince me that nowhere was safe."

Drevik sat on the edge of his bed, facing her. "How did you break out of it?"

"Someone knocked on my door and refused to leave until I ate real food and saw actual sunlight." Her smile was wry. "Consider this me knocking."

The stew was better than anything he'd had in weeks. Drevik ate in silence while Lexi thumbed through his notes, occasionally making corrections or additions in her neat handwriting.

"Your theory is sound," she said finally. "But your understanding of the Veil is incomplete. The barrier between worlds isn't static—it breathes, expands, contracts. Trying to force through during a contraction phase will shred your consciousness."

"That's not in any of the training materials."

"It's not in the acolyte materials." Lexi set down her pen. "The Order controls what information reaches each level. By the time you reach Seeker, you learn that half of what you were taught as an initiate was simplified to the point of being wrong."

"Seems counterproductive."

"Seems dangerous," she corrected. "Which is why they do it. Too much knowledge too fast can destroy an acolyte. Or drive them to try things they're not ready for."

Drevik set down his empty container. "Is that what happened to the last acolyte who trained under Thane? You mentioned her before. What was her name?"

Lexi's expression closed. "Elara. She was talented. Strong. Maybe stronger than me." Her voice dropped. "She pushed too hard, too fast. Tried to cross into the Shadowlands without proper preparation. The Veil didn't just reject her—it absorbed her."

"Absorbed?"

"She's not dead." Lexi's hand found his, her grip tight. "She's somewhere in between. The healers say her body is still alive, but her consciousness... it's scattered. Pieces of her, trapped in the barrier between worlds."

"Is there a way to bring her back?"

"No one knows." Lexi's eyes met his. "That's why I'm here. That's why I'm training you, watching you, pushing you. I'm not going to let that happen to you. I refuse."

The intensity in her voice made Drevik's chest ache. He understood, suddenly, why she'd been so fierce during his training. Why she'd insisted on being involved despite the political cost.

"Lexi." He squeezed her hand. "I'm not Elara."

"No. You're not." Her grip tightened. "You're more talented than she was. And that terrifies me. Because the more potential you show, the more the Order will push you. The more the Shadowlands will call to you."

"I have you to pull me back."

The words hung between them. Lexi's expression shifted—something vulnerable flickering across her features before she locked it down.

"Promise me." Her voice was rough. "Promise me that when it gets too much—when the darkness feels like it's winning—you'll tell me. You'll let me help."

"I promise."

Lexi released his hand, rising from the chair. But she didn't move toward the door. Instead, she stood in the middle of his cramped dorm room, surrounded by his notes and his exhaustion and his desperate attempt to become something more than he was.

"Good." Her voice was steadier now. "Because I'm not losing anyone else."

She left before he could respond. The door closed behind her with a soft click, and Drevik was alone with the darkness in his chest and the weight of promises made.

The meditation chamber was empty at midnight.

Drevik had returned to it after Lexi left, unable to sleep, unable to shake the feeling that something was waiting for him. The crystals hummed as he entered, their light pulsing in response to his presence.

He settled onto the cushion in the center of the room, closing his eyes. The darkness inside him rose immediately—hungry, eager, responding to his attention.

What are you?

He pushed the question toward the sphere, feeling it pulse and shift in response.

I am you. The voice was clearer this time, more defined. The part you hide from. The hunger you deny.

Why are you here?

Because you brought me. The sphere's surface cracked further, purple light bleeding through. I was sleeping. You woke me.

Drevik's consciousness pressed harder. What do you want?

The sphere shattered.

Darkness exploded outward, and Drevik felt himself falling—not physically, but mentally, his awareness tumbling through layers of shadow and light. The meditation chamber disappeared. The crystals vanished. Everything he knew was replaced by the endless twilight of the Shadowlands.

He stood on a plain of gray grass, beneath a sky that held no sun. The landscape stretched before him, familiar and wrong at the same time. He'd been here before, during his crossing. But this time, he hadn't chosen to come.

Drevik.

The voice came from everywhere and nowhere. He spun, trying to locate its source.

Drevik.

A figure emerged from the shadows ahead. Not one of the twisted creatures he'd faced before—something else. Something human-shaped, wrapped in darkness that moved like living cloth.

The figure stopped ten feet away. In the gray light, Drevik could make out features—female, pale, with dark hair that floated around her face as if suspended in water.

Her eyes opened. They were the same green-gold as his.

Drevik. Her voice was soft, achingly familiar. My son.

The world lurched. Drevik felt something grab him from behind—not physical, but magical, a force pulling him backward, away from her.

"Wait—" He reached for her, straining against the pull. "Who are you? How do you know—"

Find me. Her voice was fading as he was dragged away. Find the truth. It's the only way to—

The Shadowlands dissolved.

Drevik gasped awake in the meditation chamber, his body rigid, his heart pounding. The crystals had gone dark, their light extinguished. His hands were shaking so badly he could barely form a fist.

The woman's face lingered in his mind—her eyes, her voice, the way she'd said his name.

My son.

The door to the meditation chamber burst open. Thane stood in the doorway, his expression sharp.

"Your shadow signature just spiked across half the compound." The Warden's eyes swept the room, taking in the dark crystals and Drevik's trembling form. "What happened?"

Drevik opened his mouth to answer, but his voice wouldn't come. The darkness in his chest had settled into something new—not just hunger anymore, but purpose.

Find me.

"I saw someone," he managed finally. "In the Shadowlands. A woman."

Thane's expression hardened. "Who?"

"She said she was my mother."

The Warden went still. For a long moment, neither of them moved. Then Thane crossed to Drevik, crouching to meet his eyes.

"Describe her."

Drevik did—her face, her voice, the way the darkness had wrapped around her like clothing. As he spoke, Thane's expression grew grimmer.

"You crossed over without meaning to." It wasn't a question. "Your consciousness slipped through the Veil."

"I didn't try—"

"I know." Thane's hand closed around Drevik's wrist, grounding him. "Your abilities are manifesting faster than we anticipated. The darkness inside you is creating pathways, opening doors."

"Who was she?" Drevik gripped Thane's arm. "Who is my mother? The records say she's dead. The Order told me she died when I was an infant."

"The Order told you what they wanted you to believe." Thane's voice was rough. "Your mother was Veiled One. The highest rank. She disappeared into the Shadowlands twenty-one years ago, and no one has seen her since."

"Until now."

"Until now." Thane rose, pulling Drevik to his feet. "This changes things. The Council will need to know—you've made contact with something that shouldn't exist."

"A person. She's a person."

"Is she?" Thane's eyes met his. "The Shadowlands changes everything it touches. Whatever your mother was, whatever she is now—she's been in that realm for two decades. The thing wearing her face might not be her anymore."

Drevik's chest tightened. The darkness inside him pulsed with something that was like recognition, like longing.

Find me.

"She knew me," he said. "She knew my name."

"Then she's been watching." Thane's voice was grim. "Which means the Order's walls aren't as secure as we thought." He pulled Drevik toward the door. "Come. We need to report this before the wrong people hear about it from someone else."

As they left the meditation chamber, Drevik felt the darkness settle deeper into his bones. It wasn't just hunger anymore. It was direction. Purpose.

Somewhere in the Shadowlands, his mother was waiting.

And the darkness inside him wanted to lead him straight to her.

Chapter 7: Academic Pressure

Chapter 7: Academic Pressure

The fluorescent lights of the library hummed with a frequency that made Drevik's teeth ache.

He'd been staring at the same page of his Art History textbook for forty-five minutes, and the images were starting to move. Not because of any mystical phenomenon—simply because his exhausted brain was projecting shadows where none existed. After three nights of Shadowlands training followed by dawn patrol and then classes, his grasp on reality had become... tenuous.

"Drevik."

He blinked. The woman across from him had been talking for several minutes. He knew this because her lips had been moving, and occasionally sounds emerged. He just couldn't recall any of them.

"I'm sorry," he said, rubbing his eyes. "What?"

Marissa sighed, tapping her pen against her notebook. "I asked if you wanted to grab coffee. You've been dissociating for the past hour, and honestly? You look like you're about to pass out."

"I'm fine."

"You're really not." She closed her notebook, concern flickering across her features. "Is everything okay? You've been weird lately. Weirder than usual, I mean."

Drevik forced a smile that felt brittle on his face. Marissa Chen had been in his study group since freshman year. She was pre-law, aggressively organized, and had never once suspected that her classmate spent his nights fighting shadow creatures in a parallel dimension. He wanted to keep it that way.

"Just stressed about finals," he said. "You know how it is."

"Finals." She raised an eyebrow. "We're studying Art History, Drevik. We're not exactly cramming for the MCATs."

The irony wasn't lost on him. While Marissa worried about baroque versus rococo, he was learning to walk through walls and bind interdimensional entities. The academic pressure should have felt manageable by comparison.

It didn't.

"Maybe I need that coffee after all," he conceded, standing too quickly. The room tilted. He grabbed the edge of the table.

Marissa was watching him with an expression he couldn't quite read. "Are you sure you're okay? Because if you're having some kind of breakdown—"

"I'm fine," he repeated, more sharply than intended. He caught himself, softening his tone. "Sorry. I haven't been sleeping well. Coffee would help."

Her eyes lingered on him a moment longer before she shrugged. "Fine. But if you collapse in the middle of the library, I'm putting that in my closing argument someday."

The campus coffee shop was a fifteen-minute walk across the quad, and Drevik used every step to reassemble his composure. The autumn air helped—crisp and clean, carrying the distant sound of a practice session from the athletic fields. Normal. Mundane. The world that existed outside the Order's shadow.

He just wished it felt more real.

"That's the third time you've checked your phone in two minutes," Marissa observed as they entered the coffee shop. "Expecting an important call?"

Drevik pocketed the device, heat rising to his cheeks. He hadn't been checking for messages. He'd been checking for shadows. The darkness in his peripheral vision had become persistent lately—a side effect of extended time in the Shadowlands, Lexi had explained. His mind was learning to see things that human eyes weren't meant to see.

It made focusing on Art History remarkably difficult.

"Habit," he said. "What's your order?"

They found a corner table, and for the next hour, Drevik attempted to absorb information about Renaissance painters while his brain screamed about everything he was ignoring. The Order expected him to train four nights a week now. His classes expected him to remain conscious during lectures. His normal friends expected him to show up for things. And somewhere in the back of his mind, the darkness said that none of this mattered—that the Shadowlands were the only reality, and everything else was just a dream he'd wake from eventually.

"You're doing it again," Marissa said, breaking through his thoughts.

"Doing what?"

"Staring into space like you're looking at something nobody else can see."

Drevik's throat tightened. If only she knew how literal that was.

Before he could respond, the door chimed. He glanced up automatically, scanning entrances and exits—a habit drilled into him by Lexi—and his heart stuttered.

Seraphina Vane walked through the door like she owned the air itself.

She wore normal clothes, relatively speaking. Dark jeans, a cream-colored sweater, ankle boots. Her silver-blonde hair fell loose around her shoulders, catching the afternoon light. But there was nothing normal about the way every head in the coffee shop turned to follow her progress. She moved with the liquid grace of something not entirely human, and Drevik felt the familiar pull in his chest—the strange magnetic attraction that had existed between them since their first meeting.

She wasn't supposed to be here. Seraphina operated in different circles, higher echelons of Order business. Seeing her on campus was like spotting a tiger in a grocery store.

Their eyes met across the crowded room.

A smile curved her lips—not warm, exactly, but knowing. She didn't approach him. Didn't acknowledge him beyond that single glance. She simply ordered at the counter, collected her drink, and settled at a table on the opposite side of the shop, pulling out a book as though she belonged there.

"Drevik." Marissa's voice cut through his daze. "Who is that?"

"No one," he said too quickly.

Marissa's gaze sharpened with the intensity of someone who'd been waiting for exactly this kind of drama. "That's not 'no one.' That's someone who just walked in looking like a supermodel and made eye contact with you like you share secrets. Do you share secrets?"

"We've met." Drevik kept his eyes firmly on his textbook. "She's... acquainted with some people I know."
