"Acquainted." Marissa leaned forward, abandoning all pretense of studying. "Is she why you've been distracted lately? Because if you're blowing off study sessions for a girl—"

"I'm not." The denial came out sharper than intended. "I don't even know her that well."

"Could've fooled me." Marissa glanced at Seraphina again, then back at Drevik. "She's still looking at you."

He didn't need to check. He felt her attention like a physical weight, pressing against his awareness. The darkness inside him stirred, responding to something it recognized.

"Maybe you should go talk to her," Marissa suggested. "Get it out of your system so we can focus on Renaissance art."

"There's nothing to get out of my system."

"Then prove it." Marissa stood, gathering her things. "I need to use the restroom. When I get back, I expect you to either be fully present or have some kind of explanation. Your choice."

She walked away, leaving Drevik alone at the table with his textbook and the weight of Seraphina's gaze.

He lasted approximately thirty seconds before he looked up.

She was already watching him, her pale blue eyes reflecting the ambient light with an intensity that made his breath catch. She tilted her head slightly—a question, an invitation, or perhaps a command. With Seraphina, it was difficult to tell the difference.

Drevik rose from his table before he consciously decided to move.

The walk across the coffee shop felt longer than the walk across campus. He was aware of every person watching him, every whisper that might be about him, every shadow that might be something more. Seraphina remained seated, perfectly at ease, as though his approach was exactly what she'd planned.

"Unexpected," he said, stopping beside her table. "What are you doing here?"

"Getting coffee." She gestured to her cup. "Is that not allowed?"

"You know what I mean."

Seraphina's smile deepened. "Perhaps I wanted to see how you were adjusting. The Shadowlands training has been... intense lately."

"That's one word for it."

"Sit." It wasn't a request.

Drevik sat, acutely aware of how this must look to anyone watching. A beautiful woman, intimate conversation, the kind of scenario that spawned rumors. He thought of Lexi, who was probably training somewhere on campus right now, completely unaware that Seraphina had appeared like a summoned spirit.

"You look exhausted," Seraphina observed, studying him over the rim of her cup. "The darkness is wearing on you."

"I'm managing."

"Managing." She set down her cup, folding her hands on the table. "You know, I can feel it when you're in the Shadowlands. Your presence... echoes. It's quite distinctive."

The admission sent a shiver down his spine. Seraphina had never been clear about the exact nature of her interest in him. She appeared and disappeared like the moon behind clouds, offering cryptic insights and lingering touches that left him confused and wanting.

"I didn't realize you were tracking my movements," he said.

"Tracking is such a clinical word. I prefer... attuned." Her pale eyes held his. "The Order has high hopes for you, Drevik. Higher than you realize. The darkness inside you is growing, and that growth requires... cultivation."

"Is that why you're here? Cultivation?"

"Partly." She leaned back in her chair, and he noticed how the ambient light bend around her, softening edges and deepening shadows. "I also wanted to see you in your natural habitat. You look remarkably ordinary in this setting. It's charming."

Drevik couldn't decide if that was a compliment or an insult. "I'm a college student. This is what college students do."

"Mmm." Her gaze drifted across the coffee shop, landing briefly on Marissa's empty chair. "Your friend seems curious about me."

"She's suspicious. I've been... distracted lately."

"Because of the Order."

"Because of everything." The admission slipped out before he could stop it. "I'm trying to balance two completely different lives, and I'm not sure I'm succeeding at either."

Seraphina was quiet for a moment. When she spoke again, her voice had lost some of its playful edge. "The duality you're experiencing is temporary. Eventually, you'll learn to integrate both aspects of yourself. The darkness will stop feeling like a separate presence and start feeling like... you."

"Did that happen for you?"

Something flickered across her features—vulnerability, perhaps, or pain. It vanished before he could be certain. "I've always been what I am. The darkness and I have never been separate."

Before he could respond, movement caught his eye. Marissa had returned from the restroom and was standing near their table, her expression a complex mixture of curiosity and challenge.

"I see you've made a new friend," she said, her tone carefully neutral.

Drevik stood, feeling suddenly like he was balancing on a knife's edge between worlds. "Marissa, this is Seraphina. We have... mutual acquaintances."

Seraphina rose as well, extending her hand with a grace that made the simple gesture look ceremonial. "A pleasure. Drevik's mentioned you."

Marissa shook the offered hand, her eyes widening slightly—probably at the coolness of Seraphina's skin, or the strength in her grip. "I doubt that. He barely mentioned you at all, which is interesting considering how you just looked at each other."

"Marissa—"

"I'm going to head out," Marissa interrupted, gathering her bag. "You clearly have... things... to discuss. Just do me a favor and actually show up to the study session tomorrow. Professor Harrison is already giving you the side-eye."

She left before Drevik could respond, weaving through tables toward the exit. He watched her go, a pit forming in his stomach.

"She's protective of you," Seraphina observed. "That's good. You'll need people who care about your survival."

"Was that a threat?"

"An observation." Seraphina collected her cup, preparing to leave. "Be careful, Drevik. The darkness is changing you faster than you realize. Your control is slipping."

"I have it under control."

"Do you?" She reached out, her fingers brushing his jaw with a touch so light it might have been a shadow. "Then why are your hands shaking?"

He looked down. She was right. A fine tremor ran through his fingers, invisible unless someone was looking closely. He hadn't even noticed.

"The more you deny the darkness," Seraphina continued, her voice dropping to a murmur, "the more it will demand from you. Stop fighting it. Let it flow through you instead of trying to contain it."

"How do you know so much about what I'm experiencing?"

"Because I've watched others walk this path." Her hand fell away from his face. "Most of them didn't survive. You have a chance to be different—if you stop pretending you're still just a college student with normal problems."

She turned and walked out of the coffee shop without a backward glance, leaving Drevik standing alone at a table that suddenly felt very exposed.

He was still reeling from the encounter when his phone buzzed.

Training tonight. 9 PM. Don't be late. —Lexi

The message was typical Lexi—direct, efficient, with no wasted words. But reading it after Seraphina's appearance, something twisted in his chest. Lexi would not be pleased to know Seraphina had been on campus. She viewed the older woman's interest in Drevik with deep suspicion.

Not that Drevik entirely understood that interest himself.

He left the coffee shop and headed toward his next class, trying to shake off the encounter. The campus looked different now—the shadows between buildings seemed deeper, the light harsher. Every person he passed might be an Order member, or something worse.

Stop it, he told himself. You're being paranoid.

But was it paranoia if the threats were real?

His Art History lecture passed in a blur. Professor Harrison's voice droned on about perspective and composition while Drevik's mind kept drifting to shadowy corridors and silver-blonde hair. He took notes automatically, his hand moving while his thoughts raced.

After class, he made his way to the small study room in the library where Lexi had agreed to meet him. She'd offered to help him prepare for his Art History final, claiming she'd taken the class two years ago and aced it. He suspected she just wanted an excuse to spend time together outside of Order business.

He found her already there, books spread across the table, her auburn hair pulled back in a practical ponytail. She looked up as he entered, and her face softened in a way that still caught him off guard.

"You're late," she said, but there was no real accusation in her tone.

"Got held up." He dropped into the chair across from her. "Ran into... something unexpected."

Lexi's eyes sharpened. "What kind of unexpected?"

He considered lying. It would be easier. But the words wouldn't come.

"Seraphina," he said finally. "She was at the coffee shop."

The softness vanished from Lexi's expression, replaced by something harder. "On campus? Why?"

"She said she wanted to see how I was adjusting. And something about feeling my presence in the Shadowlands." He paused, watching Lexi's jaw tighten. "Is that... normal?"

"Nothing about Seraphina is normal." Lexi's voice had an edge he hadn't heard before. "She's been involved with the Order for decades, but no one knows exactly what she is. Some people think she's not entirely human."

"She said something similar. About always being what she is."

Lexia's frown deepened. "What else did she say?"

"That the darkness is changing me. That I need to stop fighting it." He met Lexi's eyes. "She said my hands were shaking."

The admission hung between them. Lexi reached across the table, taking his hands in hers. Her touch was warm—warmer than Seraphina's—and familiar in a way that settled something inside him.

"You're pushing yourself too hard," she said. "The training, the classes, everything. You need rest."

"I can't rest. Finals are next week, and the Shadowlands mission—"

"Will happen whether you're rested or not." She squeezed his hands. "Let me help you. Not just with studying. With everything."

The offer was genuine, and he saw how much it cost her to make it. Lexi wasn't the type to ask for permission or offer support easily. She was the type to take charge and assume everyone would follow.

"I don't want to burden you," he said.

"You're not a burden." Her voice softened further. "You're never a burden. I wouldn't have offered if I didn't want to help."

He studied her face—the determined set of her jaw, the vulnerability she tried so hard to hide, the way her green eyes held his without flinching. She was offering him something beyond academic support. She was offering him partnership.

"Okay," he said. "Help me."

They studied for three hours.

Lexi was a demanding tutor, drilling him on dates and movements and artistic techniques until his brain was like overworked muscle. But underneath the academic rigor, there was a comfortable rhythm to their interaction—a domesticity that felt foreign and precious.

At some point, she moved her chair beside his, leaning over his notes to point out details he'd missed. Her shoulder pressed against his, warm and solid, and he found himself breathing easier than he had in days.

"You're overthinking this," she said, tapping his notebook. "The Baroque period isn't about memorizing facts. It's about understanding the emotional intent. These artists were trying to make people feel something."

"Kind of like shadow magic," he said without thinking.

Lexi paused. "What do you mean?"

"I mean..." He struggled to articulate the thought that had been forming. "In the Shadowlands, emotion shapes reality. Fear creates monsters. Hope creates paths. The Baroque artists were doing something similar—using contrast and movement to manipulate emotional response."

Lexi stared at him for a long moment. Then, slowly, a smile spread across her face—the kind of genuine smile she rarely showed anyone.

"That's actually brilliant," she said. "I never thought about it that way, but you're right. The same principles apply. You're understanding shadow work better than you realize."

The praise warmed him more than it should have. "I have a good teacher."

"Flattery will get you everywhere." But she was still smiling, and the tension that had gripped her since he'd mentioned Seraphina have loosened.

They returned to studying, but the dynamic had shifted. Every accidental touch felt charged—her hand brushing his as they reached for the same book, her breath warm against his cheek as she leaned in to read a passage. He was acutely aware of her proximity, the scent of her hair, the way her presence made the shadows feel less threatening.

"Drevik." Her voice had dropped to barely a whisper.

He turned to find her face very close to his, her green eyes searching his features with an intensity that had nothing to do with academics.

"You have no idea how hard it is," she said, "pretending this is just studying."

His heart stuttered. "Lexi—"

"I know we said we'd take things slow. I know you're dealing with more than any acolyte should have to handle. But being around you, watching you push yourself to the breaking point, not being able to—" She stopped herself, looking away. "I'm sorry. I shouldn't."

"Shouldn't what?"

"Complicate things." She pull back, but he caught her hand.

"You're not complicating things." His voice came out rough. "You're the only thing keeping me grounded."

The admission hung between them. Lexi's gaze returned to his face, and he saw the moment her restraint crumbled.

"Then let me ground you," she said.

She kissed him.

It wasn't their first kiss, but it felt different—less hesitant, more desperate. Her lips pressed against his with a hunger that matched the darkness pulsing inside him. He pulled her closer, his hand tangling in her hair, and she made a soft sound that sent heat flooding through his body.

For a moment, the shadows retreated entirely. There was only Lexi—warm and real and his—and the way she felt against him, and the way she said his name like it meant something sacred.

Then his phone buzzed, shattering the moment.

They broke apart, both breathing heavily. Lexi's cheeks were flushed, her lips slightly swollen, her eyes still dark with wanting.

"You should get that," she said, but her voice was unsteady.

Drevik glanced at his phone. A message from Professor Harrison.

Mr. Markinsoul, please come to my office immediately. We need to discuss your recent academic performance.

He stared at the screen, a cold weight settling in his stomach.

"What is it?" Lexi asked.

"Professor Harrison wants to see me." He looked up at her. "Now."

Professor Harrison's office was on the third floor of the humanities building, a cramped space filled with books and artifacts accumulated over decades of teaching. The professor herself was a small woman with sharp eyes and an intimidating reputation for detecting academic dishonesty.

She gestured for Drevik to sit without looking up from the papers on her desk.

"Mr. Markinsoul," she said finally, removing her glasses to study him directly. "Do you know why I asked to see you?"

"I can guess."

"Your midterm paper was exceptional. Thoughtful, well-researched, showing genuine insight into the material." She paused. "Your recent work, however, has been... inconsistent. You've missed assignments. Your participation in class has declined markedly. And today, you spent the entire lecture staring at the wall."

Drevik's throat tightened. "I've been dealing with some personal issues."

"So I gathered." Professor Harrison leaned back in her chair. "I'm not without understanding, Mr. Markinsoul. College is a stressful time. Many students struggle with balancing their responsibilities. But I've noticed a pattern with you that goes beyond typical academic pressure."

"What kind of pattern?"

"You disappear for days at a time. You return looking exhausted, sometimes injured. Your friends say you've joined some kind of... organization." Her eyes sharpened. "I want to give you the benefit of the doubt, but I need to know what's really going on."

The weight of the question pressed against Drevik's chest. He couldn't tell her the truth. He couldn't tell her anything. The Order's secrecy was absolute, and revealing even a fraction of the truth would put her in danger.

"I joined a martial arts group," he said, falling back on the cover story Lexi had helped him construct. "The training is more intense than I expected. I'm learning to manage my time better."

Professor Harrison studied him for a long moment. "A martial arts group."

"Yes."

"That meets at night."

"Yes."

"That leaves you looking like you haven't slept in a week." She stood, moving to her window. "Mr. Markinsoul, I've been teaching for thirty years. I've seen students struggle with drugs, with relationships, with family problems. I've never seen someone look the way you do right now unless they were in genuine danger."

"I'm not in danger."

"Then prove it." She turned back to face him. "Ace your final exam. Show up to class. Stop looking over your shoulder like something's hunting you." Her voice softened slightly. "Whatever you're involved in, if it's hurting you, there are resources. People who can help."

The irony of her words was almost painful. She thought he needed counseling, or medical attention, or any of the normal solutions for normal problems. She had no idea that the help he needed came from a secret organization that operated beneath the university itself.

"I appreciate your concern," he said. "I'll do better."

Professor Harrison's expression suggested she didn't entirely believe him, but she nodded. "See that you do. You have potential, Mr. Markinsoul. Don't waste it."

He left her office with his mind racing. The conversation had shaken something loose—a growing awareness that his double life was becoming increasingly difficult to maintain. How long could he keep this up before everything fell apart?

The walk back to his dorm felt longer than usual. The sun had set while he was in Harrison's office, and the campus had transformed into a landscape of pools of light separated by deep shadows. He caught himself scanning the darkness automatically, looking for threats that shouldn't exist in the normal world.

His phone buzzed again. Lexi.

How did it go?

Fine. She's suspicious but bought the cover story.

Good. Get some rest. I'll see you at 9.

He pocketed the phone, but he didn't head back to his dorm. Instead, he found himself walking toward the library, drawn by some instinct he couldn't name. The building was still open, though sparsely populated at this hour. He made his way to a quiet corner on the third floor, settling into a chair where he saw the entrance.

He needed to think. To process. To figure out how to balance the impossible demands on his time and energy.

But as he sat there, the exhaustion he'd been fighting all day finally caught up with him. His eyes grew heavy. The darkness behind his eyelids seemed welcoming, almost comforting, and he felt himself drifting toward it.

The dream came quickly.

He was standing in the Shadowlands, but different than he'd ever seen them. The twilight landscape stretched before him, dotted with ruins that pulsed with dark energy. In the distance, something massive moved—a presence that made the shadows themselves recoil.

"You're changing," a voice said. It sounded like Seraphina, but distorted, as if speaking through water. "The darkness is becoming you."

Then Lexi appeared before him, her green eyes wide with fear. "Drevik, run. It's hunting you. It's been hunting you all along."

"Lexi—" He reached for her, but she dissolved into shadows.

The massive presence drew closer. He felt it now, a pressure against his consciousness that made his bones ache. It wasn't just a creature. It was looking for him. Specifically. Personally.

Drevik Markinsoul, a voice echoed in his mind, ancient and terrible. I see you at last.

He woke with a gasp.

The library was dark. Somehow, hours had passed without him noticing. His phone showed 11:47 PM—he'd missed his training session with Lexi entirely.

Damn it.

He stood too quickly, his head spinning. The dream still clung to him, its images lingering at the edges of his vision. The Shadowlands had felt so real, and that presence—

It's hunting you.

He needed to tell someone. Lexi would know what it meant. Maybe the Order had encountered entities that could track acolytes through dreams.

He made his way toward the exit, his footsteps echoing in the empty corridor. The fluorescent lights had been dimmed for nighttime, casting long shadows across the floor. He moved through them automatically, his mind still processing.

The attack came without warning.

One moment he was walking; the next, something cold and dark wrapped around his ankle, yanking him off his feet. He hit the ground hard, his phone skittering across the floor. Shadows surged around him, coalescing into a shape that shouldn't exist outside the Shadowlands.

A Shadeling. Here. On campus.

Impossible, his mind screamed. The creatures couldn't cross over without a breach point. The Order maintained wards specifically to prevent—

The Shadeling lunged.

Drevik rolled aside, feeling the rush of cold air as it passed where his throat had been. He scrambled to his feet, calling on his training. Shadow Walking was useless here—he had nowhere to walk to. But combat techniques...

The Shadeling circled, its form shifting and flowing like liquid darkness. It was smaller than the ones he'd faced in the Shadowlands, but no less deadly. Its eyes—if it had eyes—fixed on him with unmistakable intent.

It wasn't attacking randomly. It was attacking him.

"You followed me," he said aloud, understanding crashing through him. "From the Shadowlands. You followed me here."

The creature didn't answer—couldn't answer—but its intent was clear. It had tracked him through whatever connection he'd formed during training, and now it was here, in the library, in the middle of the campus where thousands of people slept, unaware.

Drevik's hand closed around a letter opener someone had left on a nearby desk. Not ideal, but better than nothing. The Shadeling coiled to strike again, and he prepared himself—

The lights flickered.

The Shadeling hesitated, its form wavering. Something about the electrical current disrupted its manifestation. Drevik filed that information away, his mind racing.

Then the creature lunged again, and there was no time for thinking—only survival.

He killed it with the letter opener through its core, the motion instinctive despite his exhaustion. The Shadeling dissolved into smoke, leaving him standing in the dim library with his heart pounding and his hands shaking.

His phone lay cracked on the floor, screen shattered but still functional. He picked it up, finding three missed calls from Lexi and a string of increasingly concerned messages.

Where are you?

Training started 20 minutes ago.

Drevik, answer me.

Something's wrong. I'm coming to find you.

He typed a response with trembling fingers, his heart still racing from the attack.

Library. Third floor. Shadeling found me. It's dead.

The response came immediately.

Stay there. I'm bringing backup.

Drevik leaned against a bookshelf, trying to steady his breathing. The darkness inside him pulsed in rhythm with his heartbeat, stronger than it had been before the fight. The creature's death had fed it somehow, and he felt the power settling into his bones.

It's hunting you.

Lexi's dream-words echoed in his mind. The Shadeling hadn't been random. It had been sent, or drawn, specifically to him. And if one could find him...

More would follow.

The campus was no longer safe.

Chapter 8: Competition

Chapter 8: Competition

The Arena of Shadows had existed beneath the university for three centuries, carved from living rock and infused with so much accumulated power that the air itself felt heavy, electric. Drevik stood at the entrance, watching other acolytes filter through the arched doorway ahead of him, each one dressed in the simple gray competition robes that marked them as candidates.

His palms were sweating. He wiped them on the fabric, earning a sharp look from Lexi beside him.

"Stop that," she muttered. "You're making yourself look nervous."

"I am nervous."

"Then stop making it obvious." She smoothed the front of her own robes, the dark blue of a coach rather than a competitor. "You've trained for this. You've shadow-walked farther than anyone in your cohort. Your veil sight is better than most seekers. The only thing that can beat you today is your own head."

Drevik turned to look at her properly. In the torchlight that illuminated the corridor, her auburn hair caught fire at the edges, and her green eyes held a fierce intensity that made something in his chest tighten. She'd been drilling him for weeks, pushing him harder than she'd pushed anyone, staying late to help him refine his techniques.

"Why do you care so much?" he asked.

The question caught her off guard. He saw it in the slight widening of her eyes, the way her lips parted and then pressed together again.

"Because—" She stopped, glancing past him to where other acolytes were gathering. "Because the Order needs competent people on this mission. And because..." She met his eyes again, her voice dropping. "Because I've seen what you can do, Drevik. I've seen you push past every limitation they told you was absolute. You're going to be something remarkable, if you survive long enough to get there."

"If?"

"When." She corrected herself firmly. "When. Now get in there and show them what you are."

The Arena opened into a vast circular space, tiered stone benches rising around a central floor inlaid with silver and obsidian in patterns that hurt to look at directly. The ceiling was lost in shadow, but Drevik felt the weight of it, pressed down by centuries of accumulated power.

Elders ringed the lowest tier, their masked faces unreadable. Above them, seekers and wardens filled the benches, while acolytes and initiates packed the upper levels. The entire Order, gathered to watch a handful of candidates prove themselves worthy of the Shadowlands mission.

Drevik's name was called third. He descended the stairs to the competition floor, the silver-obsidian pattern warm beneath his bare feet, and took his place in the line of candidates. He counted eight total—four men, four women, each of them the top of their respective training groups.

Including Mira.

She stood two places down from him, her dark hair braided back from her face, her dark eyes tracking every movement in the Arena. When her gaze swept over him, it paused, and her mouth curved into something between a smile and a challenge.

He remembered her straddling him in the training room, remembered the way she'd moved, the heat of her, and felt his face warm. Her smile widened, like she knew exactly what he was thinking.

"First trial," announced Elder Corvus, his voice amplified by the acoustics of the space. "Shadow walking precision. You will each transport yourselves to the marked location and return. Speed and accuracy both judged."

A seeker stepped forward, unrolling a scroll. "Drevik Markinsoul. Target location: the reflecting pool in the eastern garden. Return to the center stone."

Drevik nodded, already visualizing the location. The reflecting pool was a hundred yards from the Arena entrance, through three corridors, past the library, in a courtyard that saw little foot traffic. He'd shadow-walked there before, during training with Lexi.

He closed his eyes, felt for the shadows around him—the torches created pockets of darkness, and the Arena itself existed in a permanent twilight that made the power pool at his feet—and pushed.

The world folded.

He opened his eyes beside the reflecting pool, water shimmering under moonlight, and felt the satisfaction of a clean transit. No disorientation, no stagger. He could have walked there faster, but that wasn't the point. The point was precision, and he'd achieved it.

He reached for the shadows again, visualized the center stone in the Arena, and pulled.

The world unfolded.

He stood in the exact center of the silver-obsidian pattern, precisely where he'd intended. Scattered applause rippled through the upper tiers, and he saw Lexi on the bench above, her arms crossed but her expression satisfied.

The other candidates took their turns. Some were faster, some slower. One woman missed her return mark by three feet, earning a frown from the elders. But it was Mira who drew the collective breath.

She didn't just walk. She danced through the shadows, vanishing and reappearing at her target location in the span of a heartbeat, then returning before the echo of her departure had faded. She landed in perfect stillness, not even the tremor of imbalance, and bowed to the elders with fluid grace.

"Flawless," said someone behind Drevik. "She's going to be hard to beat."

The second trial tested veil sight—specifically, the ability to perceive Shadowlands creatures that had been bound into the Arena for the competition. Drevik felt them before he saw them, pressure against his awareness like weights on his skin.

"Identify and locate," Elder Corvus instructed. "We have released five entities into the Arena. You have ten minutes."

Drevik closed his eyes, extending his awareness outward. The first entity was easy—a wisp, barely corporeal, hovering near the eastern entrance. The second was more substantial, a lurker pressed against the northern wall. The third and fourth required more concentration, hidden in the shadows of the upper tiers.

But the fifth—

The fifth was right behind him.

He spun, catching a glimpse of something that defied description—darkness given form, hunger given shape—and then it was gone, dissolved into nothing.

"Five down," he announced. "Four visible, one... mobile."

Elder Corvus nodded, making a note. "Locations?"

Drevik rattled them off, including the moving pattern of the fifth entity. Other candidates made their reports, some missing entities entirely, others confusing shadows for threats. When Mira's turn came, she'd found all five—but she'd also identified a sixth that the elders had hidden as a test of overconfidence.

"Very good," Elder Corvus said, and Drevik watched Mira's smile sharpen with victory.

He was falling behind. He felt it.

The third trial was combat.

Not lethal—the Order didn't waste its acolytes on death matches—but brutal enough that the infirmary always saw increased traffic after competition days. Drevik was matched against a man named Keston, broad-shouldered and confident, who'd made it clear in the training halls that he considered Drevik an upstart who didn't belong.

"Finally," Keston said, rolling his shoulders as they faced each other. "Thought I'd have to wait until the mission to put you in your place."

"Focus on the match," Drevik replied. "Not your grudge."

"Grudge?" Keston's laugh was short and sharp. "I don't have a grudge. I just don't like pretenders."

The signal came. Keston moved fast, shadow-walking to close the distance in an instant, his fist already swinging toward Drevik's jaw. But Drevik had learned from Lexi—had learned to anticipate the feel of shadow-walking, the subtle displacement of air—and he was already moving, sliding through his own shadow to appear behind Keston.

His strike caught the other man in the kidney. Keston staggered, turned, swung again. This time Drevik blocked, the impact shuddering up his arm, and used the contact to shadow-walk them both to the other side of the Arena.

Disorientation hit Keston harder. His swing went wide, and Drevik's counter connected with his temple. The fight ended three moves later, Drevik's arm locked across Keston's throat, the other man's pride the only casualty.

"Winner: Drevik Markinsoul."

He released Keston and stepped back, ignoring the glare directed at him. His hands were shaking slightly—the shadow-walk had taken more out of him than he'd expected—but he'd won. That was what mattered.

Lexi caught his eye from the benches and gave him a single nod. Pride, he realized. That was pride in her expression.

Mira's fight came next, and Drevik stayed to watch, needing to understand her style before they inevitably faced each other. Her opponent was a woman named Thessa, quick and vicious, who'd already eliminated two other candidates in earlier matches.

Mira didn't shadow-walk. She didn't need to.

She moved like water, flowing around Thessa's attacks, redirecting force rather than meeting it head-on. Her counter-strikes were precise and economical, never wasting movement, always finding the gaps in her opponent's defense. When Thessa tried to use shadow-walking to gain advantage, Mira anticipated every transit, countering before her opponent had fully materialized.

It was beautiful. And terrifying.

The match ended with Thessa on the ground, Mira's foot on her chest, her dark eyes bright with the thrill of combat.

"Winner: Mira Vance."

The final round paired the top two candidates against each other.

Drevik stood across from Mira in the center of the Arena, the silver-obsidian pattern cool beneath his feet. Around them, the gathered Order watched in silence, the weight of their attention pressing down like a physical force.

"No hard feelings," Mira said, her voice pitched for his ears alone. "Whatever happens."

"None taken."

"Good." Her smile flickered, there and gone. "Because I'm going to enjoy this."

The signal came.

Mira moved first, shadow-walking in a pattern that Drevik could barely track—here, then there, then somewhere else entirely, her attacks coming from angles that shouldn't have been possible. He blocked what he could, dodged what he couldn't, and felt each impact in his bones.

She was fast. Faster than anyone he'd faced. And she was holding back—he felt it in the precision of her strikes, the way she pulled each hit before it could do real damage.

"Not bad," she said, appearing beside him, her lips almost touching his ear. "You're still standing. Most don't, by now."

He twisted away, but she was already gone, reappearing behind him. Her kick caught him in the ribs, and he felt something crack. The pain was distant, though, his focus narrowing to the pattern of her movements.

There was a rhythm to it. A pattern. She shadow-walked on a specific timing—three rapid transits, then a pause to reorient. Three, then pause. Three, then—

On her next series, he didn't try to follow. He waited for the pause, anticipated where she'd appear, and shadow-walked to that exact spot.

His fist connected with her jaw.

The shock on her face was worth every bruise she'd given him. She staggered back, hand rising to her face, and Drevik pressed his advantage. He didn't give her time to recover, didn't let her find her rhythm again. He shadow-walked and struck, shadow-walked and struck, driving her back across the Arena floor.

But Mira Vance hadn't reached the top of her cohort by being easy to beat.

She adapted. Her rhythm changed, the timing shifting, and suddenly he was the one on the defensive, blocking attacks that came faster than he could process. Her knee found his stomach. Her elbow caught his temple. Stars exploded across his vision, and he tasted blood.

They circled each other, both breathing hard. Drevik felt his shadow-walking ability fraying at the edges—the rapid transits were draining his reserves, and he didn't have much left.

"Yield," Mira said, "and I'll make it worth your while."

"What?"

"The mission." She wiped blood from her lip, her dark eyes never leaving his. "Yield now, and I'll owe you a favor. That's not nothing, in the Order."

"I don't want favors." He straightened, ignoring the protest from his ribs. "I want my place."

Her smile returned, sharp and dangerous. "Then come take it."

She came at him in a rush, no more shadow-walking, just pure physical assault. He met her, blocked and countered, felt her strength matching his. They grappled, hands locked, struggling for leverage—and then she did something he hadn't expected.

She kissed him.

It was brief, hard, more bite than softness, and it shocked him enough that his grip loosened. She used the moment to sweep his feet, sending him crashing to the ground, and then she was above him, her weight pinning him down, her hands on his wrists.

"Yield," she said again, breathless.

He could have fought. Could have shadow-walked them both, tried to reverse their positions. But she had him pinned properly, and his reserves were nearly empty, and—truth be told—the kiss had scattered his thoughts more than he wanted to admit.

"Yield," he said, the word bitter in his mouth.

"Winner: Mira Vance."

She held him there for a moment longer than necessary, her dark eyes searching his face for something. Then she was up, offering him a hand, her expression already shifting back to neutral.

"Good match," she said. "You almost had me, at the end."

"Almost isn't enough."

"No." She pulled him to his feet. "It never is."

The rankings were posted an hour later, after the elders had tallied scores from all three trials. Drevik stood with the other candidates, his ribs aching despite the healing draught the infirmary had provided, and waited for the verdict.

First place: Mira Vance.

Second place: Drevik Markinsoul.

Third: Keston Aldric.

Fourth: Thessa Corwin.

He'd lost. He'd known he would, after that final match, but seeing it in writing still stung. Second place wasn't enough—not when the mission was what he needed, not when the answers about his parents were locked in the Shadowlands.

Elder Corvus stood, and the gathered Order fell silent.

"The trials have concluded," he announced. "Results are final. However, given the nature of the upcoming mission, the council has determined that standard selection protocols will be... adjusted."

Murmurs rippled through the benches. Drevik's head came up, hope flickering.

"The Shadowlands mission requires a team of four," Elder Corvus continued. "Two acolytes, one seeker, and one warden. The acolyte positions will be filled by our top two candidates. Mira Vance and Drevik Markinsoul will both participate."

Drevik's heart stuttered. Both. He was going.

"The seeker position will be filled by Lexi Thornwood, whose combat and reconnaissance records make her the optimal choice."

Lexi. He turned, found her in the crowd, saw her expression shift from surprise to satisfaction. She was coming too. Whatever waited in the Shadowlands, he wouldn't face it alone.

"The warden position will be determined by the council within the week. The mission departs on the new moon. Candidates, prepare yourselves."

The gathering disperse, but Drevik barely noticed. He was going. He'd failed to win, and he was still going. The relief was so profound it made his knees weak.

"Drevik."

He turned. Mira stood behind him, arms crossed, her expression unreadable.

"Second place," she said. "Not bad for an outsider."

"Not bad for someone who kissed me to win."

Her mouth curved. "Would you have preferred I didn't?"

"I would have preferred to win."

"Then next time, don't let a kiss distract you." She stepped closer, her voice dropping. "Or do. I certainly won't mind."

She walked away before he could respond, her stride confident, her victory complete. He watched her go, uncertain whether he'd just been threatened or flirted with—or both.

Lexi found him in the corridor outside the Arena, leaning against the stone wall, trying to convince his ribs that they didn't hurt as much as they thought.

"You're an idiot," she said, without preamble.

"For losing? I noticed."

"For letting her get in your head." She stopped in front of him, her green eyes sharp. "She kissed you, and you froze. I saw it."

"I didn't freeze. I—"

"You froze." She stepped closer, close enough that he could smell the herbs she used in her hair, the faint trace of smoke from the Arena torches. "Next time, when someone tries to distract you with... whatever that was... you ignore it. You focus. You win."

"There won't be a next time. We're both going."

"There's always a next time, Drevik." Her hand came up, hovering near his face, and he saw uncertainty flicker in her eyes. "In the Shadowlands, in the Order, in everything. You can't let people get that close unless you're willing to—"

She stopped. Her hand dropped.

"Unless I'm willing to what?"

"Nothing." She stepped back, her expression hardening into something professional. "Get your ribs looked at. Properly. And start preparing for the mission. The new moon is in two weeks, and I'm not letting you die because you were too stubborn to train properly."

She turned and walked away, and Drevik was left with the ghost of her touch, the echo of her unfinished sentence, and the growing certainty that the mission ahead was going to be more complicated than any of them expected.

He found Ivy in the upper gardens, sitting on a bench that overlooked the Arena entrance. Her silver hair was loose around her shoulders, her dark eyes distant, and she didn't look up when he sat beside her.

"Second place," she said.

"First loser."

"That's not fair. Mira's been training since she could walk. You've been here months." Her hand found his, cool fingers intertwining with his. "You did well."

"I lost."

"You placed. There's a difference." She squeezed his hand. "You're going to the Shadowlands. You're going to find whatever answers you're looking for. And when you come back..."

"When I come back?"

She turned to look at him, and something in her expression shifted—softened, became vulnerable in a way he'd never seen from her.

"When you come back, I'll be here. Waiting." Her thumb traced circles on the back of his hand. "That's all. I just... wanted you to know that."

He didn't know what to say. The words caught in his throat, tangled with feelings he hadn't had time to examine, with fears about the mission and hopes about what he might find and the strange, impossible situation of having multiple women look at him the way Ivy was looking at him now.

"Ivy..."

"You don't have to say anything." She released his hand, rising from the bench. "Just come back. That's all I ask."

She walked away into the darkness, leaving him alone with his thoughts, his aching ribs, and the growing certainty that whatever waited in the Shadowlands, the hardest challenges were still ahead of him.

That night, Drevik dreamed of the Shadowlands.

He stood on a precipice, looking out over a landscape of eternal twilight. Shadows moved in the distance, shapes that defied comprehension, and a voice said from somewhere behind him—

You don't belong here.

He turned, but there was no one. Only darkness, and the sense that something was watching. Waiting.

But you will.

He woke with a gasp, his ribs protesting, his skin damp with sweat. The new moon was two weeks away. The mission was coming. And somewhere in the depths of his mind, the shadows were already beginning to stir.

Chapter 9: Unquiet Hours

Chapter 9: Unquiet Hours

The ceiling of Drevik's dorm room had become a map of his own restlessness.

Two hours past midnight. Three hours until the mission briefing. Six hours until they crossed into the Shadowlands proper, and he still couldn't silence the noise in his head. Shadows pooled in the corners of his room—deeper than natural darkness, alive with the ambient magic that had seeped into his bones over months of training. They said in frequencies just below hearing, and for once, he welcomed the distraction.

His phone buzzed against his chest.

Can't sleep either?

Lexi.

He typed back without lifting the device: Shouldn't you be resting?

Her response came immediately. Shouldn't you?

He smiled despite himself, despite everything. Despite the weight of what tomorrow promised and the things he'd been running from since Lexi had kissed him in the training hall three days ago. Since Mira had pressed him against the library shelves and demanded he stop hiding. Since Ivy had looked at him with those knowing eyes and told him she understood—whatever he needed to figure out.

Since he'd realized that running wasn't working anymore.

His phone buzzed again.

Common room. Ten minutes. Don't make me come up there.

That sounded like a threat.

It was.

Drevik set the phone aside and stared at the ceiling one more moment. The shadows rippled in response to his mood—agitated, uncertain. He needed to center himself before the mission, needed clarity. Instead, he was drowning in women who made him feel things he'd spent years teaching himself not to feel.

Belonging was dangerous. He'd learned that young. But the Order had a way of dismantling every lesson his grandmother had taught him about emotional self-preservation.

He pulled on a black henley and jeans, stepped into the hallway, and let the shadows carry him down three flights of stairs to the common room in the compound's residential wing. The space was typically warm—leather couches, fire pit that burned with enchanted flame, bookshelves lined with Order histories. Tonight, it was like walking into a trap.

Lexi sat on the central couch, her auburn hair loose around her shoulders. Mira occupied the armchair to the left, her dark curls tied back, her expression sharp even in the low light. Ivy perched on the windowsill, pale and watchful, moonlight catching the silver threads in her white-blonde hair.

Three women. One man who had no idea how he'd gotten here.

"You're late," Lexi said.

"Two minutes," he countered. "And I didn't agree to anything."

"No," Mira agreed, her voice carrying that familiar edge. "You didn't agree to anything. That's rather the point."

Drevik remained standing, arms crossed. The shadows clustered near his feet like anxious pets. "I'm not doing this tonight. We have a mission in six hours—"

"Which is exactly why we're doing this tonight," Lexi interrupted, rising from the couch. The fire pit painted her features in shifting gold and amber. "In case any of us don't come back."

The words landed like stones dropped into still water. Drevik felt the ripples spread through his chest.

"That's dramatic," he said. "Even for you."

"The Shadowlands don't care about dramatic." Lexi moved toward him, each step deliberate. "You've read the briefing. You know what we're walking into. And you've spent the last three days avoiding every single one of us."

"I've been preparing—"

"You've been hiding." Mira stood from the armchair, her movements fluid and dangerous. "You've been retreating into duty because you don't want to face what's happening between us."

Drevik's jaw tightened. "There is no 'us.' There's a mission, and there are complications I don't need right before—"

"Stop."

Ivy's voice cut through the tension—soft, but carrying an authority that silenced everyone. She hadn't moved from the windowsill, but her pale eyes were fixed on Drevik with unsettling intensity.

"Stop pretending you're the only one struggling," Ivy continued. "Stop acting as though your confusion gives you the right to dismiss our feelings."

Drevik turned to face her fully. Of the three, Ivy was the quietest—the least likely to push, the most likely to retreat. If she was confronting him now, something had shifted.

"I'm not dismissing anything," he said. "I'm trying to keep focus on what matters."

"What matters." Ivy tilted her head, her expression unreadable. "You mean the Order? The mission? The heroic acolyte who doesn't need emotional entanglements?"

"I didn't say that—"

"You didn't have to." Ivy rose from the windowsill, her movements graceful and silent. "You think we haven't noticed? You think Lexi hasn't been pacing her room every night since the training hall? You think Mira hasn't been snapping at everyone who crosses her path? You think I haven't been—" She stopped, drawing a breath. "We're all waiting for you to decide. And you won't even acknowledge we're waiting."

The silence that followed was thick enough to cut.

Drevik looked between them—Lexi with her fierce defiance, Mira with her sharp challenge, Ivy with her quiet devastation. Three women who had, impossibly, inexplicably, set their sights on him. Three women he couldn't stop thinking about, even when he tried.

"I don't know what you want me to say," he finally managed.

Lexi made a sound of frustration and stepped forward, closing the distance between them until she stood directly in front of him. Her green eyes held firelight and fury and something deeper—something that made his chest tight.

"Say you feel it," she said. "Say you've been thinking about that kiss since it happened. Say you haven't been able to focus because every time you close your eyes, you see me. Or Mira. Or Ivy. Say you're just as tangled up in this as we are."

Her voice dropped on the last words, vulnerability cracking through the demand.

Drevik looked down at her—this woman who had been his rival, his mentor, his fiercest defender. Lexi, who had trained him when no one else would take him seriously. Lexi, who had believed in him before he'd believed in himself.

"I've been thinking about that kiss," he admitted, his voice rough. "Every night. Every time I try to meditate. Every time the shadows get too quiet."

Lexi's breath held. The anger in her expression flickered, replaced by something rawer.

"Then why—"

"Because I can't afford to want this." The words came harder now, torn from somewhere deep. "Because the mission tomorrow isn't routine. Because the Shadowlands don't forgive mistakes, and I've already lost people I—"

He stopped, but the damage was done. The name hung unspoken in the air. His grandmother. His parents. Everyone who had ever mattered.

Lexi's expression softened. She reached up, her hand settling against his jaw, and he let himself lean into the touch despite every instinct screaming at him to pull away.

"You think caring makes you weak," she said. "You think loving people gives the universe leverage to hurt you."

"Don't—"

"It's not weakness." Her thumb traced his cheekbone. "It's the only strength that matters. And if we're walking into the Shadowlands tomorrow, I refuse to leave things unsaid."

Drevik's control cracked. He'd been holding himself together through weeks of training, months of adjustment, years of careful emotional distance. Lexi's touch, her words, the fierce determination in her eyes—it was too much.

His hand came up to cup the back of her neck, and he pulled her to him.

The kiss was desperate and angry and relieved all at once. Lexi responded instantly, her fingers sliding into his hair, her body pressing against his with a hunger that matched his own. She tasted like cinnamon and challenge, like every argument they'd ever had transformed into something electric.

When they finally broke apart, both breathing hard, Drevik became aware of the others still watching.

Mira's expression had shifted—still sharp, but with an edge of satisfaction. Ivy's pale eyes had gone soft, her earlier intensity gentling into something that looked almost like hope.

"Finally," Mira said. "That wasn't so difficult, was it?"

"That was a beginning," Lexi corrected, still pressed against Drevik's chest. "Not an ending." She pulled back enough to meet his eyes. "I need to know where we stand. All of us. Because I won't compete—"

"You're not competing." Drevik's voice came out steadier than he expected. "I don't want you competing. I want—" He stopped, trying to articulate something he'd never allowed himself to examine.

"What do you want?" Mira asked, moving closer. "Not what you think you should want. Not what the Order expects. What do you want, Drevik?"

The question hung in the air like a challenge.

He looked at each of them in turn. Lexi, fierce and protective, who had been his anchor through chaos. Mira, brilliant and demanding, who pushed him harder than anyone and made him want to rise to meet her. Ivy, quiet and perceptive, who saw through every defense and offered understanding instead of judgment.

"I want to stop being afraid," he said finally. "I want to let myself care about people without waiting for the universe to take them away. I want—" His voice roughened. "I want you. All of you. And that terrifies me more than anything in the Shadowlands."

The confession settled into the silence like a stone finding water.

Mira's smile sharpened. "Good. Honesty. That's a start."

She moved toward him with purpose, and Drevik had a moment to register Lexi stepping aside before Mira's hand fisted in his collar and pulled him down to her level.

"If you think," she said against his lips, "that you can kiss Lexi and leave me waiting indefinitely, you understand less about women than I thought."

Then she kissed him—different from Lexi's desperate hunger, more controlled and deliberate, like she was conducting an experiment and satisfied with the results. Her lips moved against his with precision, and Drevik felt himself respond despite the exhaustion, despite the emotional upheaval. Mira kissed like she did everything—with intelligence and intent, like she was mapping his responses and filing them away.

When she released him, her eyes were bright with satisfaction.

"Acceptable," she pronounced. "Room for improvement."

"Your ego is going to be a problem," Drevik heard himself say.

"It already is." Mira stepped back, smoothing her blouse. "But at least now we understand each other."

Drevik stood in the center of the room, caught between three women, his pulse quickening and his defenses in ruins. He was like a man who'd stepped off a cliff and discovered he didn't know how to fly.

Then Ivy rose from the windowsill.

She approached with none of Lexi's fury or Mira's challenge. Her movement was patient, inevitable—like moonlight creeping across a floor.

"Drevik." Her voice was barely above a whisper. "May I?"

He swallowed hard and nodded.

Ivy's hand came up to rest against his chest, over his heart. Her touch was gentle, but her eyes held an intensity that cut through every wall he'd tried to build.

"I'm not like them," she said. "I won't demand or challenge or push. But I won't disappear either. I've been invisible my whole life—too quiet, too strange, too sensitive to the Shadowlands' call. I won't be invisible with you."

Her hand pressed firmer against his chest.

"I'll wait," she continued. "I'll give you time to figure out how this works. But I won't be a secret, and I won't be an afterthought. Do you understand?"

Drevik looked into her pale eyes and saw the depth of feeling there—the years of being overlooked, the fierce determination to matter. Ivy, who had every reason to hide, was choosing to stand in the light and ask for what she needed.

"I understand," he said, and meant it.

Ivy nodded once, then rose on her toes to press a kiss to his jaw—brief and gentle, but weighted with promise. When she pulled back, her smile was small but real.

"Good," she said. "Now we should discuss the mission. Because there are things you need to know before tomorrow."

The shift in atmosphere was immediate. Lexi straightened, her expression sharpening. Mira's playfulness vanished, replaced by the focused analyst. Ivy moved toward the couch, her earlier vulnerability tucked away behind professional focus.

Drevik followed, grateful for the change in subject even as part of him reeled from what had just happened. He settled into an armchair, and the three women arranged themselves around him—Lexi on the couch's edge, Mira in the adjacent chair, Ivy standing by the fire.

"The Shadowlands briefing was incomplete," Lexi said without preamble. "The Elders don't want to spook the acolytes, but there are things we need to prepare for."

"The Veiled Ones have been tracking increased activity near the border zones," Mira continued. "More breaches, more entities crossing into the mortal realm. Something is destabilizing the barrier."

"That's why we're going in," Drevik said. "To reinforce the anchor points."

"That's part of it." Ivy's voice was quiet but certain. "But there's something else. Something they didn't include in the official briefing."

Drevik's shadows stirred, responding to his sudden tension. "How do you know this?"

Ivy's smile was thin. "My sensitivity to the Shadowlands has certain advantages. I hear things the Elders don't realize I can hear. I see things they think are hidden."

"What things?"

Lexi leaned forward. "The entity that's been destabilizing the barrier—it's not random. Someone is directing it. Someone with knowledge of Order magic."

"Traitors?" The word tasted wrong in Drevik's mouth.

"Or True Believers," Mira said grimly. "There's a faction within the Order that thinks we've grown too cautious. They want to open the borders completely, let the Shadowlands merge with our reality."

"That's insane."

"That's fanaticism." Lexi's voice was hard. "And if they're willing to sacrifice acolytes to achieve their goals, we need to be prepared for more than routine reinforcement."

Drevik absorbed this, his mind racing through implications. The mission had seemed dangerous but manageable—a standard test of acolyte readiness. Now it sounded like walking into an ambush.

"Why tell me now?" he asked. "Why not bring this to the Elders?"

"We did," Lexi said. "Elder Thornwood listened. He promised to increase security protocols."

"But you don't think that's enough."

"I think there are Elders we can trust and Elders we can't." Mira's expression was grim. "Until we know which is which, we handle this ourselves."

Drevik nodded slowly. It made a terrible kind of sense—the Order had survived for centuries by being paranoid, by assuming infiltration was constant. Why would now be different?

"There's more," Ivy said. All eyes turned to her. "The coordinates we're meant to reinforce—they're near the sector where your mother was last seen."

The room contract. Drevik felt the shadows around him surge, responding to the sudden spike of emotion.

"My mother?"

"Officially, she was lost during a standard reconnaissance mission. Unofficially—" Ivy hesitated. "Unofficially, there are rumors. Whispers among the older members. Your mother wasn't just reconnaissance. She was looking for something. Or someone."

"Who?"

"We don't know." Lexi's voice was gentle now, careful. "But the coordinates for tomorrow's mission put us within range of her last known location. If you wanted to investigate..."

Drevik's mind raced. His mother had been a mystery his entire life—her existence confirmed only through Order records, her fate unknown. His grandmother had refused to discuss her, and his father was a question mark. The possibility of answers, of truth...

"The Elders would never authorize a detour," he said.

"No," Mira agreed. "They wouldn't. Which is why we're not asking."

Drevik looked at the three women—each determined, each ready to risk censure for him. It was more than he deserved, more than he'd ever expected to find when he'd stumbled into this world.

"Why?" he asked. "Why risk this for me?"

Lexi's expression softened. "Because you've earned it. Because you deserve to know what happened to your family. Because—"

"Because we care," Ivy finished simply. "Isn't that enough?"

Before Drevik could respond, the common room door swung open.

Seraphina Vane stood in the doorway, her dark hair disheveled, her clothes marked with travel. She looked like she'd run the entire way from wherever she'd been—her eyes wild, her breathing ragged.

"Seraphina?" Drevik stood. "What are you doing here? You're supposed to be at the northern temple—"

"Forget the temple." She stepped inside, her gaze sweeping the room before landing on him. "I got your message about the mission coordinates. I've been trying to reach you for hours."

"We've been... discussing," Lexi said.

Seraphina's laugh was sharp and humorless. "Discussing. Good. Then you know about the coordinates. About Drevik's mother."

"We know," Mira confirmed.

"Then you know why I'm coming with you." Seraphina moved toward Drevik, her intensity palpable. "The Elders denied my request, but I'm going anyway. I have to."

"Why?" Drevik asked. "What's in that sector that's so important to you?"

Seraphina's eyes met his, and for a moment, the mask she always wore slipped. Beneath it was something raw—grief and determination and desperate hope.

"Because I'm the only one who can guide you to where your mother was last seen," she said. "Because I was there when she disappeared. Because I've spent fifteen years trying to find a way back to that place, and now you're walking straight into it."

The silence held, heavy with revelation.

"You knew my mother?" Drevik's voice came out rough.

"I served with her. I trained under her." Seraphina's jaw tightened. "And I watched her walk into a trap that was meant for me. Every day since then, I've been trying to make it right."

Drevik felt the world shift beneath his feet. The shadows around him churned, responding to emotions too tangled to name.

"Tell me everything," he said.

Seraphina nodded. "I will. But not here. Not with ears that might report to the wrong people." Her gaze swept the room again, landing on each of the women in turn. "All of you need to hear this. Because tomorrow, we're not just reinforcing anchor points. We're going to find out what happened to Elara Markinsoul—and we're going to make whoever's responsible pay."

Drevik stood in the center of the room, surrounded by women who had chosen to stand with him, facing a mystery that had haunted his entire life. The mission had transformed into something else entirely.

"Then let's move," he said. "We have six hours, and I want to know everything before we cross into the Shadowlands."

Seraphina smiled—sharp and dangerous and fierce. "Finally. Someone who understands."

The shadows around Drevik's feet settled into stillness. For the first time in days, the noise in his head had quieted into focus. Tomorrow would bring danger, revelation, possibly death. But tonight, he had clarity.

Tonight, he had people willing to fight beside him.

Tonight, for the first time in longer than he could remember, he wasn't facing the darkness alone.

End of Chapter 9

Chapter 10: Threshold

Chapter 10: Threshold

The ceremonial chamber smelled of burnt sage and something older—something that reminded Drevik of the first time he'd opened his grandmother's attic trunk and found photographs of people long dead. Memory and loss, preserved in dust.

"Remove your shoes." Seraphina's voice echoed against stone walls that hadn't seen sunlight in centuries. "The Shadowlands require skin contact for the crossing. Grounding."

Drevik toed off his sneakers, the cold floor seeping into his soles. Beside him, Lexi was already barefoot, her auburn hair pulled back in a practical braid that exposed the line of her jaw. She caught him looking and raised an eyebrow.

"Focus, Markinsoul."

"Always." But his pulse was hammering against his throat, and he suspected she knew it.

The chamber had transformed since yesterday's preparation. Where before there'd been a simple circle of salt, now intricate symbols carved the floor—spiraling patterns that shift when he looked too long. Candles burned at each cardinal point, their flames unnaturally still. And in the center of it all, a tear hung in the air like a wound that refused to heal.

Through it, Drevik glimpsed twilight. Not the gentle purple of evening, but something thicker, more substantial—a perpetual dusk that pressed against the edges of the rift like water behind glass.

Mira shifted on his left, her dark eyes tracking the way the shadows moved independent of any light source. "How long has it been since anyone crossed through this threshold?"

"Three months." Seraphina circled the perimeter, checking each symbol with the methodical attention of someone defusing a bomb. "The pathway needs to be reestablished with each crossing. The Shadowlands don't like visitors."

"Then why does it feel like it's calling?" The words left Drevik's mouth before he could stop them.

Everyone stilled.

Seraphina turned slowly, her silver-white hair catching candlelight. "What did you say?"

"The rift." He gestured toward the hanging tear, uncertain how to explain the sensation. "It feels... hungry. Not hostile, exactly. More like it's been waiting."

Lexi moved closer, her shoulder brushing his. "That's not normal," she said, low enough that only he heard. "Most acolytes feel fear at the threshold. Revulsion. Not recognition."

"I'm not saying I want to go through."

"But you don't not want to." Her green eyes searched his face with an intensity that made his chest tight. "Your darkness is responding to it. I can see it in your shadow."

He looked down. His shadow lay stretched across the floor as expected, but the edges rippled. Reached toward the rift like fingers straining for a lover's touch.

"Shit." He took an involuntary step backward, but his shadow held its ground, anchored to something he couldn't see.

"Don't fight it." Seraphina had stopped her circling, her full attention now on Drevik. "Your shadow is trying to cross ahead of you. Let it. The Shadowlands will be... disorienting. Having part of yourself already anchored there will help."

"That doesn't sound concerning at all," Mira muttered, but her hand found Drevik's wrist, grip tight. "What happens if his shadow gets there first and decides it likes the neighborhood?"

"Then we retrieve him and burn the shadow away." Seraphina's tone was clinical, matter-of-fact. "It's happened before. The Order has protocols."

"Comforting."

"It's not meant to be comforting, acolyte. It's meant to be true."

The words hung between them, heavy with implication. Drevik watched his shadow stretch further, the dark shape sliding across the floor with the purpose of a living thing. His stomach clenched, but beneath the fear, something else stirred. Curiosity. Recognition. A strange, magnetic pull that made him want to step forward instead of back.

Lexi's hand slid down to lace through his fingers. "I'll go first. If something goes wrong, I can pull you back."

"Or get pulled in with me."

"I'm stronger than I look."

He met her gaze, saw the determination there, the fierce protectiveness that had defined their relationship since he'd stumbled into this world. "I know you are. That's why you're staying put. If I lose myself in there, someone needs to be anchored enough to drag me home."

Her jaw tightened, but she didn't argue. That was new. In the months since they'd met, Lexi had argued every point, challenged every decision, pushed back against every assertion of authority. Now she stood beside him in the face of something that terrified her—he could smell the fear beneath her skin, sharp and bright—and she let him make the call.

It meant something. He wasn't sure what yet, but it meant something.

"Form the circle." Seraphina's voice cut through his thoughts. "Hands joined. The crossing will feel like drowning. Do not let go of each other, or you'll be lost in the space between."

Drevik gripped Lexi's hand on one side, Mira's on the other. Beyond Mira, Seraphina completed the circle, her palm cool and dry against Mira's skin. The four of them stood at the edge of the ritual space, the rift pulsing before them like a heartbeat.

"Close your eyes," Seraphina instructed. "Breathe. When you feel the pull, step forward. Together. One step at a time."

Drevik closed his eyes.

The darkness behind his lids wasn't empty. Shapes moved there—fragments of memory, pieces of dreams, the shadow-self that had been stretching toward the rift. He felt it now, the part of him that had already begun crossing. A thin tendril of connection stretched between his body and his shadow, pulling taut as his awareness split.

Come home, said something in the dark. Not words, exactly. More like intention, like hunger given form. Come home come home come home—

"Drevik." Lexi's voice, sharp and close. "Your nose is bleeding."

He opened his eyes. A drop of crimson had splashed against his bare foot, bright against the stone. He swiped at his face, felt the wet warmth, the metallic tang that coated his lips.

"It's fine." He didn't know if he was lying. "The threshold is pulling harder than expected."

"Then we don't cross." Lexi's grip tightened. "Seraphina, we abort."

"No." The word came from Drevik's mouth, but it didn't feel like his own. "If we don't cross now, the path closes. The alignment won't return for weeks, and the breach we're tracking will have time to spread. We go."

"Your shadow is eating you from the inside out—"

"I said we go." He looked at Lexi, saw the fear and frustration warring in her expression. "Trust me."

A beat of silence. Two.

Then she exhaled, a sharp hiss through her teeth. "If you die in there, I'm going to be so fucking angry."

"Fair."

They stepped forward.

The world inverted.

Drevik had expected darkness—he'd prepared for the absence of light, for the cold press of shadow against his skin. What he wasn't prepared for was the weight. The Shadowlands pressed against him from all sides, dense and heavy and aware, like swimming through water that knew your name.

Sound worked differently here. When he opened his mouth to breathe, the air tasted of copper and old stone. The ground beneath his feet gave slightly with each step, spongy and uncertain, though when he looked down, he saw only darkness. No floor. No sky. Just endless twilight stretching in every direction, broken by shapes that might have been trees or might have been bones.

"Don't let go." Seraphina's voice came from everywhere and nowhere, muffled by the pressing reality. "The landscape will try to separate you. It's not malicious—the Shadowlands simply don't understand permanence the way we do."

Drevik gripped Lexi's hand tighter, felt her fingers dig into his palm in return. Beside her, Mira had gone pale, her usual sharp edges softened by something that might have been wonder or might have been terror.

"The camp." Drevik's voice came out strange, distorted by the thick air. "Where are we going?"

Seraphina pointed. He followed the line of her finger and saw—something. A structure in the distance, angles wrong, geometry that made his eyes water. It shift even as he watched, reforming into new configurations like a building dreaming of itself.

"That's not where we're heading." Seraphina started walking, pulling them with her. "The Shadowlands reflect intent. What you're seeing is your mind trying to make sense of the landscape. Focus on my footsteps. Nothing else."

Easier said than done.

The ground pulsed beneath them, each step sending ripples through the darkness. As they walked, Drevik became aware of sounds at the edge of hearing—whispers that slithered away when he tried to focus, laughter that echoed from impossible directions. His shadow had merged with the landscape, and he felt it now, stretched thin across the Shadowlands like a fishing line cast into deep water.

There you are, said the whisper. We've been waiting.

"Something's talking to me." He kept his voice level, controlled. "In my head."

"What's it saying?" Lexi was pressed against his side now, her body warm despite the ambient chill. Her fear-scent had sharpened, cutting through the copper taste of the air.

"That it's been waiting."

"Tell it to get in line." Mira's attempt at humor fell flat, her voice thin. "We've been waiting too. For weeks. For this whole goddamn mission."

The landscape shifted around them. Drevik blinked, and suddenly trees stood where there'd been emptiness—twisted things with bark like charred skin and branches that moved without wind. They formed a path, or maybe a corridor, their limbs reaching toward each other overhead until the twilight darkened into something closer to true night.

"The Shadowlands are responding to us." Seraphina didn't slow. "Emotions here have weight. Fear shapes the landscape. Stay calm, and the path stays clear."

"Stay calm," Lexi muttered. "In a realm that eats fear and shits nightmares. Sure. Easy."

Drevik pulled her closer, his arm wrapping around her waist without conscious thought. She stiffened, then relaxed into his hold, her body fitting against his like she'd always been there.

"I've got you," he said against her hair. "You're not falling behind."

"Neither are you." Her hand pressed flat against his chest, right over his heart. "Whatever's calling you, whatever's pulling at your shadow—I'm not letting go."

He looked down at her. In the strange twilight, her eyes had taken on an almost luminous quality, green flecked with gold. She was beautiful in a way that had nothing to do with the realm's distortions—beautiful in the way of someone who'd chosen to stand beside him despite every reason to run.

"Later," he said, voice rough. "When we get back. We need to talk."

"Talk." Her mouth curved, something between a smile and a grimace. "Is that what we're calling it?"

"I'm calling it whatever gets us both out of here alive."

"Then walk, Markinsoul. And don't stop until we're through."

They moved.

Hours passed, or what was like hours—time moved strangely in the Shadowlands, stretching and compressing in ways that made Drevik's head spin if he thought about it too hard. The landscape continued to shift around them, but Seraphina's guidance kept them on course. When the ground tried to soften into something that would swallow their feet, she found solid path. When the trees pressed too close, their branches reaching with hungry intent, her will cleared the way.

"How do you know where to go?" Mira asked during a rest, her back against one of the twisted trunks. "This place changes every time I blink."

"It responds to certainty." Seraphina stood apart from the group, her attention on the distance. "I know where I'm going because I refuse to be lost. The Shadowlands respect conviction."

"Respect." Drevik tested the word. "That's an interesting choice. Not obey. Not understand. Respect."

"The Shadowlands have their own will, their own desires. They're not a tool to be used—they're a partner to be negotiated with." Her pale eyes found his. "Something you seem to understand instinctively, acolyte. Your shadow has been guiding us for the past hour."

Drevik looked down. His shadow stretched ahead of them, no longer connected to his feet but still tethered to his body by that thin, invisible thread. It had taken on a shape that didn't match his movements—something larger, more defined, moving through the darkness with purpose.

"That's not normal." He'd known it wasn't normal. But saying it aloud made it real in a way it hadn't been before.

"No." Seraphina's expression gave nothing away. "It's not. Your connection to this realm is deeper than any acolyte I've trained. Deeper than most Elders. When we return, there will be questions."

"Questions." He laughed, the sound brittle. "Is that what we're calling them?"

"Interrogation, then. Examination. The Order doesn't take kindly to unknowns." Her mouth curved, just slightly. "But that's a problem for later. Right now, we need to reach the breach point before the landscape realizes we've stopped."

They moved again.

The deeper they traveled, the more the Shadowlands seemed to notice them. Drevik felt eyes on his skin—not physical eyes, but the weight of attention, of being observed. The whispers had grown louder, more distinct, and he found himself catching fragments of meaning:

...the one with the shadow...

...finally come home...

...the gate opens...

"There." Seraphina's voice cut through the whispers like a blade. "The camp."

Drevik followed her gesture and stopped.

In a clearing that hadn't existed moments before, a cluster of tents stood arranged in a loose circle. They were ordinary tents—nylon and aluminum poles, the kind you could buy at any outdoor store—but they looked wrong against the twisted landscape. Human geometry imposed on a realm that didn't understand it.

The camp had been abandoned recently. Very recently. A cooking fire still smoldered, smoke rising in a column that freeze in the air rather than dissipate. A pack leaned against one of the tent poles, its contents spilled across the ground: clothes, a flashlight, protein bars in wrappers that reflected the twilight.

"Someone was living here." Lexi moved forward, her investigation instincts overriding caution. "Recently. There's still coffee in that mug."

Drevik followed her, his shadow stretching ahead like a scout. The ground around the camp was solid—too solid, as if someone had willed it to stay fixed. He knelt beside the fire, felt the warmth still radiating from the coals. "An hour ago. Maybe less."

"Who crosses into the Shadowlands and sets up camp?" Mira circled the perimeter, her movements sharp, predatory. "This place isn't for living. It's for passing through."

"Unless you can't leave." Seraphina's voice had gone quiet. "Unless you're trapped."

Drevik stood, his eyes tracking the ground around the camp. The landscape had begun to respond to their presence, trees pressing closer, shadows deepening. But through the distortion, he saw something else.

Footprints.

Human footprints, leading away from the camp in a direction that made his shadow strain against its tether.

"Someone's been here for weeks." He followed the prints with his eyes, saw them disappear into the darkness ahead. "Living here. Surviving. And recently, they moved deeper in."

"Surviving on what?" Lexi appeared beside him, her shoulder brushing his. "There's no food in this realm. No water. Just... shadow."

"Shadow sustains those who belong to it." Seraphina had gone still, her face unreadable. "If someone has been living here, they've been changed by it. Fed by it. The Shadowlands would have claimed them by now."

"Claimed them how?" Mira's hand had found her blade, a shadow-forged weapon she'd carried since their first mission together. "What comes back from a place like this?"

"Nothing human."

The word hung in the air, heavy with implication.

Drevik looked at the footprints again, at the trail leading deeper into the Shadowlands. His shadow wanted to follow—he felt it pulling, straining, desperate to chase whatever had made those marks. The whispers had grown clearer, almost audible:

...the lost one...

...the one who stayed...

...the gatekeeper waiting...

"We follow the trail." The decision came from somewhere deeper than his conscious mind. "If someone's been living here, they might have information about the breaches. They might know what's causing them."

"Drevik—" Lexi's hand closed around his wrist. "Your shadow. It's trying to take over."

He looked down. His shadow had grown, stretched, taken on dimensions that didn't match the light. It pooled around his feet like something liquid, reaching upward with tendrils that brushed against his ankles, his calves.

"It's not trying to take over." He felt it now, the connection between himself and the darkness. "It's trying to protect me. Guide me." He met Lexi's eyes. "The realm recognizes me. I don't know why. But it's not trying to hurt me—it's trying to help."

"That's what they always say." Mira had moved to his other side, her blade drawn but held low. "Right before they get swallowed."

"Then you should probably stay close."

"Like I'd let you walk into the dark alone." Lexi's grip tightened. "You're stuck with me, Markinsoul. Whether you like it or not."

"I like it." The words came out rougher than intended. "I like it a lot."

Her eyes widened slightly, something shifting behind the fear and determination. In the strange twilight, with his shadow reaching for the unknown and the weight of the Shadowlands pressing down, he wanted to kiss her.
