The desire hit him like a wave—sudden, overwhelming, completely inappropriate for the moment.

Later, he reminded himself. When they got back. When they survived.

"Let's move." Seraphina had already started forward, following the trail of footprints. "Whatever's waiting ahead, I'd rather face it head-on than let the landscape trap us here."

They followed.

The deeper they went, the more the Shadowlands opened around them. The oppressive weight lift, replaced by something that felt almost like anticipation. Drevik's shadow stretched further ahead, scouting paths he couldn't see, finding solid ground in the shifting landscape.

And then they found her.

She stood in a clearing that shouldn't exist—perfectly circular, perfectly still, ringed by trees that had stopped moving. Her back was to them, her figure slight, her hair a dark cascade down her shoulders. She wore ordinary clothes—jeans, a t-shirt, sneakers that had seen better days—but she stood in the Shadowlands like she belonged there.

When she turned, Drevik's breath held.

Her face was young, maybe his age, maybe younger. Her eyes were the problem. They reflected the twilight with an intensity that wasn't human, and when she smiled, her teeth were too sharp, her expression too knowing.

"Finally," she said, her voice carrying across the clearing without rising. "I've been waiting for you, shadow-walker. The realm told me you were coming."

"Who are you?" Seraphina had moved to the front, her stance protective. "How long have you been here?"

"I'm nobody." The girl's smile widened. "I'm everyone. I'm the space between spaces." Her too-sharp eyes found Drevik, pinned him in place. "But you already know that, don't you? You can feel it. The part of you that's already home."

Drevik's shadow had gone still. Completely still, for the first time since they'd crossed the threshold. It stretched toward the girl like a hand reaching for its reflection.

"What are you?" His voice came out steady despite the fear curling in his chest.

"I'm what happens when you stay too long." She tilted her head, a gesture that should have been human but wasn't. "I'm what the Shadowlands make of mortals who refuse to leave. I'm the gate that's been waiting to open."

Behind him, Lexi's hand found his. Her fingers were cold, trembling slightly.

"We're not here for cryptic bullshit," Mira called out, her blade raised. "We're here for answers. Start giving them, or I start cutting."

The girl laughed, the sound echoing in ways that hurt Drevik's ears. "Cutting. Yes. That's what the last ones tried too. The ones who came before you." Her smile faded, replaced by something older, hungrier. "They're part of me now. Part of the landscape. Would you like to join them?"

The trees move.

Not the gradual shifting they'd seen before—this was purposeful, predatory. Branches reached toward them like fingers, roots rose from the ground like serpents. The twilight darkened, pressing down, and Drevik felt the weight of the realm settling around them like a fist.

"Run," Seraphina ordered, but the word was barely out of her mouth before the clearing close.

Drevik's shadow moved without conscious thought. It spread outward, slammed against the encroaching trees, held them back with a force he didn't know he possessed. The effort sent pain lancing through his skull—hot, bright, blinding—but he held on.

"Go," he gasped. "I'll hold it. Get everyone out."

"Not without you." Lexi refused to release his hand. "I told you. You're stuck with me."

"Lexi—"

"Shut up and run, Markinsoul."

She pulled him forward, and he let her. Behind them, the clearing collapsed, the girl's laughter following them like a ghost as they fled through the twisting landscape.

The Shadowlands had noticed them.

And something in Drevik's blood sang in response.

They didn't stop running until Seraphina found a defensible position—a hollow between two massive trees whose roots formed a natural shelter. They collapsed inside, breathing hard, the sound of pursuit fading behind them.

"That was a gatekeeper." Seraphina's voice was flat. "A human who's been transformed by long exposure to the Shadowlands. They guard territory, control access. She claimed that area."

"She said she was waiting for me." Drevik pressed his back against the wood, feeling his pulse hammer against his ribs. "For us. How would she know we were coming?"

"The realm told her." Seraphina's eyes had gone distant. "Gatekeepers develop a connection to the Shadowlands. They sense changes, movements. If she was waiting specifically for you..."

"Then someone sent her." Lexi had moved to sit beside him, her shoulder pressed against his. "Someone wanted us to find her."

"Or someone wanted to test us." Mira had taken position at the entrance, her blade still drawn. "See how far we'd get before the realm swallowed us whole."

Drevik closed his eyes, felt the weight of exhaustion settling into his bones. His shadow had returned to normal—mostly. It still rippled at the edges, still reached for the darkness beyond their shelter, but it had reconnected to his feet. The pain in his skull had faded to a dull throb.

"We need to keep moving." He opened his eyes. "That thing—the gatekeeper—she'll come looking. And she won't be alone."

"The Shadowlands will be hunting us now." Seraphina stood, her face hard. "We've drawn attention. The realm knows we're here, and it's curious."

"Curious." Mira's laugh was sharp. "Great. The nightmare dimension is curious about us."

Lexi's hand found Drevik's in the darkness. Her fingers were still cold, but steady now. She didn't say anything—didn't need to. The press of her palm against his said enough.

We'll get through this.

I'm not letting go.

He squeezed back, once, and felt her squeeze in return.

"We find the breach," he said, forcing himself to stand. "We close it. We get out. Questions about what I am can wait until we're not being hunted by a nightmare dimension."

"Agreed." Seraphina moved toward the entrance. "The breach is northeast. We move fast, we move quiet, and we don't stop for anything."

They moved.

And as they traveled deeper into the Shadowlands, Drevik felt the realm pressing against his mind, curious, hungry, and waiting.

Welcome home, it said. We've been waiting so long.

His shadow rippled in response, reaching toward the darkness with something that felt terrifyingly like joy.

The breach appeared without warning.

One moment they were navigating through trees that moved like living things; the next, the landscape opened into a crater of absolute darkness. The edges were ragged, torn, as if something had ripped through from the other side.

"That's not natural." Mira's voice had gone quiet. "That's a wound."

"Someone forced their way through." Seraphina knelt at the edge, her hand hovering over the void. "From our side. Someone opened a door and didn't close it properly."

The breach pulsed. Drevik felt it in his shadow—a pull, a drag, like gravity had suddenly reversed. The darkness wanted him to fall in, to surrender, to let go.

"That's where the gatekeeper came from." He saw it now—the connection between the woman and the wound. "She fell through. Or was pushed."

"And the Shadowlands claimed her." Lexi's voice was grim. "Turned her into something that doesn't belong anywhere."

Drevik stepped closer to the edge. His shadow stretched down into the void, exploring, mapping. He felt the shape of the breach—the way it had been made, the carelessness of it, the power that had torn through without regard for consequences.

"I can close it." The words came from somewhere deeper than thought. "My shadow can fill the gap. Seal it."

Seraphina stood sharply. "That's not—"

"I know what it will cost." He felt it already—the way the darkness wanted more of him, the way it would take and take until there was nothing left. "But if I don't, more gatekeepers will come through. More breaches will open. The Shadowlands are already pushing against the edges of our world."

Lexi's hand closed around his arm. "There has to be another way."

"I don't think there is." He met her eyes, saw the fear and the anger and the something else she refused to name. "I'll be careful. I won't let it take too much."

"You don't know what 'too much' means in a place like this."

"No." He pulled her close, pressed his forehead against hers. "But I know you'll pull me back if I go too far."

Her breath held. Her hands came up to frame his face, her touch gentle despite the strength in her fingers.

"If you die in there, I'll find whatever afterlife you end up in and drag you back myself."

"That's the most romantic thing you've ever said to me."

"I hate you."

"I know."

He kissed her—brief, fierce, full of everything they hadn't said. Then he stepped back, turned toward the breach, and let his shadow reach for the void.

The Shadowlands rose to meet him.

And Drevik Markinsoul fell into the darkness of his own power, praying he would be strong enough to climb back out.

The sealing took everything he had.

He poured his shadow into the breach, filling the gaps, knitting the edges together with darkness that was like extension of his own soul. The Shadowlands resisted—they wanted the door to stay open, wanted the connection to the mortal realm—but his will was stronger.

Close, he commanded, and the darkness obeyed.

When it was done, when the breach had smoothed over into nothing, he found himself on his knees, gasping, his vision swimming with colors that didn't exist. Hands pulled at him—Lexi's hands, steady and sure—and then he was being held, carried, supported.

"Stay with us." Her voice came from far away. "Drevik. Stay with me."

"Always," he tried to say, but his voice was gone, lost in the darkness.

The last thing he saw before unconsciousness took him was Lexi's face, her green eyes fierce with tears she refused to shed, her mouth forming words he couldn't hear.

I've got you.

I'm not letting go.

Then the Shadowlands closed around him, and Drevik Markinsoul fell into a darkness that, for the first time, was like home.

He woke to twilight and the sound of Lexi's voice.

"...don't care what the protocols say. He saved us. He closed the breach. If you want to report him, you'll have to go through me."

"He's waking." Seraphina's voice, calm and measured. "Give him space."

Drevik opened his eyes. The Shadowlands pressed around them, but differently now—softer, less predatory. His shadow had returned to its proper place, stretched beneath him like a sleeping thing.

"Easy." Lexi's face appeared above him, her expression caught between relief and fury. "You've been out for hours. The realm was... it was trying to keep you."

"I closed it." His voice came out rough, damaged. "The breach."

"You closed it." Seraphina knelt beside him, her pale eyes intent. "And in doing so, you formed a connection to this realm that wasn't there before. The Shadowlands know you now. They'll remember you."

"Is that bad?"

"That remains to be seen." She stood. "For now, we need to move. The gatekeeper is still out there, and the realm's attention has been drawn. We need to reach the extraction point before nightfall."

Drevik sat up, felt the world tilt and steady. Lexi's hand remained on his shoulder, anchoring him.

"How do you feel?" she asked, quiet enough that only he heard.

"Different." He looked at his hands, saw the way his shadow didn't quite match his movements. "Stronger. Like something that was missing finally clicked into place."

"That's what scares me." Her fingers tightened on his shoulder. "You're changing, Drevik. Every time we come here, you lose a little more of what makes you human."

"I'm still here." He covered her hand with his own. "I'm still me."

"For now." Her voice was barely a whisper. "But I've seen what this realm does to people. I've seen what happens when someone stays too long, lets the shadows in too deep." Her eyes met his, fierce and frightened. "I'm not going to watch you become another gatekeeper."

"You won't." He stood, pulling her up with him. "I promise."

"Your promises don't mean anything if you're not human enough to keep them."

It was the most honest thing she'd ever said to him. And it terrified him more than the Shadowlands ever could.

"Then I'll stay human." He released her hand, stepped back. "For you."

She held his gaze for a long moment, something unspoken passing between them. Then she turned away, her braid swinging, her posture rigid with everything she wasn't saying.

"Move out," Seraphina called. "We have a long walk ahead, and the realm is watching."

They moved.

And behind them, in the darkness, something else moved as well—something that had learned Drevik's scent, his shadow, his soul.

Something that would be waiting when he returned.

They reached the extraction point as the twilight deepened—though in the Shadowlands, it was always twilight, always that perpetual moment between day and night. The threshold hung in the air like a mirror, reflecting their world back at them.

Seraphina went first, stepping through with practiced ease. Mira followed, her usual sharp edges dulled by exhaustion.

Drevik hung back, his eyes on the darkness behind them.

"Something's coming." He felt it now—the attention of the realm, the curiosity that had become something hungrier. "The gatekeeper. She's following."

"Then we close the door before she can get through." Lexi stood beside him, her hand finding his. "Together."

He looked at her—really looked, taking in the determination in her jaw, the fear she refused to acknowledge, the way she positioned herself between him and the darkness.

"I meant what I said." His voice came out rough. "I'll stay human. For you."

"I meant what I said too." Her grip tightened. "I'll drag you back from whatever afterlife you end up in. So don't think death gets you out of this."

"I wouldn't dream of it."

They stepped through together.

And on the other side, in the chamber that smelled of burnt sage and memory, Drevik Markinsoul returned to a world that suddenly felt smaller, dimmer, less real than the one he'd left behind.

His shadow stretched behind him, content.

Waiting.

Home, it say. We're finally home.

And somewhere in the Shadowlands, something laughed.

End of Chapter 10

Chapter 11: Shadows Within

Chapter 11: Shadows Within

The Shadowlands had teeth.

Drevik felt them sinking into his consciousness with every step—small, insistent bites that nibbled at the edges of his sanity. The realm of eternal twilight pressed against his skin like a living thing, and somewhere deep in his chest, something was waking up.

Something hungry.

"Stop," Seraphina commanded, her voice cutting through the oppressive silence. She held up one hand, silver rings catching light from no visible source. "We're not alone."

Drevik froze, his boots sinking into ground that wasn't quite solid. Around them, the landscape twisted—ancient trees with bark like charred bone stretched toward a sky that held no stars, only swirling curtains of deep purple and black. The air tasted of copper and forgotten memories.

Lexi moved to his left flank, her auburn hair plastered to her neck with sweat despite the cold. Her green eyes scanned the shadows with practiced intensity. "I don't see anything."

"That's rather the point." Seraphina's通常 composed features held an edge of tension Drevik hadn't seen before. "The things that hunt here don't wish to be seen. Not until they're ready."

Mira clutched her jacket tighter, the young initiate's face pale but determined. She'd insisted on coming despite the risks—something about proving herself. Drevik admired her courage even as he questioned her judgment.

Just like mine.

"What do we do?" Mira asked, her voice smaller than she probably intended.

Drevik felt the answer before anyone spoke it. The darkness inside him stirred, stretching like a cat waking from a long nap. It recognized this place—not the specific location, but the nature of it. The Shadowlands weren't foreign territory.

They were home.

"We move forward," he said, and the words came from somewhere deeper than his throat. "Whatever's watching us has already made its decision. Running won't change anything."

Lexi shot him a look that carried a dozen arguments, but she didn't contradict him. Her jaw tightened. "Fine. But stay close, and if I tell you to run—"

"I run. I know." Drevik managed a thin smile. "You've only told me thirty times since we entered."

"Thirty-one," she corrected. "I counted."

He would have laughed under different circumstances. Would have let himself appreciate the way her dry humor cut through his fear. But the thing inside him was too busy reaching—extending tendrils of awareness toward the shadows around them like a plant turning toward sunlight.

Let me out, it whisper. Let me see. Let me hunt.

Drevik pressed his palm against his sternum, feeling his heartbeat thunder beneath his ribs. "Which way?"

Seraphina consulted the small obsidian disc in her hand, its surface swirling with faint luminescence. "Northeast. The breach point should be less than a mile. But the readings are... strange." She frowned. "The path keeps shifting."

"Shifting how?" Lexi moved closer to Drevik, her shoulder brushing his. The contact sent warmth flooding through him—sharp contrast to the cold seeping into his bones.

"Like something is redirecting us." Seraphina's dark eyes met Drevik's, and something passed between them. Recognition, perhaps. Or warning. "The Shadowlands sense us. And they're... curious."

"Curious." Mira's voice cracked slightly. "About what?"

"About him." Seraphina didn't look away from Drevik. "The darkness in you—it's not entirely yours, is it? It's been here before. Part of it remembers."

Drevik's mouth went dry. "I don't understand."

"No. You wouldn't yet." Seraphina tucked the disc away. "But you will. Come. We're losing time."

They moved deeper into the Shadowlands, and with every step, Drevik felt the realm pressing closer. Not hostile—curious. Like a child poking at a strange insect to see what it would do.

The darkness inside him responded in kind. It unfurled through his veins, cold and familiar, and he let it. Let it spread through his fingers and toes, let it settle behind his eyes like a second sight.

The shadows around them became clearer. Sharper. He saw the way they moved—not randomly, but in patterns. Flows and eddies like water around stones.

"There," he said, pointing. "The path isn't shifting. It's being directed. Someone's channeling the shadows."

Seraphina stopped, her head tilting. "You can see that?"

"I can see... something." Drevik blinked, trying to find words for what he was perceiving. "Like currents. And there's a source—something's pulling them."

Lexi's hand found his wrist, her grip tight. "Drevik. Your eyes."

He touched his face reflexively. "What about them?"

"They're black." Her voice held a steadiness that her expression didn't match. "Completely. Even the whites."

Mira made a small sound of distress.

"It's fine." Drevik surprised himself with how calm he felt. "It doesn't hurt. It's just... letting me see."

"Letting you see what the Shadowlands see." Seraphina's voice was quiet, thoughtful. "That's not supposed to be possible for an acolyte. Even Elders struggle with True Sight in this realm."

"I don't think I'm doing it on purpose."

"No. I don't think you are either." Seraphina studied him with an intensity that made him want to step back. "The question is whether that makes it better or worse."

Before he could respond, the ground beneath them shuddered. The shadows convulsed, writhing like wounded things, and from somewhere ahead came a sound that wasn't quite a roar—more like a scream stretched into infinity.

"Everyone down!" Lexi yanked Drevik to the ground as something massive passed overhead. He caught only fragments—leathery wings, too many limbs, eyes that burned like dying stars.

It moved faster than should have been possible, vanishing into the twisting trees without pause. Hunting something else. Something that wasn't them.

Yet.

"What in the hells was that?" Mira's whisper was barely audible.

"A Hollow." Seraphina rose slowly, brushing dirt from her robes. "They're... collectors. They take things from this realm to others. Sometimes they bring things back."

"Things?"

"Best not to ask." Seraphina's usually warm voice had gone flat. "We need to move. Now. Before it circles back."

Drevik didn't need to be told twice. But as they moved through the twisting landscape, following currents only he saw, the darkness inside him grew stronger. More demanding. It wanted to take—to spread through him completely and use his body as a vessel.

You could control it, a voice said from somewhere deep. Not the darkness itself, but something adjacent. Something that felt almost like memory. You could make it serve you instead.

"Drevik." Lexi's voice cut through the haze. "You're shaking."

He looked down at his hands. She was right—tremors ran through his fingers, and his skin had gone pale as moonlight. The darkness was trying to consume him, and his body was fighting back.

"I'm fine."

"You're a terrible liar." Lexi positioned herself in front of him, forcing him to stop. "Let me see your eyes."

"Lexi—"

"Let me see." Her voice brooked no argument.

He met her gaze, letting her look. The concern in her face shifted to something harder—fear, maybe, or determination. Her fingers came up to brush his cheek, cool against his feverish skin.

"Your pupils are blown, but the black is receding." She spoke quietly, for him alone. "Whatever's happening, you're fighting it. Keep fighting."

"I don't know how much longer I can."

"Then let us help." Her thumb traced his cheekbone, a gesture so tender it made his chest ache. "You don't have to do everything alone, Drevik. No matter what the shadows tell you."

He leaned into her touch despite himself. Just for a moment. Just long enough to remember what warmth was like.

"Path's clear ahead," he managed. "About half a mile. Something's... different there. The currents are pooling."

"Pooling?" Seraphina moved closer. "Like a nexus?"

"Maybe. I don't know enough to say for certain." Drevik forced himself to straighten, to pull back from the darkness. It retreated reluctantly, like a sullen child denied a treat. "But it's the most stable path I've seen. Everything else keeps shifting."

"Then we follow it." Seraphina glanced at the sky—which held no sun, but somehow still showed passage of time. "We have perhaps three hours before we need to return. The veil weakens at night, and I'd rather not be here when the Hollows wake fully."

They pressed on, and Drevik led them through the currents he sensed. Twice more they had to hide—once from a creature that looked like a wolf made of crystallized shadow, once from something that had no visible form at all, only a presence that made the air itself scream.

Each time, Drevik felt the darkness inside him respond. I could destroy that, it said. Let me out. Let me show you.

Each time, he pushed it back. But the effort was cumulative. Each refusal cost him something.

By the time they reached the clearing, he was barely standing.

The space opened suddenly—one moment they were pushing through trees that breathe, the next they stood in a perfect circle of bare earth. In the center, a single stone pillar rose from the ground, its surface covered in symbols that hurt to look at directly.

"The breach point," Seraphina said. "It's... intact. That's not possible."

"The Order sealed these months ago." Lexi moved to the pillar, running her fingers over the symbols without touching them. "This one should have collapsed."

"Someone's maintaining it." Drevik didn't know how he knew, but the certainty settled in his bones. "The currents here—they're being fed. Deliberately."

"By who?"

"By—" He stopped, the darkness suddenly surging. Not attacking. Showing.

The world around him went still. The shadows peeled back, revealing layers of reality that had been hidden. He saw the breach—really see it—a wound in the fabric between worlds that someone was keeping open with deliberate care. Power flowed through it, power that tasted familiar.

Power that tasted like him.

"Drevik!" Lexi's voice reached him from somewhere far away. "Drevik, your nose is bleeding."

He blinked, the vision fading. Warm liquid trickled over his lips. His hand came away red.

"I saw it," he said. "The breach. It's anchored to something in the mortal realm. Something that's... connected to me."

Seraphina's expression had gone carefully blank. "Are you certain?"

"The power signature. It's similar to mine. Similar to the darkness I carry." He wiped the blood away, but more came. "Someone is using my connection to keep this open. Using me."

Lexi's grip on his arm tightened. "That's not possible. You've been with the Order for months. No one could—"

"I don't think they need my permission." Drevik looked at his hands, still trembling. Still pale. "I think the darkness in me is old. Older than I am. And I think someone has been using it for a very long time."

The silence that followed was broken only by Mira's quickened breathing.

"We need to go back," Seraphina said finally. "Now. We have what we came for."

"We don't have anything useful—"

"We have more than you realize." Seraphina was already moving toward the path they'd come by. "And your acolyte is about to collapse. Look at him, Lexi. Really look."

Lexi did. Her sharp intake of breath told him everything about how bad he must look.

"Drevik, your skin..." She touched his face again, and this time her fingers came away marked with something dark. Something that moved.

Shadow. He was bleeding shadow.

"We need to get him through the veil." Lexi's voice had gone hard with command. "Mira, support his other side. Seraphina, get us out of here."

"I'm trying." Seraphina held up the obsidian disc, now pulsing rapidly. "The path is unstable. The Shadowlands don't want to let him go."

"Then make them." Lexi's tone left no room for argument. "Whatever it takes."

Drevik wanted to tell them not to bother. Wanted to tell them the darkness was winning, was spreading, was finally taking what it had been promised. But his voice wouldn't work.

His legs stopped working too.

The last thing he saw before the shadows took him was Lexi's face—fierce and terrified in equal measure. The last thing he heard was her screaming his name.

Black.

Endless, suffocating black. The kind that existed before light was invented. The kind that would exist after the last star died.

Drevik floated in it—or stood, or knelt, the distinction didn't seem to matter here. There was no up or down. No sensation except the cold that had seeped into his bones and the hunger that had taken residence in his chest.

"You're fighting." The voice came from everywhere and nowhere. "You don't have to."

"Who are you?" His thoughts formed words without his mouth moving.

"I'm the part of you that knows what you want." The darkness rippled, and a figure emerged from it. His own face looked back at him—but different. Older. Eyes completely black. Smile too wide. "I'm the part that's been waiting."

"Waiting for what?"

"For you to stop pretending you don't want power. Stop pretending you don't want revenge against everyone who's ever hurt you. Stop pretending you don't want to take what's yours." The other-him circled slowly. "I know what you dream about, Drevik. I know what you think when you look at Lexi. When you look at Seraphina. When you think about all the ways they could be yours."

"That's not—I don't—"

"Lies." The figure's smile widened. "You've been alone your whole life. Your grandmother tried, but she couldn't understand. The other children at school couldn't see you. The Order sees you only as a tool. But I see you. I see everything."

"Why should I trust anything from this place?"

"Because I'm the only one being honest." The figure stopped in front of him. "The darkness in you isn't a curse, Drevik. It's a birthright. Your mother gave it to you before she disappeared. Your father tried to steal it back. You think it's an accident you ended up with the Order? You think any of this is random?"

"What do you know about my parents?"

"Everything." The other-him leaned close. "But I won't give it away. That's not how this works. You want answers? You have to come get them yourself. Wake up. Find the path. And when you're ready to stop being afraid of what you are..."

The figure pressed a hand to Drevik's chest—right over the hunger, the cold, the thing that had been growing inside him.

"...let me out."

He woke to soft sheets and candlelight.

Drevik blinked, disoriented. The ceiling above him was stone, crisscrossed with shadows that dance of their own accord. His body felt heavy, like it had been filled with sand, and his head pounded with the remnants of the vision.

"Easy." A hand pressed against his shoulder. "You've been out for two days."

He turned his head. Lexi sat beside the narrow bed, her auburn hair loose around her shoulders, dark circles under her eyes. She looked like she hadn't slept since he'd collapsed.

"Two days?" His voice came out as a croak.

"Thirty-seven hours, technically. But who's counting?" She tried for a smile, but it didn't reach her eyes. "How do you feel?"

"Like someone filled my veins with ice water."

"That's not far from accurate. Seraphina had to purge the shadow contamination. It..." Lexi's voice caught. "It nearly killed you. Would have, if she hadn't acted fast enough."

Drevik tried to sit up. His arms shook with the effort.

"Don't." Lexi pushed him back down, gentle but firm. "Your body is still recovering. You need rest."

"I need answers." He met her gaze. "What happened to me?"

Lexi's jaw tightened. "We don't know exactly. Seraphina's theory is that the Shadowlands recognized the darkness in you and tried to... claim it. Activate it. Whatever the right word is."

"And the breach?"

"Still open. Sealed from our side, but whoever's maintaining it has resources we didn't anticipate." She looked away. "The Order is sending more acolytes. But there's something else."

"Something else?"

Lexi's silence held. When she finally spoke, her voice was barely audible. "You told us your parents were dead."

"They are." But even as he said it, doubt crept in. The figure in his vision had mentioned his mother. Had said she'd given him the darkness.

"Seraphina did some digging while you were unconscious. The records she found..." Lexi handed him a folded piece of paper. "Your mother was a member of the Order. A Seeker. She didn't die, Drevik. She vanished. Into the Shadowlands. Twenty-one years ago."

The paper trembled in his hands. A photograph had been printed on it—faded, grainy, but clear enough to make out. A woman with dark hair and familiar green-gold eyes stood in acolyte robes, her expression serious but her eyes laughing.

"She looks like me," he said.

"She looks exactly like you." Lexi's voice was gentle. "Same eyes. Same bone structure. She was researching something in the Shadowlands when she disappeared. No body was ever found."

The darkness inside him stirred—not with hunger this time, but with recognition. Memory. Something deep and old and wanting.

Find me, it whisper. I've been waiting.

"Drevik?" Lexi's hand covered his. "What is it?"

He looked at her—really looked, letting himself see past the fear and exhaustion to the woman beneath. The one who'd stayed by his bedside for thirty-seven hours. The one who'd screamed his name when he'd fallen.

"I saw something," he said. "In the vision. Someone. He told me to find a path."

"What path?"

"I don't know yet." He set the paper aside, his hand finding hers. "But I know I can't do this alone. Any of it."

Lexi's fingers intertwined with his. "You're not alone. You haven't been alone for months. You just keep refusing to see it."

"Maybe I'm done refusing."

Her breath held. "What does that mean?"

He didn't answer with words. Instead, he pulled her toward him—gently, giving her every chance to resist. She didn't. Her lips met his with a desperation that matched his own, months of tension and fear and wanting coalescing into a single point of contact.

"Wait—" She pulled back, her forehead against his. "You're recovering. You nearly died. This isn't—"

"Lexi." He cupped her face, feeling the slight tremor in her jaw. "I nearly died. I might die tomorrow. Or the day after. And I'm tired of pretending I don't want you when every moment I spend with you is a moment I'm fighting not to touch you."

Her eyes searched his, looking for something. Whatever she found made her decision.

"To hell with it," she said, and kissed him again.

This kiss was different. Deeper. The gentle exploration of before gave way to something hungrier. Her hands found his chest, pushing aside the thin fabric of whatever shirt someone had dressed him in. His fingers tangled in her hair, pulling her closer.

She broke away long enough to shed her jacket, her tank top, the practical clothes she wore like armor. In the candlelight, her body was all muscle and softness, scars from old battles mapping her skin like constellations.

"You're staring," she said, a hint of her usual sharpness in her voice.

"I'm appreciating." He sat up properly, ignoring the protest of his muscles, and pulled her onto the bed with him. "There's a difference."

"Is there?"

"Let me show you."

He kissed her collarbone, her shoulder, the curve of her breast. She made a sound that wasn't quite a moan—more like a surrender. Her fingers found his hair, his back, pulling him closer.

"Tell me if it's too much," he said against her skin. "Tell me to stop."

"Don't you dare."

He laughed, the sound surprising him. When had he last laughed? When had he last felt anything other than the weight of responsibility and fear?

Her hands were everywhere, tracing the lines of his chest, his stomach, lower. He returned the favor, learning her body with the same intensity he'd learned everything else about her—carefully, thoroughly, with complete focus.

When he finally pressed inside her, they both went still. The sensation was overwhelming—not just physical, but emotional. This was Lexi, who'd fought him and trained him and pushed him and protected him. Lexi, who'd stayed.

"Look at me," she said.

He opened eyes he hadn't realized he'd closed. Her gaze was fierce, challenging, just like always.

"Don't disappear on me," she said. "Stay. Stay right here, with me."

"I'm not going anywhere."

He moved, and she moved with him, and for a few moments the darkness inside him went quiet. There was only Lexi—her breath, her skin, her voice saying his name like it meant something. Like he meant something.

After, they lay tangled together in the narrow bed. Her head rested on his chest, her fingers tracing absent patterns on his skin.

"We should get up," she said eventually. "Seraphina will want to know you're awake."

"Seraphina can wait."

"Drevik—"

"I mean it." He tilted her chin up, forcing her to meet his eyes. "I know this doesn't solve anything. I know we still have a breach to close and a mystery to untangle and probably a dozen other crises I don't know about yet. But right now, in this moment, I just want to stay here. With you. Can I have that?"

Her expression softened in a way he'd rarely seen. "Yeah. You can have that."

They lay in silence as the candles burned lower. Eventually, Drevik's eyes droop—not from exhaustion this time, but from peace. From warmth.

"Drevik?" Lexi's voice was soft, drowsy.

"Hmm?"

"I'm glad you didn't die."

He smiled into her hair. "Me too."

He dreamed again, but this dream was different.

The darkness didn't press in. Instead, it parted—revealing a path he hadn't seen before. Symbols floated past him, coordinates and coordinates and coordinates, burning themselves into his memory.

And at the end of the path, a woman waited.

She looked older than the photograph. Lines of exhaustion around her eyes, silver threading through dark hair. But the green-gold eyes were the same. The quiet intensity was the same.

Drevik, she said, though her lips didn't move. You found your way.

Who are you?

You know who I am.

You're dead. The records—

Are wrong. Deliberately so. She smiled, and it was a tired smile, but real. I've been waiting for you, my son. You have so much to learn. So much to become. But first, you have to find me.

Where are you?

The coordinates burned brighter. Remember these. When you're ready—truly ready—follow them. Not your body. Your shadow. Walk where light cannot go.

Why can't you come to me?

Because I'm not in the mortal realm anymore, Drevik. I'm not in any realm you know. Her expression shifted. The darkness in you is a gift. But it's also a key. Use it wisely. Trust carefully. And whatever you do—

—don't let your father find you first.

Drevik woke with a gasp, the coordinates still burning in his mind. Beside him, Lexi stirred, her hand finding his automatically.

"What is it?" she mumbled.

He stared at the ceiling, his pulse quickening, his thoughts a chaos of questions and fear and desperate hope.

"Lexi." His voice came out hoarse. "I know where she is. I know where my mother is."

She was awake instantly, sitting up, her exhaustion forgotten. "What? How?"

He turned to look at her, and whatever she saw in his face made her go still.

"She's alive," he said. "She's been waiting for me. And I know how to find her."

The shadows in the corner of the room shift, to lean closer. For once, Drevik didn't fight them.

Soon, he promised. Soon I'll understand.

But whether he was speaking to the darkness or to himself, he couldn't say.

Chapter 12: Mother's Trail

Chapter 12: Mother's Trail

The Shadowlands didn't smell like death.

Drevik had expected decay, rot, something that spoke of the endless twilight that bathed this parallel dimension. Instead, the air carried notes of ozone and something sweeter—night-blooming jasmine, maybe, though he'd never seen jasmine grow in eternal darkness.

"Stop cataloging and start moving." Lexi's voice cut through his thoughts, sharp but not unkind. Her hand found the small of his back, a touch that would have been purely functional two weeks ago. Now it carried weight. Memory. The press of her fingers against his skin carried echoes of the night before—her breath hot against his neck, the way she'd said his name like a promise she hadn't meant to make.

He stepped forward, letting the shadows fold around him.

The transition between worlds had become easier with practice. What once was like drowning in cold water now felt more like sliding into a bath—still strange, still requiring adjustment, but no longer terrifying. Lexi followed a heartbeat behind, her presence a warmth against the perpetual chill of the Shadowlands.

"Which way?" Mira called from behind them. The dark-haired seeker had insisted on joining despite barely being cleared after her injuries. Drevik suspected she didn't trust him alone with Lexi. Or maybe she didn't trust Lexi alone with him.

The dynamic between the three of them had shifted since he and Lexi had finally stopped dancing around each other. Mira's glances carried something sharper than professional concern, though he couldn't quite read what lay beneath.

Drevik pulled the worn photograph from his pocket—his mother's face smiling up at him, young and bright before everything went wrong. Behind her in the image, barely visible, stood a structure he'd only just recognized during his research in the Order's archives. A spire of black stone that shouldn't exist in the mortal realm.

"North," he said, though cardinal directions meant little here. The Shadowlands operated on different principles—intent, connection, the pull of significant locations. "The spire should be about two miles. If the archives were right."

"And if they weren't?" Lexi's hand pressed more firmly against his back.

"Then we walk until we find something that doesn't want to eat us."

"Comforting." Mira moved up beside them, her movements still carrying a slight favor to her left side. "I feel like I should mention that this is a terrible idea. The Elders specifically forbade anyone from investigating the deeper Shadowlands without full seeker escort."

"Good thing we have a seeker escort, then." Drevik gestured at Lexi.

"An acolyte and a seeker who's currently supposed to be teaching introductory shadow-walking to initiates." Mira's voice carried a thread of something that might have been amusement. "Ivy would be furious if she knew."

At the mention of Ivy's name, something tightened in Drevik's chest. The communication crystal hung against his sternum, warm but silent. Ivy had been pulled away on urgent business—something about a breach at the northern perimeter—and her absence was like a missing tooth. A constant, dull awareness of absence.

"She'd tell us to be careful and then give us seventeen contingency plans." Lexi's voice softened. "I miss her too."

The acknowledgment surprised him. Lexi had never been particularly warm toward Ivy, their relationship defined by professional respect and occasional friction. But something in her tone suggested she understood what he was feeling.

"Let's just find what we came for," Drevik said, starting forward. "Then everyone can argue about protocols later."

The Shadowlands stretched before them in waves of gray and purple, the landscape folding in on itself in ways that defied mortal geometry. Paths that looked straight curved unexpectedly. Distances compressed or expanded based on factors he couldn't identify. The Order's teachings spoke of the Shadowlands as a realm of intention—where willpower shaped reality as much as physical movement.

They walked in silence for what was like twenty minutes but could have been hours. Time moved differently here, another reason the Order limited exposure. Extended stays in the Shadowlands could age a person unpredictably, or not at all. Some seekers returned after apparent days to find years had passed in the mortal realm.

"There." Drevik pointed toward a shadow that didn't quite match its surroundings. The darkness pooled in a way that suggested structure rather than absence. "Do you see it?"

Lexi squinted, her eyes shifting into the enhanced vision that came with her training. "A building. Maybe. It's hard to tell with the way light behaves here."

"I see it clearly." Mira moved past them, her earlier caution apparently forgotten. "A tower of some kind. Black stone, just like your photograph."

The confirmation sent a jolt through Drevik's system. For weeks, he'd been chasing fragments—archival references, secondhand accounts, the mysterious circumstances of his mother's disappearance. The Order had told him she'd been lost to the Shadowlands during a routine mission. Just another casualty of the eternal war between dimensions.

But the archives had told a different story. Notes hidden in margins, supply requisitions for locations that didn't exist on official maps, and finally, the photograph—taken years after her supposed death.

"Stay close." Lexi's hand found his again, their fingers intertwining without conscious thought. The newness of the gesture still caught him off guard. Three days of physical intimacy hadn't erased years of rivalry and mistrust, but it had created something new—a foundation they were still learning to walk on.

As they approached the structure, details emerged from the shadows. The spire rose from a base of twisted rock, its surface carved with symbols Drevik didn't recognize. Not the Order's traditional glyphs, something older. Something that made his skin prickle with recognition he couldn't explain.

"She was here." The words left his mouth before he could question them. "I can feel it."

"Shadow sensitivity," Mira said, her voice careful. "Your mother's bloodline carried strong talents. It makes sense you'd pick up on her residual presence."

"Not just presence." Drevik moved toward the entrance, a doorway carved into the base of the spire. "She left something for me. I can feel it."

The interior of the structure defied its external dimensions. What should have been a cramped tower opened into a vast chamber, its ceiling lost in shadow. Candles lined the walls—not burning, but glowing with their own internal light. Someone had prepared this place. Someone had been maintaining it.

"By the Veiled Ones," Lexi said, her grip tightening on his hand. "This is a sanctuary."

Drevik had read about sanctuaries during his training—hidden spaces within the Shadowlands where members of the Order could find refuge during extended missions. They were supposed to be rare, heavily guarded secrets passed down through generations.

But this one wasn't on any map he'd studied.

"Look at the walls." Mira moved deeper into the chamber, her voice hushed with something approaching reverence. "These carvings... they're not Order work. This predates the Order."

Drevik followed her gaze, studying the symbols carved into the stone. They shift under his attention, revealing layers of meaning that slid away before he could grasp them. But one image remained constant—a figure surrounded by radiance, arms outstretched, with something emerging from the chest.

"Is that...?"

"A heart-bond ritual." Lexi's voice carried an edge of disbelief. "But that's impossible. The Order developed heart-bond magic only three hundred years ago."

"Apparently not." Mira traced one of the carvings with her fingertips. "Someone's been teaching us things we claimed to discover."

A sound echoed from deeper within the structure—not threatening, but deliberate. Footsteps, slow and measured, approaching from a passage Drevik hadn't noticed.

"Someone's here." He pulled Lexi behind him, instinct overriding training. The protective gesture felt natural now, a reflex born from their new connection.

"Wait." Mira held up a hand. "Look at the shadows."

The darkness in the passage wasn't moving toward them—it was retreating. Drawing back like a curtain to reveal a figure made entirely of shadow and light, its form shifting between humanoid and something less defined.

Drevik's heart stopped.

The entity's face held no features, yet he felt its attention fixed on him. Not hostile. Not hungry. Curious.

"You carry her blood." The voice came from everywhere and nowhere, resonating in his bones rather than his ears. "The mother's line continues."

"You knew my mother?" Drevik stepped forward despite Lexi's hand trying to hold him back. "What happened to her? Where is she?"

"Safe. Hidden. Protecting what must be protected." The entity's form flickered, showing glimpses of something vast behind the humanoid shape. "She left preparations for you. Knew you would come."

The shadows near the eastern wall rippled, and a small alcove revealed itself. Inside sat a wooden box, aged but intact, carved with the same symbols that covered the walls.

"That's hers." Drevik didn't question the certainty. He knew it the way he knew his own name. "She left that for me."

"Then take it, blood of her blood." The entity fade, its form dissolving back into the ambient shadow. "But know that truth carries weight. What you learn here cannot be unlearned."

"Wait—I have questions—" Drevik moved toward the fading presence, but Lexi caught his arm.

"Let it go. Shadow guides don't give straight answers. It's like pulling information from a particularly cryptic riddle." Her voice carried forced lightness, but her eyes remained fixed on the alcove. "Whatever your mother left, it's been waiting for you. Don't keep it waiting longer."

He approached the alcove slowly, aware of Mira and Lexi flanking him. The box sat on a pedestal of black stone, unmarked by the dust that covered everything else in the Shadowlands. Whatever protected this place had kept it pristine.

The lid opened easily, revealing contents wrapped in cloth that shimmered with protective enchantments. Drevik's hands trembled as he pulled back the fabric.

A journal. A small crystalline orb. A sealed envelope with his name written in handwriting he recognized from birthday cards and homework help sessions—his mother's careful script.

"Drevik." Mira's voice cut through his frozen moment. "The crystal. It's activated. Someone's trying to contact us."

Not us. Him. The crystal pulsed with light, and Drevik realized it was a communication device—but more sophisticated than anything the Order currently used. The enchantments layered through it spoke of power he'd only read about in theoretical texts.

He touched the crystal's surface, and Ivy's face materialized in the air above it.

"There you are." Her voice carried relief mixed with frustration. "I've been trying to reach you for an hour. The communication crystals we carry don't work in the deep Shadowlands."

"Ivy." Her name came out rougher than intended. "We found something. A sanctuary. My mother—"

"I know." Her image flickered, showing the strain of maintaining connection across dimensions. "I've been researching since you left. Drevik, your mother didn't disappear. She was hidden. By someone inside the Order."

The words hit like a physical blow. "What?"

"The archives have gaps—deliberate gaps. Someone's been removing information about her for years. But I found fragments. She was placed in the Shadowlands deliberately, given sanctuary status, and then..." Ivy's image wavered. "And then the records just stop. Like she ceased to exist."

"Because she was hiding." Lexi's voice cut through, her mind clearly working through implications. "Someone in the Order wanted her gone, but couldn't kill her. So they hid her instead."

"Or she chose to hide." Mira moved to examine the journal Drevik had set aside. "From someone. Or something."

Ivy's image stabilized, her eyes finding Drevik through the connection. "There's more. The Elders have called a meeting about you. Tomorrow night. They're discussing your 'rapid advancement' and whether it represents a threat to Order stability."

"They know I'm asking questions."

"They know you're getting close to something." Ivy's expression shifted to something softer, the professional concern giving way to personal worry. "Be careful. Whatever you find there, think twice before you share it with anyone."

The connection faded before he could respond, leaving Drevik staring at empty air.

"We need to move." Lexi's hand found his shoulder, squeezing with reassurance. "If the Elders are already suspicious, we can't be found here."

"She's right." Mira had opened the journal, scanning pages with trained speed. "This is... Drevik, your mother was researching something. Something about bloodlines and activation rituals. I don't understand half of it, but she references 'the vessel' repeatedly. And she mentions you."

The envelope sat in his hands, heavier than paper should be. His mother's handwriting on the front, his name in careful letters. Whatever was inside had been meant for him alone.

"Take everything." Lexi gathered the crystal and journal, tucking them into the pack she'd brought. "We can examine it somewhere safer."

Drevik barely registered her words. His attention was fixed on the envelope, on the possibility of answers contained within. After years of wondering, of being told his mother was simply gone, of accepting that he might never know the truth—

He opened it.

The letter inside was brief, written in the same careful hand. But the words it contained made the air leave his lungs.

My dearest Drevik,

If you're reading this, you've found the sanctuary I prepared for you. You've inherited my gifts—gifts that make you special and dangerous in equal measure.

I didn't disappear. I was hidden. And I chose to stay hidden because the alternative was watching you become a weapon for people who see you as nothing more than a vessel for power they can't access themselves.

The Order isn't what it seems. The Elders serve purposes they've kept secret for generations. They don't protect the balance—they manipulate it. And they've been waiting for someone with your specific heritage to appear for a very long time.

Trust the women who choose to love you. Your heart will be your guide when nothing else can be trusted.

And above all, remember: Don't trust the Elders. They know what you are. They're not sending you to save anyone—they're sending you to activate what's inside you.

I'm still watching. Still protecting what must be protected. When the time is right, we'll meet again.

All my love, Your mother

Drevik read the words twice, three times, letting them sink into his understanding. When he finally looked up, he found Lexi watching him with an expression that mixed concern with something deeper.

"What does it say?"

He handed her the letter without speaking. Mira moved to read over her shoulder, and the silence in the sanctuary stretched like a held breath.

Lexi finished first, her jaw tightening as she processed the implications. "This changes everything."

"Or confirms what we already suspected." Mira's voice carried a hard edge. "The Order has always had secrets. But this..."

"They're using me." Drevik's voice sounded hollow to his own ears. "Everything they've told me about rising through the ranks, about proving myself—they have another agenda entirely."

"Then we need to figure out what it is before they succeed." Lexi folded the letter carefully, tucking it into the pack with the other items. "We need to get back. We need to plan."

"We need to tell someone." Mira's eyes met Drevik's, and he saw the question she wasn't asking aloud. The question about trust and who deserved it.

"Not yet." He surprised himself with the certainty in his voice. "Not until we understand more. My mother said to trust the women who love me. That means we keep this between us for now."

Lexi's hand found his, her fingers threading through with practiced ease. "Then we keep it between us. All of it."

The return journey passed in tense silence. Drevik felt the weight of the journal in his pack, the crystal, the letter—evidence of a conspiracy that reached the highest levels of the Order. Every shadow hold watching eyes. Every step was like it carried him further from the person he'd been when he entered the Shadowlands.

By the time they emerged in the mortal realm, the sun had set. Hours had passed in what was like a single afternoon—a reminder of how differently time moved in the Shadowlands.

Mira split from them almost immediately, claiming exhaustion that Drevik suspected was an excuse. He watched her go, noting the rigid set of her shoulders.

"She's struggling." Lexi spoke softly, standing close enough that her shoulder pressed against his arm. "With us. With what we've become to each other."

"I know." Drevik turned to face her, taking in the exhaustion written in her features, the worry she was trying to hide. "Are you struggling?"

The question surprised her—he saw it in the slight widening of her eyes. But she recovered quickly, falling back into the directness that defined her.

"I'm struggling with how much I care." The admission came reluctantly, as if the words fought against her natural reserve. "I didn't expect this, Drevik. I didn't expect you."

"Is that a problem?"

"It's a complication." She stepped closer, close enough that he felt the warmth radiating from her skin. "I've spent years defining myself by my training, my rank, my ability to protect others. And now there's someone I want to protect more than any mission. More than my position."

Her hand came up to rest against his chest, over his heart. The gesture was simple, intimate in a way that went beyond physical.

"I'm not asking you to solve this tonight," she continued. "I'm just... telling you where I am. So you know."

Drevik covered her hand with his own, feeling her pulse jump at the contact. "I know where I am too. Standing right beside you."

The words were inadequate for the weight of what he felt, but her smile suggested she understood anyway. She rose on her toes, pressing her lips to his in a kiss that started gentle and deepened into something more urgent. When they finally separated, both slightly breathless, he saw something new in her eyes—not just affection, but commitment. Partnership.

"We should debrief," he said, though his body argued against anything that required moving away from her.

"We should." She didn't move either. "Tomorrow. After we've processed everything."

"After the Elders' meeting."

"That too." Her hand slid from his chest to his jaw, cupping his face. "Be careful tomorrow. If what your mother wrote is true..."

"I know." He turned his face into her palm, pressing a kiss to the center of her hand. "I'll have you watching my back."

"Always."

They stood in silence for another moment, the weight of the day pressing against them. Tomorrow would bring new challenges, new dangers, new revelations. The Elders were watching. His mother was hiding. And somewhere inside him, something was waiting to be activated—something the Order had been planning to use him for since before he knew they existed.

But for now, in this moment, he had Lexi's hand in his and the knowledge that he wasn't facing any of it alone.

"Let's go home," he said finally.

She nodded, her fingers tightening around his. "Together."

As they walked back toward campus, the shadows follow—watching, waiting, patient as only eternal darkness could be. And somewhere in the depths of the Shadowlands, in a sanctuary built by hands that predated the Order itself, the echo of his mother's presence lingered like a promise.

Trust the women who love you.

He intended to.

The communication crystal pulsed again as they reached the edge of campus. Ivy's voice emerged before her image, rushed and worried.

"Drevik, I found something else. There's a prophecy in the restricted archives—mentioning someone with your exact heritage. It says 'when the vessel carries the shadow's heart, the gates will open of their own accord.'" A pause. "Drevik, I think you're the vessel. And I think the gates they're trying to open aren't for protection—they're for something else entirely."

The connection cut before he could respond, leaving him standing in the darkness with Lexi's hand still wrapped in his and the weight of revelation settling onto his shoulders.

The Elders weren't protecting anyone.

They were waiting for him to open doors that should remain closed.

And tomorrow night, they would learn just how much he knew about their plans.

Chapter 13: Loyalties Tested

Chapter 13: Loyalties Tested

The words of his mother's letter burned in Drevik's memory like acid etched into glass.

They're using you, my son. The Order doesn't protect the balance—they exploit it. And you're the key to something much darker than they'll ever admit.

He stood at the window of the safehouse, watching shadows pool in the alley below. The lunar cycle was waxing, which meant his power was building, but it also meant the Shadowlands grew restless. Everything grew restless during the waxing moon.

"You've read it seventeen times."

Drevik didn't turn at Lexi's voice. The letter was creased now, the paper worn soft from his fingers. "Something doesn't make sense. If she knew the Order was corrupt, why leave me with them? Why let me be raised as an acolyte at all?"

"Maybe she didn't have a choice." Lexi moved to stand beside him, close enough that he felt the warmth radiating from her skin. "Maybe she was trying to protect you the only way she knew how."

"By lying to me my entire life?" Drevik finally turned to face her. In the dim light of the safehouse, Lexi's auburn hair caught the glow of the streetlamps outside, turning it to burnished copper. Her green eyes held that familiar intensity, but beneath it, he saw something else. Uncertainty. The same uncertainty that had been gnawing at him since they'd found the letter hidden in his mother's effects. "That doesn't feel like protection. It feels like abandonment."

Lexi's jaw tightened. "You don't know what it's like—the pressure inside the Order. The choices people make to survive." She crossed her arms, a defensive posture he'd learned to read over the months. "My mother made choices too. Choices I didn't understand until I was older. Sometimes the people who love us hurt us because they don't see another way."

"Is that supposed to make me feel better?"

"No." Her voice softened. "It's supposed to make you feel less alone."

Before Drevik could respond, the door to the main room swung open. Mira swept in, her dark hair pulled back in a severe knot, her expression carved from the same stone as the Order's founding pillars. Behind her came Sera, her mysterious gaze moving over the room like she was cataloging exits.

"We need to talk," Mira said. "The mission parameters have changed."

The five of them gathered around the table in the safehouse's main room: Drevik, Lexi, Mira, Sera, and Kade—the last minute addition to their team, a Warden with more experience than the rest of them combined. Kade's silver-streaked hair and scarred hands spoke to decades of service, but his eyes remained sharp, missing nothing.

Mira spread a map across the table, marking positions with quick, precise movements. "The extraction point is here. We have twelve hours before the lunar peak, which means we need to move soon if we're going to reach the shrine before the Shadowlands breach."

"Hold on." Drevik's voice cut through her tactical planning. "We're not just going to continue like nothing happened."

Mira's dark eyes fixed on him. "The mission—"

"The mission was based on intelligence from the Order." Drevik stepped forward, planting his hands on the table. "Intelligence that my mother's letter suggests might be compromised. We don't even know what we're really walking into."

"The shrine contains an artifact," Mira said, her tone carefully controlled. "An artifact that could stabilize the breach points. That hasn't changed."

"But why us?" Lexi spoke up, her voice sharp. "Why send a team of junior members for something this important? If the Order has Elders, Wardens with decades of experience—" She turned to Kade. "No offense."

"None taken." Kade's weathered face remained impassive. "She's asking the right question."

Mira's composure cracked, just slightly. "The mission requires specific abilities. Shadow Walking precision. Veil Sight acuity. You were selected because you have the necessary skills."

"Or because we're expendable." Drevik straightened. "That's what my mother's letter implies. That the Order sends acolytes on dangerous missions to test them—to see who survives. Who's useful."

"That's not—" Mira started.

"It is." Sera's soft voice silenced the argument. The mysterious woman had been quiet since they'd arrived at the safehouse, but now she leaned forward, her pale eyes reflecting lamplight like mirrors. "You know it's true, Mira. You've known since the beginning."

The silence that followed stretched thin as spider silk.

"What is she talking about?" Lexi's hand moved to the knife at her hip—a reflexive gesture, born from years of Order training. "Mira?"

Mira's face went still. Too still. The kind of stillness that preceded an explosion.

"The Order has... factions," she said finally. "Different chapters with different philosophies about how to maintain the balance. The Northern Chapter believes in testing members through practical challenges. The Southern Chapter favors tradition and careful study. The Eastern Chapter—" She stopped herself.

"The Eastern Chapter believes the Order needs new leadership." Sera's voice remained soft, but an edge ran beneath it. "They believe the current Elders have lost sight of the true purpose. And they've been watching Drevik since his Initiation."

Drevik felt the blood drain from his face. "What?"

"You have abilities that haven't been seen in generations." Mira's mask was cracking now, fragments of emotion showing through. "The Sight isn't just about perceiving the Shadowlands. You can see possibilities. Futures. That makes you valuable to people who want to reshape the Order."

"I was sent to evaluate you." The words came out flat, controlled. "To determine if you would be receptive to the Eastern Chapter's goals. To see if you could be... recruited."

The safehouse suddenly felt smaller, the walls pressing in. Drevik stared at Mira—the woman who had trained him, pushed him, challenged him. The woman he had trust.

"You were spying on me."

"I was doing my duty." Mira's chin lifted. "The mission parameters changed when your mother's letter surfaced. The Northern Chapter wants you contained. The Eastern Chapter wants you cultivated. And the Southern Chapter wants to use you as a bargaining chip in their political games."

"And you?" Drevik's voice came out harder than he intended. "What do you want?"

For the first time since he'd known her, Mira's composure shattered completely. Her carefully maintained mask fell away, revealing something raw beneath. Something that looked almost like pain.

"I wanted—" She stopped. Swallowed. Started again. "I was supposed to report on your abilities. Your weaknesses. Your loyalties. But you weren't supposed to be—" She turned away, her shoulders rigid. "You weren't supposed to matter."

Lexi moved first, positioning herself between Drevik and Mira. Her protective instincts had sharpened over the months they'd served together, and Drevik had learned to recognize the set of her shoulders, the angle of her stance. She was ready for a fight.

"What game are you playing?" Lexi demanded. "What do the Eastern Chapter really want?"

"They want change." Sera answered when Mira remained silent. "They want to tear down the old hierarchy and build something new. And they believe Drevik could be the catalyst."

"Or the excuse." Kade spoke for the first time since the confrontation began. His gravelly voice carried the weight of long experience. "A figurehead is useful. But figureheads can also be disposed of when they've served their purpose."

Drevik's head was spinning. The letter. The factions. Mira's betrayal—or was it something more complicated? He looked at each member of the team in turn, trying to see past the surface.

"The mission," he said. "The shrine. The artifact. What's really there?"

Mira turned back to face him, and something in her expression had shifted. The professional mask was gone, replaced by something more vulnerable.

"There is an artifact," she said. "But it's not what we were told. The shrine contains a binding circle—one of the oldest in existence. The Order wants to use it to seal something away. Something that's been trying to break through for centuries."

"What kind of something?" Lexi's hand hadn't left her knife.

"A Shadowlands entity," Sera said. "One of the Old Ones. The Order has been keeping it contained for three hundred years, but the seals are weakening. The Eastern Chapter believes it should be destroyed entirely. The Northern Chapter wants to study it. And the Southern Chapter—" She smiled, a thin expression without warmth. "The Southern Chapter wants to make a deal with it."

The implications settled over the room like a suffocating blanket. Drevik thought about his mother's letter, about the warnings she'd left behind. About the Order he'd trusted—the structure he'd climbed, the people he'd believed in.

"They're using me," he said. "All of them. I'm just a tool to whichever faction wins."

"Yes." Mira's agreement came without hesitation. "You are. To them."

"But not to you?"

The question hung in the air between them. Mira's dark eyes met his, and Drevik saw something there that surprised him. Something that looked almost like the same feeling he'd been trying to suppress for months.

"No," she said. "Not anymore."

The argument that followed lasted for over an hour.

Kade wanted to abort the mission entirely. "We don't have enough information," he insisted. "Walking into a three-hundred-year-old binding circle with factions fighting over the outcome? That's suicide, not strategy."

Sera argued for continuing. "The shrine is real. The threat is real. Whether or not we trust the Order's motives, the entity in that circle needs to be dealt with. If the seals fail, we're looking at a breach event that could consume the entire city."

Lexi wanted to know who they could trust. "If Mira was sent to evaluate Drevik, who else has an agenda? How do we know you're not working for the Southern Chapter?" she demanded, turning to Sera. "How do we know Kade isn't Northern Chapter's backup plan?"

"Because," Kade said dryly, "if I were Northern Chapter, I'd have killed the boy already and collected the reward for his body."

That silenced the room.

Drevik stepped away from the table, needing space to think. The safehouse felt suffocating, every shadow a potential threat. He moved toward the narrow hallway that led to the bedrooms, but stopped when he felt a hand on his arm.

Lexi's grip was firm, her fingers pressing into the muscle above his elbow.

"We need a moment," she announced to the room. "Don't follow."

She pulled him down the hallway and into the smaller of the two bedrooms, closing the door firmly behind them. The room was sparse—a narrow bed, a wooden chair, a window overlooking the alley. But it was private, and that was all they needed.

"Talk to me." Lexi's voice was quiet now, stripped of the aggression she'd shown in the main room. "What are you thinking?"

Drevik laughed, the sound hollow. "I'm thinking that everything I believed about my life is a lie. The Order, my training, my mother—" He shook his head. "I don't know what's real anymore."

"I'm real." Lexi stepped closer, close enough that he saw the gold flecks in her green eyes. "This is real. Whatever else is happening, that hasn't changed."

"Hasn't it?" Drevik's voice cracked. "You're Order-born. Your mother is an Elder. Everything you've ever believed—" He pulled away, turning toward the window. "How can I ask you to choose between me and everything you were raised to believe in?"

"You're not asking." Lexi moved behind him, her warmth pressing against his back. Her arms wrapped around his waist, pulling him against her. "I'm telling you. I made my choice months ago, Drevik. Before I even understood what I was choosing."

He turned in her arms, facing her. The lamplight from the window caught her features, illuminating the fierce determination in her expression.

"The Order raised me," she continued. "They trained me, shaped me, gave me purpose. But they didn't teach me this." She pressed her hand flat against his chest, over his heart. "They didn't teach me what it feels like to care about someone more than duty. More than hierarchy. More than survival."

"Lexi—"

"Let me finish." Her eyes glistened, but her voice remained steady. "My whole life, I've been told that the Order comes first. That personal attachments are weaknesses to be overcome. That love is a luxury we can't afford." She swallowed hard. "But watching you—watching you fight, watching you grow, watching you become the person you were meant to be—I realized something."

"What?"

"The Order is wrong." The words came out fierce, almost angry. "Not just about you. About everything. About me." She pulled back slightly, her hands moving to frame his face. "They taught me to protect the mission at all costs. But they never taught me that some things are worth more than any mission."

Drevik stared at her, his heart pounding against his ribs. He'd known, somewhere in the back of his mind, that this was coming. That the tension between them had been building toward something inevitable. But he hadn't expected it to happen like this—in a cramped safehouse room, surrounded by enemies and uncertainty, with the weight of betrayal pressing down on them from all sides.

"I don't know what happens next," he admitted. "I don't know who to trust or what path to take. But I know—" He stopped, gathering his courage. "I know I don't want to face it without you."

Lexi's expression softened. "You won't have to."

She kissed him.

It wasn't their first kiss—that had happened weeks ago, in the aftermath of a mission that had nearly killed them both. But this was different. That first kiss had been desperate, fueled by adrenaline and fear. This kiss was deliberate. A choice. A declaration.

"You've gotten more impatient."

She laughed—a genuine sound that surprised them both. In the darkness of their situation, the laughter was like a small rebellion. A refusal to let the circumstances strip them of joy.

"Maybe I'm just tired of waiting." Lexi's hands continued their exploration, sliding around to his back, pulling him toward the narrow bed. "Maybe I've decided that some things shouldn't wait."

In the dim light filtering through the window, her face was half in shadow, half in light. The duality suited her—the Order soldier and the woman who had chosen to stand against everything she'd been taught.

"I don't have protection," he said, the practical words feeling absurd in the moment.

Lexi reached into the pocket of her jacket, which had fallen to the floor beside the bed, and produced a small packet. "Acolyte training teaches us to be prepared for everything."

Drevik laughed, the sound lighter than he'd expected. "You planned this?"

"I hoped." Her expression grew serious again. "I've been hoping for a while now."

"I know." Her fingers traced idle patterns on his chest. "But it reminds me what I'm fighting for."

Mira stood by the window, her posture rigid. Sera had taken a position near the door, her mysterious gaze fixed on something only she saw. Kade remained at the table, his scarred hands folded in front of him.

Kade raised an eyebrow but said nothing.

"The shrine is real," Drevik continued. "The threat is real. Whatever the Order's motives, whatever the factions want—the entity in that binding circle needs to be dealt with. We can't just walk away."

"And after?" Mira asked. "What happens when we've completed the mission?"

"We figure out which side we're on." Drevik met her eyes, seeing the complex emotions she was trying to hide. "Or maybe we decide we don't need sides. Maybe we find our own path."

Sera smiled, a thin expression that held more warmth than he'd seen from her before. "That sounds like something the Eastern Chapter would say."

"Maybe it is." Drevik straightened. "But it's my choice. Not theirs. Not the Northern Chapter's. Not anyone's but mine."

The room fell silent, processing his declaration. Then Kade stood, his weathered face unreadable.

"Well then," he said. "We have a mission to complete. I suggest we start moving."

They gathered their gear in efficient silence—weapons, supplies, communication devices. The mission had been compromised, but they were still trained acolytes. They still had a job to do.

"I meant what I said earlier," she told him quietly. "You weren't supposed to matter."

"But I do."

"Yes." Her voice dropped lower. "You do. And that complicates things for me in ways I didn't expect."

"Why?"

She looked away, her dark eyes fixed on something in the distance. "Because somewhere along the way, you became more than an assignment. You became—" She stopped herself. "You became someone I don't want to see hurt."

Before Drevik could respond, she turned and walked away, joining Sera at the door. But her words lingered, adding another layer to the tangled web of relationships he was navigating.

The attack came without warning.

They were two blocks from the shrine when the shadows around them move. Not the natural movement of light and darkness—something else. Something wrong.

Shadowlands entities. At least a dozen of them, their forms flickering between dimensions, half-materialized and half-ghost. They moved with terrifying speed, flowing around corners, emerging from alleys, pouring out of the darkness between streetlights.

He went down hard, feeling the cold of the Shadowlands seep through his skin. The entity was trying to pull him across—to drag him into the dimension where physical laws didn't apply, where humans were nothing but prey.

Shadow Walking instinct took over. Drevik reached for the darkness, not to fight it but to become it. His form flickered, destabilizing, and suddenly he was behind the entity instead of beneath it. His blade found its core, and the thing shrieked—a sound that existed somewhere between hearing and feeling—and dissolved.

"One down!" he called, but the others were already engaged.

Lexi moved like a dancer, her blade cutting precise arcs through the air. Each strike was designed to sever the connection between the entity and the physical realm, and her efficiency was terrifying to watch. Kade fought with the methodical brutality of long experience, while Sera used some form of magic Drevik had never seen before—her hands glowing with pale light that burn the shadows away.

Mira—

Mira wasn't fighting.

She stood at the edge of the battle, her hands raised, and Drevik realized she was chanting. Not attacking. Communicating.

"Mira!" He Shadow Walked to her side, appearing in a flicker of darkness. "What are you doing?"

Her eyes were unfocused, seeing something beyond the physical realm. "They're not attacking randomly. They're here for us specifically. Someone told them where we would be."

The implications hit him like a physical blow. A traitor. Someone on their team was feeding information to the Shadowlands.

"Who?"

Mira's chant ended, and the remaining entities faltered, their coordination breaking. "I don't know. But I can feel the connection—the signal is coming from nearby."

Drevik spun, scanning the battlefield. Lexi was holding her own against two entities. Kade had dispatched another and was moving to assist Sera. Everyone was accounted for. Everyone was fighting.

Everyone except—

His gaze fell on Kade's weathered face, the silver-streaked hair, the scarred hands.

The hands that had stayed folded during the argument. The man who had said nothing during the revelations about the Order's factions.

Kade met his eyes across the chaos, and something cold and knowing passed between them.

Then the older man smiled, raised his hand in a casual salute, and stepped backward into a shadow that shouldn't have been large enough to hold him.

The Shadowlands entities renewed their attack with redoubled fury.

"Kade was the traitor," Drevik said, the realization crashing over him. "He's been signaling our position this whole time."

Mira's expression went hard. "Then we finish this without him."

The battle resumed, fiercer than before. But now Drevik knew the shape of the threat—not just the entities in front of them, but the betrayal that had brought them here.

And somewhere in the back of his mind, his mother's warning echoed again.

They're using you, my son.

The worst part was, he was starting to understand exactly how.
