"I'm saying I already am." Her eyes held his, dark and depthless. "From the moment you looked at me and saw past the mystery to the woman underneath. You see people, Drevik. That's a rare gift. And it's one I find... compelling."

Before he could respond, footsteps echoed in the hallway. Lexi appeared in the doorway, her hair still damp from a hasty shower, her expression alert.

"The perimeter's clear," she announced, then stopped, taking in the scene—Seraphina's hand on his face, their close proximity. Something flickered in her eyes, but she smoothed it away quickly. "But we have company incoming. Order vehicle, about ten minutes out."

"Friend or foe?" Drevik was already moving, reaching for the coffee he'd barely touched.

"Unknown." Lexi's voice was clipped. "But given that we're in a Vane property that's supposed to be secret, I'm leaning toward foe."

Seraphina's expression had hardened, the intimate moment forgotten. "My family hasn't shared this location with the Order. If they're here, someone's talked."

"Or someone tracked us from the cottage." Drevik's mind raced through possibilities. "My mother said the Shadowlands has interests in me. Maybe they have ways of tracking me that the Order can access."

"Either way, we need to move." Lexi was already heading for the door. "Grab your book. We leave through the back."

The morning erupted into controlled chaos. They gathered what they needed, Seraphina activating secondary wards that would obscure their exit, Lexi scouting the route with the precision of a trained operative. Within minutes, they were slipping out a hidden exit, moving through grounds that would conceal their passage.

As they reached the edge of the property, Drevik glanced back. The manor stood serene and beautiful, giving no indication of the secrets it held.

His mother's final words echoed in his mind: The Order isn't the only ones coming. The Shadowlands has its own plans for you, my son.

Behind him, a car approached on the main road—Order vehicles, probably filled with people who wanted to know what he was, what he could do, what threat he might pose.

Ahead of him, the unknown stretched like a shadow.

And in his pocket, his father's book held answers he wasn't sure he was ready to read.

He took a breath, squared his shoulders, and followed Lexi and Seraphina into the trees.

Chapter 18: Fratricide

Chapter 18: Fratricide

The first sign of betrayal was silence.

Drevik stood at the window of the safehouse, watching the empty street below. Three days they'd been here—three days of checking shadows, of mapping escape routes, of waiting for the extraction team that Commander Vance had promised. The message had come through Mira's encrypted channel: Team inbound. Trusted operatives only. Expect contact at midnight.

Midnight was seven minutes away.

Behind him, the women moved through the small space with the practiced efficiency of people who'd learned to live ready. Lexi checked her blade for the dozenth time, the soft whisper of steel against leather a familiar comfort. Seraphina sat cross-legged on the floor, her dark eyes distant as she communed with something none of them saw. Mira paced near the back door, her earlier defiance replaced by a coiled tension that made Drevik's skin prickle.

"You're doing it again," Lexi said without looking up.

"Doing what?"

"Thinking so loud I can hear it." She finally met his eyes, and something in her expression softened despite the circumstances. "They'll be here. Vance gave his word."

"That's what worries me." Drevik turned from the window. "Vance gives his word to a lot of people. I'm starting to wonder what it's worth."

Mira stopped pacing. "You don't trust him?"

"I don't trust anyone who hasn't bled for me yet."

The words hung in the air, heavier than he'd intended. Each of these women had bled for him—had risked everything. The weight of that responsibility settled into his bones like ice.

"You trust us," Seraphina said from the floor, her voice carrying that distant quality it always held when she communed with the shadows. "That's enough."

"Is it?" Drevik moved toward the center of the room, positioning himself where he saw both exits. "Three days ago, we were almost killed by people I thought were allies. Now we're supposed to believe a rescue team is coming? Because someone in the Order sent a message?"

"The message came through me," Mira said, her voice sharpening. "I verified the signatures. The encryption keys are legitimate."

"Keys can be stolen." Drevik's hand went to the shadow-blade at his hip, a weapon he'd manifested from his own connection to the darkness. "Signatures can be forged. And people who were supposed to be brothers—" He cut himself off, the old grief rising before he could suppress it.

Lexi stood, crossing to him with purposeful steps. She stopped just short of touching him, her green eyes fierce in the low light. "We don't have time for doubt. If you don't trust the extraction, we find another way out. Right now. Together." She glanced at the others. "All of us."

Drevik opened his mouth to respond when the shadows in the corner of the room shifted.

Not the natural movement of light and darkness—something deeper. Something that made his blood sing and his instincts scream simultaneously. The Veil Sight activated without conscious thought, and suddenly he saw the darkness writhing, taking shape, reaching toward them with fingers made of nothing and everything.

"They're coming," he said. "Now. Everyone down—"

The window exploded inward.

Drevik moved on instinct, grabbing Lexi and pulling her behind the overturned couch as glass showered the room. A figure descended through the shattered frame, landing with preternatural grace. Moonlight glinted off familiar features, off dark hair and a face Drevik had seen in the Order's halls a hundred times.

Kestrel. One of Vance's most trusted operatives. The woman who'd trained him in shadow-walking techniques during his first month as an acolyte.

"Hello, boy." Her voice was flat, empty of the warmth he remembered. "Commander sends his regards."

"Kestrel—" Drevik rose from behind the couch, keeping his body between her and the women. "What is this? The extraction—"

"Is real." She smiled, and it didn't reach her eyes. "We're here to extract you. Just not in the way you were hoping for."

More figures materialized from the shadows—four, five, six—spreading through the small space with the coordinated precision of a military unit. Drevik's heart slammed against his ribs as he recognized faces. People he'd trained with. People he'd shared meals with. People he'd trusted.

"How sweet," one of them said—Marcus, an acolyte who'd been just ahead of Drevik in rank. "He looks surprised. Did nobody tell him what happens to problems in the Order?"

"Vance sent you to kill me?" Drevik's voice came out steadier than he felt. "After everything—"

"Vance sent us to contain a threat." Kestrel's hand moved to the blade at her hip. "You've been running with traitors, boy. Associating with known enemies of the Order. Learning things no acolyte should know." Her smile sharpened. "And then there's the small matter of what you are."

"What I am?"

"The Commander has questions. The Elders have questions." She drew her blade, and Drevik felt the shadows recoil from the cold iron. "You can come quietly, answer those questions, and accept judgment. Or we can drag your corpse back and let the mages pull the answers from your cooling blood."

"Those are my only options?"

"Those are your only options."

Drevik glanced back at the women. Lexi had risen, her own blade drawn, positioned to protect Seraphina and Mira. The look in her eyes said she'd seen this coming—had warned him—and she was ready to fight beside him to the death.

Something in his chest settled. Calm spread through his limbs, the strange peace that came when there were no more choices to make. Only action.

"You know," he said, turning back to face Kestrel and the others, "I've spent my whole life being dragged places. Being told where to go, what to learn, who to trust." He let his power uncurl, felt the shadows in the room respond to his will. "I think I'm done with that."

"Then you choose death."

"I choose my own path."

Kestrel moved faster than he'd expected—faster than she'd ever moved in training. Her blade swept toward him in a lethal arc, and Drevik didn't try to block it. He simply... wasn't there anymore.

Shadow-walking in the same room was nearly impossible for most practitioners. The distances were too short, the concentration required too intense. But Drevik wasn't most practitioners. He emerged behind Kestrel, his own blade manifesting from pure darkness, and drove it toward her back.

She spun, parrying with inhuman speed. "You've improved."

"I had good teachers." He pressed forward, their blades meeting in a shower of sparks that shouldn't have been possible with shadow-forged steel. "That's what makes this tragic."

"That's what makes this necessary."

Marcus and two others moved to flank him, but Lexi intercepted them with a feral cry. Her blade sang through the air, catching Marcus's strike and deflecting it with practiced precision. She moved like water, like fury, her auburn hair streaming behind her as she danced between opponents.

"Stay behind me!" she shouted, and Drevik felt a flash of irritation even as gratitude surged through him.

"I can fight—"

"I know you can fight!" She blocked a strike aimed at his blind side, her body pressing against his for a brief moment as she moved. "That doesn't mean you have to fight alone!"

Mira had engaged another operative near the back door, her movements jerky with conflicting emotion. This was her chapter—her brothers and sisters in the Order. Every strike she blocked, every counter she made, cost her something visible in her expression.

"Don't hold back on my account," the operative facing her snarled. "Traitor."

"I'm not the traitor here." Mira's voice cracked, but her blade stayed steady. "The Order was supposed to protect people. Was supposed to stand for something."

"The Order stands for order. Something you've forgotten."

Seraphina remained on the floor, eyes closed, hands moving in complex patterns that made the shadows writhe and twist. Whatever she was doing, it wasn't combat—but the operatives sense it was dangerous, because two of them broke away from the main fight to rush toward her.

Drevik saw them move. Saw the killing intent in their postures. And something inside him... shifted.

The future split before his eyes—possible outcomes branching like lightning. In one, Seraphina died. In another, she lived but was captured. In a third, he reached her in time but took a wound that would slow him for weeks. In a fourth—

He chose the fifth option.

Shadow exploded outward from him in a wave of pure darkness, freezing everyone in the room for a single heartbeat. In that suspended moment, Drevik moved. Not shadow-walking—something deeper. Something that bent the rules of what was possible. He stepped between moments, between heartbeats, and placed himself in front of Seraphina.

The operatives' blades descended.

Drevik raised his hand, and the shadows obeyed.

Not just the ambient darkness of the room—every shadow, every sliver of shade, every scrap of night responded to his call. They rose like living things, wrapping around the descending blades, catching them in grips that couldn't be broken. The operatives' eyes went wide with shock.

"What are you?" Kestrel breathed from across the room.

Drevik didn't have an answer. He felt the shadows connected to him now—really connected—in a way they'd never been before. It was like feeling the heartbeat of the darkness itself, like becoming part of something vast and ancient and terrifying.

"I don't know," he admitted. "But I'm done being afraid of it."

He pushed, and the shadows flung the operatives backward, pinning them against the walls with tendrils of pure darkness. Marcus struggled against his bonds, face contorted with rage. The other operatives fought and failed, their own shadow-walking abilities useless against Drevik's command of the substance.

Kestrel alone remained free. She stood in the center of the room, blade lowered, staring at Drevik with an expression he couldn't read.

"You're supposed to be an acolyte," she said. "You're supposed to be untrained, untested. A curiosity, nothing more."

"Looks like you underestimated me."

"Looks like." She raised her blade again, and Drevik felt the shadows strain toward her, eager to bind her like the others. "But I didn't survive this long by fighting fair."

She moved—and so did the shadows. But not Drevik's shadows. The darkness around Kestrel twisted, and suddenly she wasn't where she'd been. She was behind him. Behind Lexi.

Her blade descended toward Lexi's exposed back.

"NO!"

Drevik threw himself forward, shadow-walking without thinking, trying to close the distance. But he was too slow. Too far. The blade was already falling, already—

Lexi turned.

She didn't try to dodge. Didn't try to parry. She simply turned, placing herself between Kestrel and the path to Drevik, and took the blade through her shoulder with a sound that would haunt him forever.

Time stopped.

Drevik watched Lexi's body absorb the impact, watched her eyes widen with shock and pain, watched her mouth open in a silent scream. He saw Kestrel's surprise at the woman's sacrifice, saw the operative's blade lodged deep in flesh and muscle.

Then time resumed, and Drevik's world became rage.

The shadows didn't just respond to him this time—they became him. He felt his consciousness expand, felt the darkness pour through him like water through a broken dam. The room vanished. The safehouse vanished. Everything became shadow and fury and the desperate need to destroy the thing that had hurt her.

He crossed the distance without moving. Grabbed Kestrel's wrist without touching her. Felt her bones crack under the pressure of shadows he didn't remember summoning.

"You touched her." His voice came out wrong—too deep, too resonant, like multiple voices speaking in unison. "You dared to touch her."

Kestrel screamed, and somewhere in the back of his mind, Drevik recognized that sound. It was the scream of someone facing something beyond their understanding. Something beyond human.

"Yield!" The word tore from her throat. "Yield! Please—"

"Drevik, stop!"

Mira's voice cut through the haze. He turned toward her, shadows still writhing around him, and saw her standing over Seraphina, hands raised in a placating gesture.

"If you kill her, you become what they say you are." Mira's voice shook, but her eyes held steady. "Don't give them the excuse. Don't become the monster."

The shadows hesitated. Drevik hesitated. In that moment of stillness, he became aware of the room again—of the operatives pinned against the walls, of Marcus's furious struggles, of Lexi on the floor behind him with blood spreading across her shirt.

"Lexi." The shadows collapsed as his attention shifted, rushing back to him in a wave that left him dizzy and drained. He stumbled toward her, dropping to his knees beside her fallen form. "Lexi, look at me. Stay with me."

Her eyes fluttered open, glassy with pain but still fierce. "Told you," she said. "Stay behind me."

"Don't." His voice cracked. "Don't you dare make jokes right now."

"Someone has to." Her hand found his, squeezing weakly. "Takes your mind off the dying."

"You're not dying." He pressed his hands over the wound, feeling the warm blood pulse between his fingers. "I won't let you."

"Drevik—" Seraphina's voice came from behind him, strained but steady. "The wound isn't fatal. Not if we bind it now. Move your hands."

He obeyed, watching as Seraphina crawled forward—when had she moved?—and pressed her palms over Lexi's shoulder. Dark energy flowed between them, not healing but stabilizing, slowing the blood loss to a trickle.

"The blade missed anything vital," Seraphina said, her voice carrying that distant quality again. "But she needs a healer. A real one."

"Then we get her one." Drevik turned toward the operatives, still pinned against the walls. "Where's the extraction point? Where were you supposed to take us?"

Kestrel's face was pale, her broken wrist cradled against her chest. "Commander Vance... he has a facility. Eastern district. Medical wing."

"Then you're going to take us there."

"You'll be walking into a trap."

"I don't care." Drevik rose, and the shadows responded to his anger, rising with him. "You came here to kill me. Instead, you're going to save her. And then you're going to tell me everything you know about what Vance is planning."

Kestrel's eyes met his, and he saw fear there. Real fear, the kind that came from facing something that didn't fit into your understanding of the world.

"Or?" she asked, her voice barely audible.

"Or I let the shadows decide what happens next." He let darkness curl around his fingers, let her see the power he was barely holding in check. "And they're very, very angry right now."

The silence held between them. Then Kestrel nodded, a jerky motion that spoke of defeat.

"Fine. I'll take you. But you're making a mistake, boy. Vance has resources you can't imagine. People who can do things with shadows that make what you just did look like a parlor trick."

"Then I'll deal with them too."

"You'll die."

"Maybe." Drevik glanced back at Lexi, pale and still on the floor, and felt his resolve crystallize into something harder than stone. "But not today. Not while she still needs me."

He turned to Mira, who had watched the entire exchange with an expression of mingled horror and wonder.

"Can you walk them to the extraction point? You know the routes better than I do."

"I..." Mira pull herself together. "Yes. I can get you close. But Drevik, what you just did—the shadows, the way they responded—"

"I know." He kept his voice level, fighting down the panic that wanted to rise at the memory. "I don't know what I am anymore. But I can't think about that right now. Right now, I need to get Lexi somewhere safe."

"Then we move." Seraphina had finished her work, binding Lexi's wound with strips torn from her own shirt. "She can walk, but she'll need support."

"I'll carry her."

"You'll need your hands free in case there's trouble." Seraphina's dark eyes met his, and he saw understanding there. "I know you want to hold her. But you need to be ready to fight."

The words landed like stones in his chest. She was right. He knew she was right. But looking at Lexi's pale face, at the blood still seeping through the makeshift bandages, every instinct screamed to gather her close and never let go.

"Fine." He forced the word out. "Mira, take point. Seraphina, help Lexi. I'll watch our backs."

"And the prisoners?" Mira gestured to the operatives still pinned against the walls.

Drevik looked at them—really looked. Marcus, who he'd shared meals with. Kestrel, who had trained him. Others whose faces were familiar but whose names he'd never learned. They'd come here to kill him. To drag him back for questioning, for judgment, for whatever the Order did to people like him.

But they were also people. People who had lives, families, purposes. People who had believed they were doing the right thing, even if that belief was manipulated.

"Let them go," he said.

Kestrel's eyes widened. "What?"

"I said let them go. The shadows will release you in thirty seconds. After that, you can follow us if you want. Try to complete your mission if you want." He met her gaze steadily. "But I'm not going to kill people I once called friends. I'm not going to become the monster you think I am."

"That's..." Kestrel shook her head slowly. "That's the stupidest thing I've ever heard. You're letting enemies live. Enemies who will come after you again."

"Maybe." Drevik moved to Lexi's side, helping her stand. She leaned against him, warm and alive and breathing, and that was all that mattered. "But at least I'll be able to look at myself in the mirror. Can you say the same?"

He didn't wait for an answer. With Mira in the lead and Seraphina supporting Lexi's other side, they moved toward the back door, stepping over glass and blood and the bodies of people who had tried to kill them.

Behind them, Drevik heard the shadows release their hold. Heard Kestrel's sharp intake of breath, the sound of bodies sliding down walls, the groans of the wounded.

He didn't look back.

The night air hit them like a slap, cold and sharp after the closeness of the safehouse. Drevik said it in, letting it clear some of the fog from his mind. The shadows still said at the edges of his consciousness, but quieter now, calmer. Waiting.

"You know they'll follow us," Mira said, leading them down an alley that connected to the broader streets. "You showed them what you are. They'll report back to Vance, and he'll send more. Better equipped. Better prepared."

"I know."

"Then why—"

"Because I'm not them." Drevik adjusted his grip on Lexi, feeling her warmth against his side. "I'm not going to become the thing they're afraid of. I'm not going to give them justification."

"You think mercy is weakness." Lexi's voice came out thin, pained, but steady. "It's not. It's the only thing that keeps us human."

Mira turned, and in the moonlight, Drevik saw something shift in her expression. A softening. A realization.

"I didn't understand," she said. "What you were. What you could become. The Order... they told us you were dangerous. A threat. But watching you just now—" She shook her head. "You could have killed all of them. Easily. And you chose not to."

"Would you have? Killed them?"

The silence held between them. Then Mira looked away, her voice barely audible.

"I don't know anymore. I thought I did. I thought the Order was right about everything. But watching you spare people who came to murder you..." She looked back, her eyes bright. "That's not the behavior of a monster. That's the behavior of someone worth following."

"I don't want followers. I want to survive. I want the people I care about to survive."

"Then let us help." Mira's voice strengthened. "Let me prove that not everyone in the Order is your enemy. Let me show you that some of us still remember what we're supposed to stand for."

Drevik studied her face, looking for any sign of deception. He saw exhaustion, grief, and underneath it all, a desperate hope.

"Fine," he said finally. "But if you betray me—"

"I won't." She met his eyes steadily. "I'm done betraying people I believe in."

They moved through the streets in silence, a strange procession of the wounded and the weary. Behind them, Drevik heard distant sounds—footsteps, voices, the stirring of pursuit. But they didn't come closer. Not yet.

"Your mother's voice," Lexi said suddenly, her voice barely a whisper. "When you were holding the shadows... I heard it too. Faint, like an echo."

Drevik tensed. "What did it say?"

"It said—" She winced, pain crossing her features. "It said your mercy would be your greatest strength. That you inherited it from her." Her eyes met his. "You hear her voice? Your mother's?"

"Sometimes. When I use the shadows. When I'm afraid." He looked away, uncomfortable with the vulnerability of the admission. "She tells me things. Guides me. I don't know if it's really her or just my mind trying to make sense of what I'm becoming."

"Maybe it doesn't matter." Lexi's hand found his, squeezing gently. "Maybe all that matters is that it helps you choose the right path."

"Does it? The right path?" He gestured at the darkness around them, at the blood on his clothes, at the woman he loved bleeding from a wound she'd taken for him. "I've lost count of the wrong paths I've walked lately."

"Then let us walk with you." Seraphina's voice came soft and certain. "That's what the Order was supposed to be, once. People walking together, sharing burdens. Maybe it can be that again."

"Maybe." Drevik looked up at the sky, at the moon hanging heavy and silver among the stars. "Or maybe it's too late for the Order. Maybe the rot goes too deep."

"Then build something new." Mira's voice carried a fierce determination that surprised him. "Something better. Something that remembers what honor actually means."

Drevik didn't respond. He didn't have answers—only questions, only fears, only the desperate hope that they would make it through the next hour alive.

But as they moved through the shadowed streets, heading toward a confrontation he wasn't sure he could win, he felt something he hadn't felt in a long time.

Hope. Small and fragile, but present.

These women had fought for him. Bled for him. Chosen him over everything they'd known. And somewhere in that choice, in that sacrifice, he found the strength to keep moving forward.

The shadows said at the edges of his consciousness, promising power, promising revenge, promising everything he'd ever wanted. But beneath those whispers, quieter but stronger, came his mother's voice.

You are not alone, my son. You were never alone. And that is why you will survive this.

Drevik tightened his grip on Lexi's hand and stepped into the darkness, trusting that the light would follow.

The extraction point was an abandoned warehouse on the eastern edge of the district, exactly where Kestrel had said it would be. Drevik felt the shadows around it, sensed the people waiting inside—more operatives, more threats, more people who wanted to use him or destroy him.

"Stay close," he said to the women. "And be ready for anything."

Lexi's grip tightened on his hand. "Always."

They stepped through the door together, and Drevik felt the weight of what was coming settle across his shoulders like a mantle. Behind them, the shadows followed, patient and hungry, waiting for him to decide what kind of man he would become.

The answer to that question would have to wait.

First, they had to survive.

Chapter 19: Extraction

Chapter 19: Extraction

The Shadowlands had teeth.

Drevik felt them sinking into his consciousness with every step—serrated edges of hunger and malice that scraped against the barriers he'd hastily constructed around his mind. The realm knew what he carried now, and it wanted to tear it free.

"Move faster." Mira's voice cut through the twilight gloom, her usual composure fractured by urgency. Blood seeped between her fingers where she clutched her side, the wound leaking shadows instead of red. "The breach is destabilizing. We have maybe twenty minutes before it collapses entirely."

Twenty minutes. Drevik's legs burned from the sprint across the obsidian plains, his lungs gasping against air that felt more like breathing water. Behind them, the horizon had begun to curdle—darkness gathering into shapes that hadn't been there moments before.

Seraphina stumbled, and he caught her arm before she could fall. The mysterious woman who'd haunted his dreams for weeks before he'd ever met her in the flesh now looked painfully mortal—dark circles under her eyes, her silver hair tangled with debris from their flight through the Crystalline Forest.

"I can make it," she insisted, pulling away from his support. Pride sharpened her words, but exhaustion softened them. "Worry about Lexi."

As if summoned by her name, Lexi's weight shifted against Drevik's shoulder. He'd refused to let anyone else carry her after the battle at the nexus point—her wound too precarious, the shadow-poison too volatile to risk jostling. The gash across her ribs had started as something small, but the Entity's touch had corrupted it into something that pulsed with sickly violet light.

"I'm fine," Lexi muttered, the words slurring. Her auburn hair was plastered to her forehead with sweat, her green eyes unfocused. "Stop... stop looking at me like that."

"Like what?" Drevik tightened his grip around her waist, feeling the tremor that ran through her frame.

"Like you're already planning my funeral."

Despite everything—the creatures closing behind them, the threshold shrinking with every passing second, the chaos that awaited on the other side—something warm flickered in his chest. Even half-delirious with pain, Lexi was still fighting. Still fierce.

"Planning would imply I've accepted the possibility," Drevik said, adjusting her arm across his shoulders. "I don't accept anything that ends with you dead. Too stubborn."

A ghost of a smile crossed her face. "Asshole."

"Takes one to know one."

The terrain shifted beneath them—smooth obsidian giving way to crystalline formations that jutted from the ground like broken teeth. They were approaching the boundary zone, where the Shadowlands pressed closest against the mortal realm. The air here tasted of copper and ozone, and the sky above had begun to ripple with visible waves of distortion.

"There." Mira pointed toward a shimmer in the darkness—a tear in reality that flickered like a dying flame. "The threshold. It's still open."

Something shrieked behind them.

Drevik didn't look back. He didn't need to. He felt the Entities gathering, their attention fixed on him with predatory focus. The power he'd absorbed at the nexus point marked him now—made him a beacon in the eternal twilight.

Come to us, little acolyte. Let us show you what you truly are.

The voices slithered through his mental barriers, sweet and terrible. He understood now why the Elders spoke of temptation—why the Order's teachings emphasized discipline above all else. The Shadowlands offered exactly what he'd always wanted. Belonging. Purpose. Power.

You could rule here. You could burn brighter than any Veiled One. You could keep them all safe forever—all your women, all your loves. Just let go.

"Drevik." Seraphina's hand closed around his wrist, her grip surprisingly strong. "Stay with us. Whatever they're showing you—"

"How did you know?"

"Because I can see it in your eyes." She stepped closer, and for a moment the exhaustion fell away, revealing the woman who'd walked through his dreams with cryptic warnings and riddles. "The same thing calls to me. Every time I Shadow Walk, I feel it pulling harder. The realm wants us, Drevik. It's been waiting for people like us for centuries."

"People like us?"

"Those who can see beyond the veil. Those who can shape it." Her eyes held his, unflinching. "That's why we can't stay. That's why we have to run."

The first Entity materialized from the shadows to their left—a mass of writhing darkness that resolved into something vaguely humanoid, its face a smooth expanse of nothing where features should have been. It moved without sound, gliding across the crystal formations with terrible grace.

Mira's blade caught it mid-strike, shadows against shadows, and the Entity shattered like glass. But three more were already forming.

"Go!" Mira shouted. "The threshold won't wait!"

Drevik ran.

Lexi's weight pulled at his shoulder, her breathing ragged and shallow against his neck. Every step sent jolts of pain through his body, but he pushed harder, faster—his newly enhanced abilities screaming against the strain of holding two forms of magic simultaneously. The nexus power burned through his veins like liquid fire, and the Shadowlands responded to it. Paths opened before him that hadn't existed moments before. Shadows bent around his footsteps, hiding their passage.

You're learning, said a voice in his mind—not the Entities' seductive calls, but something older. Something that was like approval. Good. You'll need this.

He didn't question it. Questioning meant slowing down, and slowing down meant death.

The threshold grew larger with every stride—a wound in reality that leaked mortal light into the eternal twilight. Through it, he saw the ritual chamber they'd left behind: stone walls covered in protective sigils, the bodies of the Order's advance team sprawled across the floor, and a figure standing at the center, arms raised, surrounded by a circle of flickering candles.

Ivy.

She'd stayed behind to keep their exit open. She'd been alone for hours, maintaining a breach that should have required at least six acolytes. Her face was pale as parchment, blood trickling from her nose, her whole body trembling with the effort of holding reality apart.

"Ivy!" Seraphina's voice cracked on the name. "Hold on! We're coming!"

Through the threshold, Ivy's eyes found them—wide with relief and something else. Fear. Not for herself, but for them.

"They're coming," she gasped, her voice carrying through the breach like it came from underwater. "More of them. I can feel them gathering on this side. Drevik, you have to—"

The threshold flickered.

"NO!" Mira lunged forward, her shadows wrapping around the edges of the breach, lending it strength. "Don't you dare close it! Don't you dare—"

"Not... closing..." Ivy's words came through gritted teeth. "Holding... but something's... wrong..."

Drevik felt it too. A pressure building against the threshold from the mortal side—not Entities, but something else. Something that felt disturbingly familiar.

The Order, he realized with cold clarity. They're trying to seal us in.

"FASTER!" He poured more power into his legs, into his lungs, into the shadows that wrapped around their small group. Lexi cried out as the pace increased, her wound tearing further, but there was no choice. No option except forward.

The Entities were closing from behind. He felt them now, dozens of them, their attention fixed on the threshold like predators who'd cornered prey. If the breach closed before they reached it—

"GO!" Mira grabbed Seraphina's arm and threw her through the threshold. The mysterious woman tumbled into the ritual chamber, rolling across the stone floor, gasping as mortal air filled her lungs.

Drevik was five feet away. Four.

The threshold shrank by half.

"Drevik!" Ivy's voice rose to a scream. "I can't hold it—the Elders are pushing against me—they want it closed—"

Three feet. Two.

Lexi's body went limp against his shoulder, unconsciousness finally claiming her. Her weight increased, dragging at his stride, and he felt her starting to slip—

Mira appeared at his side, her hands closing around Lexi's other arm. "Together," she snarled, blood still seeping between her fingers from her own wound. "Don't you dare let her go."

One foot.

Drevik threw himself forward, Lexi's body clutched against his chest, Mira's hands gripping them both, and they crashed through the threshold like swimmers breaching the surface of a frozen lake.

The mortal realm hit him like a wall.

Air—real air, clean and sharp and tasting of dust and candle smoke—filled his lungs. The stone floor of the ritual chamber was cold against his back, solid and real in ways the Shadowlands never were. For one perfect moment, he simply lay there, breathing, feeling the weight of Lexi's unconscious form across his chest, the burning in his muscles slowly beginning to fade.

Then the screaming started.

Drevik scrambled upright, keeping Lexi against him, his eyes sweeping the chamber for the source of the chaos. What he saw made his blood freeze.

The ritual chamber was in ruins. The protective sigils had been torn from the walls, their silver lines now nothing but scorch marks on ancient stone. The bodies of the advance team lay scattered like broken dolls, their faces contorted in expressions of terror. And standing at the far end of the room, surrounded by a dozen acolytes in full combat gear, was Elder Kestran.

His face was unreadable, but his eyes—his eyes held something Drevik had never seen in an Elder before.

Fear.

"Secure the breach," Kestran ordered, his voice flat. "All remaining shadow-casters, reinforce the boundary. Nothing gets through."

"But the others—" one of the acolytes began.

"There are no others." Kestran's gaze found Drevik, and something flickered there—recognition, perhaps, or calculation. "The Shadowlands are lost. Everything beyond this threshold is now enemy territory."

"Lost?" Mira pushed herself to her feet, swaying slightly. Blood dripped from her side, pooling on the stone. "We just crossed through. The breach is holding. If you'd give us time to—"

"The breach is an open wound." Kestran's voice cut through her words like a blade. "Every second it remains, more of the Shadowlands bleeds through. We've already lost three acolytes to possession. The entities are using it as a doorway." He turned to the acolytes. "Seal it. Now."

"You'll kill Ivy!" Seraphina staggered forward, her earlier gracelessness forgotten in the face of the threat. "She's anchored to the threshold—if you close it while she's still connected—"

"Then she should have released her hold." Kestran didn't look at Ivy, who knelt in the center of her circle, her hands still raised, her eyes rolled back in her head as she maintained the spell that had saved them all. "The needs of the Order supersede any individual."

Something broke inside Drevik.

It wasn't anger—anger was too simple, too clean. This was something older and colder, a clarity that cut through exhaustion and pain like ice through flesh. He understood, in that moment, why the Order had survived for centuries. Why it had grown cruel.

It wasn't about protecting people. It was about protecting power.

"No."

The word fell into the chamber like a stone into still water. Every eye turned to him—acolytes, Elder, his fellow survivors. Even Ivy, lost in her trance, stir at the sound.

Kestran's expression didn't change. "You are an acolyte, Markinsoul. You do not give orders. You follow them."

"I'm not giving an order." Drevik rose to his feet, keeping Lexi cradled against his chest. His shadow stretched across the floor, responding to the power that still hummed through his veins. "I'm making a choice."

He walked toward Ivy's circle.

Every acolyte in the room moved to stop him—shadows coiling, hands reaching for weapons, faces set with the rigid certainty of those who followed orders without question. But before any of them could cross the distance, before Kestran could give the command that would end Ivy's life, Drevik's shadow moved.

It didn't spread like normal shadow. It didn't cling to the floor or hide in corners. It rose—a column of darkness that stretched from his feet to the ceiling, blocking the acolytes' path, forming a wall between them and Ivy's circle.

The room fell silent.

"Impossible," Kestran breathed. "That's not—no acolyte can—"

"I told you." Seraphina's voice was soft, but it carried in the sudden stillness. "I told you what he was becoming. What he found at the nexus." She moved to stand beside Drevik, her hand finding his shoulder. "The Shadowlands didn't want to keep him, Kestran. They wanted to follow him."

The Elder's face went even paler. "You touched the core."

"I didn't just touch it." Drevik's shadow writhed, reshaping itself into something that made the acolytes retreat another step. "I absorbed it. Part of it, anyway. Enough to understand what your Order has been missing for centuries." He met Kestran's eyes over the barrier. "The Shadowlands isn't our enemy. It's our mirror. And you've been trying to fight your own reflection."

"Pretty speeches won't save her." Kestran's voice had gone hard again, the momentary fear buried beneath centuries of discipline. "The threshold must close. If you've truly absorbed what you claim, then you know the cost of leaving it open. Every moment, more entities slip through. More innocents die. The balance—"

"Is already broken." Mira had moved to Ivy's other side, her hands positioning themselves above the unconscious woman's temples. "The breach at the nexus point changed everything. Closing this threshold won't fix it—it'll just force the pressure somewhere else. Somewhere we can't predict."

"Then we find that somewhere and we seal it too."

"You'll be chasing leaks forever." Drevik knelt beside Ivy, keeping his shadow-wall in place. "The Order's approach has been wrong from the start. You treat the Shadowlands like a disease when it's actually a circulatory system. You don't cure it by cutting it out—you heal it by changing the flow."

Kestran stared at him. For a long moment, no one moved. The only sounds were the flickering candles and Ivy's shallow breathing.

Then, slowly, the Elder lowered his hand.

"Show me."

The command hung in the air. Drevik felt the weight of every eye in the room—acolytes ready to strike if he made a wrong move, his fellow survivors swaying on their feet, Ivy's life depending on his next words.

"I need to reach into the threshold," he said. "Ivy's anchored to it—if I can extend my shadow through the connection, I can stabilize the breach without closing it. Turn it from a wound into a... a doorway. Something controlled."

"And if you're wrong?"

Drevik looked down at Lexi's face, pale and still against his chest. At Ivy, whose nose had begun bleeding freely, whose life was slipping away with every second the threshold remained unstable. At Mira and Seraphina, both wounded, both watching him with expressions that mixed fear with desperate hope.

"Then I die with them."

The words settled into his bones as he spoke them. True words. Honest words. He would rather burn himself to nothing than watch another person he loved die because he'd been too slow, too weak, too afraid to try.

Kestran studied him for a long moment. Then, impossibly, something shifted in the Elder's ancient eyes.

"Proceed."

Drevik didn't waste the opportunity.

He closed his eyes and reached—not outward, but inward, into that place where the nexus power had taken root. The core of Shadowlands essence that now lived inside him, waiting to be used. It rose at his call like a living thing, eager and hungry and terribly, beautifully vast.

His shadow extended.

Not across the floor—through it. Through reality itself, threading into the threshold where Ivy's consciousness was tangled. He felt her there, a bright thread of will holding open a door that should have been impossible for any single person to manage. She was fading, her grip slipping, but she hadn't let go. She'd held on for him. For all of them.

I've got you, he thought, wrapping his shadow around her essence. Let go now. I'll take it from here.

For a moment, nothing happened. Then, slowly, Ivy's bright thread release its hold, transferring the weight of the threshold into Drevik's waiting shadow.

It was like catching a falling mountain.

Drevik's body locked as the full force of the breach slammed through him—not pain, exactly, but pressure. Immense, crushing pressure that made every breath a battle. The threshold wanted to close. The entities on the other side wanted to push through. The laws of reality themselves screamed that this was wrong, impossible, forbidden.

Wrong, he agreed, sweat breaking across his forehead. But not impossible. Watch me.

He didn't fight the pressure. He shaped it. The power he'd absorbed at the nexus responded to his will, turning the chaotic energies of the breach into something ordered. Something controlled. Like water through a pipe, like electricity through a wire, like—

Like love through a bond.

The thought came unbidden, and with it came understanding. The Order had been wrong about so many things, but this most of all: they treated power as something to be hoarded, controlled, locked away. But power wasn't static. It was a relationship—a flow between source and recipient. The threshold wasn't a wound to be closed; it was a connection to be balanced.

Give and take. Push and pull. The way of shadows.

He felt the breach stabilize. The screaming pressure eased into something manageable, something sustainable. The doorway remained open, but now it was his doorway—controlled by his will, maintained by his shadow, powered by the core of Shadowlands essence that now lived inside him.

Drevik opened his eyes.

The ritual chamber was silent. The acolytes stood frozen, their weapons half-drawn, their faces masks of shock. Kestran had gone very still, his expression unreadable.

But Drevik only had eyes for the women in front of him.

Ivy had slumped forward the moment the weight transferred, her body finally releasing the strain it had carried for too long. Mira caught her before she could hit the ground, her earlier rivalry forgotten in the face of shared survival.

Seraphina had moved to Lexi's side, her hands hovering over the wound that still pulsed with corrupted violet light. "The shadow-poison is spreading. We need to treat this now, or—"

"I can help." Drevik's voice came out rougher than he intended, hoarse with exhaustion. "Let me try."

He didn't wait for permission. Kneeling beside Lexi, he placed his hand over her wound and pulled. The nexus power responded, drawing the shadow-poison out of her flesh and into his own shadow, where it could be safely dispersed. Lexi gasped, her eyes flying open, her body arching off the floor as the corruption left her.

"Drevik?" Her voice was weak, confused. "What... where..."

"Shh." He brushed the hair back from her face, his thumb tracing the line of her jaw. "I've got you. You're safe."

"Like hell I am." But she didn't pull away from his touch. "You look terrible."

"Look who's talking."

A ghost of her usual fire flickered in her green eyes. "I've been better." Her gaze swept the room—the acolytes still frozen, the Elder watching with an unreadable expression, the other women gathered close. "We made it?"

"We made it."

"Good." She closed her eyes again. "Because if you ever put me through something like that again, I'll kill you myself."

"Lexi—"

"I mean it." Her hand found his, squeezing weakly. "Don't ever do that to me again. Don't ever..." Her voice cracked. "Don't ever make me watch you almost die."

The words hung between them, weighted with everything they hadn't said. Everything they'd been dancing around for weeks, hiding behind rivalry and banter and the careful distance they'd maintained.

Drevik bent his head and kissed her.

It wasn't gentle. It wasn't hesitant. It was desperate and raw and tasting of blood and shadow, his lips crushing against hers like he was trying to pour everything he couldn't say into that single point of contact. For one heartbeat, she lay frozen—then her hand came up to fist in his hair, pulling him closer, kissing him back with equal ferocity.

Someone made a sound behind them. Mira, maybe, or Seraphina. Drevik didn't care. All that mattered was Lexi's mouth against his, her fingers in his hair, the fierce pounding of her heart beneath his palm.

When they finally broke apart, both gasping, her green eyes were bright with something that looked dangerously close to tears.

"You're an idiot," she said.

"I know."

"A complete and total idiot."

"I know."

"I..." She swallowed hard. "I can't believe you waited until now to do that."

"Bad timing?"

"The worst." But she was smiling, and her hand was still tangled in his hair, and for one perfect moment nothing else mattered.

Then Kestran cleared his throat.

Drevik turned, his body positioning itself between Lexi and the Elder without conscious thought. His shadow rose behind him, a silent warning.

"The threshold," Kestran said, his voice carefully neutral. "It's stable?"

"For now." Drevik rose, keeping one hand on Lexi's shoulder. "I can hold it indefinitely, but it's not a permanent solution. The breach at the nexus point needs to be addressed, or the pressure will keep building until something breaks."

"And you know how to address it?"

"I have ideas." He met the Elder's eyes steadily. "But I'll need access to the Order's archives. The real ones, not the sanitized versions they show acolytes. And I'll need help." His gaze swept the room, landing on each of the women in turn—Lexi, Mira, Seraphina, Ivy. "All of them."

Kestran was silent for a long moment. The acolytes shifted uneasily, their weapons still half-raised, their attention jumping between the Elder and the shadow-wrapped acolyte who'd just done the impossible.

"Very well," Kestran finally said. "But understand this, Markinsoul: whatever you've become, whatever power you now wield—you are still bound by the Order's laws. The threshold may be stable, but the consequences of tonight's events have only begun to unfold." His gaze hardened. "The other Elders are missing. Vanished from their chambers sometime during your... excursion. The Veiled Ones have been silent for weeks. And the breach at the nexus point is spreading faster than our projections anticipated."

The words settled into Drevik's chest like stones.

"Missing?"

"All of them. Every Elder who was not in this chamber tonight has disappeared. No sign of struggle. No trace of shadow-walking. Simply... gone." Kestran's mask slipped for just a moment, revealing the fear beneath. "Whatever is coming, Markinsoul, it has already begun. You may have bought us time with your stunt, but time is all it is."

Drevik looked down at Lexi, still pale and weak but watching him with steady eyes. At Mira, supporting an unconscious Ivy. At Seraphina, her mysterious air replaced by something raw and vulnerable.

He'd survived the Shadowlands. He'd mastered powers that should have been impossible. He'd held the threshold and kissed the woman he loved and proven himself stronger than anyone had expected.

But as Kestran's words sank in, as the weight of what came next settled onto his shoulders, Drevik understood that everything before this moment had been nothing but preparation.

The real test was just beginning.

"Then we'd better get started," he said, and his shadow rose to meet the coming storm.

Chapter 20: Confrontation

Chapter 20: Confrontation

The chamber beneath the university had never felt so small.

Drevik stood at the center of the circular room, stone walls pressing inward like ribs around a heart. Above him, the vaulted ceiling disappeared into shadow, but it was the semicircle of elevated seats that made his chest tight. Seven thrones carved from obsidian, and six of them occupied by figures whose faces he couldn't quite see—features blurred by the same veil of power that had hidden the Order's true nature for centuries.

The seventh seat stood empty.

His hands remained steady at his sides, though every instinct screamed to reach for the shadows coiling in his chest. He'd dressed carefully for this: dark shirt, no jewelry, nothing that could be construed as a challenge. But everything about his presence here was a challenge, and they all knew it.

"Drevik Markinsoul." The voice came from the center throne, a woman's voice like wind through dead leaves. "You stand before the Council of Elders accused of harboring forbidden knowledge, consorting with entities beyond the Veil, and willfully undermining the authority of this Order."

He kept his eyes forward, not bowing. Not quite.

"I stand here because you summoned me," he said. "But we all know the real reason isn't what's written in your charges."

A rustle of robes from his left. One of the Elders leaned forward, and he caught the glint of eyes behind the obscuring veil.

"You speak boldly for an acolyte who hasn't earned the right to address this Council."

"I speak because staying silent hasn't worked." Drevik let his gaze drift across the assembled Elders, counting the ones who shifted, the ones who remained still. "Three members of this Order are dead. The Shadowlands are bleeding through in places they shouldn't. And every time I've tried to report what I've discovered, I've been dismissed, redirected, or threatened."

"Threatened?" The woman in the center throne—Elder Thornwood, Lexi's grandmother, though she'd never acknowledged that connection in front of him—tilted her head. "You mistake guidance for threat, acolyte. A mistake born of youth and arrogance."

"Then let's talk about guidance." Drevik's voice hardened. "Like being guided to investigate a chapter that had already been cleansed of evidence. Being guided away from the Shadowland breaches that didn't match the official narrative. Being guided to believe that Mira's chapter had gone rogue when they were actually trying to expose what you've been doing."

The silence held like a held breath.

Behind him, the heavy wooden doors groaned open. He didn't turn—couldn't, not without showing how badly he wanted to see who had entered. But the familiar rhythm of footsteps, one set quick and determined, another measured and deliberate, told him everything.

Lexi's voice cut through the chamber like a blade.

"He's right."

The words landed in the quiet like stones dropped into still water. Elder Thornwood's veiled head turned toward the entrance, and Drevik finally allowed himself to look.

Lexi stood in the doorway, shoulders squared, her auburn hair pulled back in a severe knot that made her features look sharper. Beside her, Ivy's silver-white hair caught the candlelight, her expression unreadable but her presence a statement in itself.

"Alexa Thornwood." The Elder's voice remained flat, controlled. "This Council did not summon you."

"No." Lexi's chin lifted. "You summoned Drevik to stand trial for crimes he didn't commit while the real criminals sit on their thrones and pretend the Veil isn't tearing apart."

"Careful, granddaughter." The word dripped with ice. "Blood may grant you entrance to this chamber, but it doesn't grant you immunity from consequence."

Drevik watched the exchange with his chest tight. He'd known Lexi would come—had seen it in one of the fragmentary futures that flickered at the edges of his consciousness—but knowing hadn't prepared him for the reality of her standing against her own blood.

"I've spent my entire life being careful." Lexi stepped forward, moving to stand at Drevik's side. Not behind him. Beside him. "Careful not to embarrass the family. Careful not to ask questions. Careful not to see what was right in front of me." Her hand found Drevik's, fingers interlacing with a grip that was like an anchor. "I'm done being careful. I'm choosing to be honest instead."

Elder Thornwood rose from her throne.

The temperature in the chamber dropped. Shadows gathered in the corners, drawn by power older than the university above them. Drevik felt the pressure against his chest, the weight of authority that the Elders wielded like a weapon.

"You defy this Council?"

"I defy corruption." Lexi's voice didn't waver. "I defy lies. I defy whatever you've been doing to the Veil that's causing breaches in locations that should be protected." Her eyes found her grandmother's veiled face. "I'm not the only one."

The doors opened again.

Mira entered first, her dark skin luminous in the candlelight, the sigils of her chapter still visible on her collarbones. Behind her came others—acolytes and seekers that Drevik recognized from late-night gatherings, from said conversations, from the faction that had been building since he'd first started asking the wrong questions.

Ivy moved to Drevik's other side, her shoulder brushing his arm as she positioned herself. When she spoke, her voice carried the precise diction that made every word feel calculated.

"I have documentation."

Every eye in the room fixed on her.

She produced a leather satchel from beneath her cloak, extracting papers that had been folded and refolded, their edges soft with handling. "Correspondence between Elder Vance and the chapter in Prague. Financial records showing transfers to accounts that don't exist in any mortal banking system. And testimony"—she paused, letting the word settle—"from three seekers who witnessed the deliberate weakening of protective wards in the eastern quadrant."

The Elders stirred. One of them, a man whose throne sat at the far end, spoke for the first time.

"Where did you obtain these documents?"

"Where I always obtain information." Ivy's smile held no warmth. "From the places you forgot to look. From the people you dismissed as unimportant. From the shadows you thought belonged only to you."

Drevik felt a surge of something that wasn't quite pride, wasn't quite fear. Ivy had been gathering evidence for weeks, working in silence while he'd been playing the visible role. The trust she'd shown by bringing this forward now—

It was more than strategy. It was faith.

Elder Thornwood descended from her throne, each step deliberate, the shadows parting before her like curtains. She stopped an arm's length from Drevik, and for the first time, he saw past the veil just enough to make out the lines around her eyes, the silver hair pulled back in a style that mirrored Lexi's.

"You've assembled quite a following." Her voice had lost its otherworldly echo, becoming something almost human. "Tell me, acolyte, what exactly you believe this will accomplish?"

"I believe," Drevik said, "that the Order has been compromised from within. Not by Shadowland entities, but by people who should be protecting the Veil. I believe you've been using breaches as weapons, controlling where the shadows bleed through to eliminate enemies and consolidate power." He met her gaze through the veil. "And I believe you brought me here because I can see futures—possible paths that others can't—and that scares you."

The silence that followed pressed against his ears.

"You don't know what you're meddling with." Elder Thornwood's voice had dropped to barely a whisper. "The Shadowlands are evolving. Changing. What we've done has been necessary to—"

"Necessary?" Mira's voice cracked through the chamber. "My entire chapter was slaughtered because they discovered what you were doing with the breach in the catacombs. Don't talk to me about necessary."

Another Elder spoke from the far end—Elder Vance, Drevik remembered, the one whose handwriting appeared on Ivy's documents. "The Prague chapter was compromised. They had to be contained before their reckless experiments destabilized the entire Veil."

"Contained." Mira's hand went to the hilt of the blade at her hip. "You mean murdered. You mean sixteen seekers and acolytes who trusted the Order, slaughtered and then blamed on 'rogue elements' so no one would ask questions."

"The truth of the Veil requires sacrifice." Elder Vance's veil rippled with what might have been a shrug. "You cannot comprehend the pressures we face, the decisions that must be made—"

"Then help us comprehend." Drevik stepped forward, breaking the invisible line that separated accused from accuser. "Show us what you're protecting. Let us see the full scope of the threat so we can help you fight it instead of standing here debating whether murder counts as sacrifice."

Elder Thornwood's head turned slowly.

"You would trust us? After everything you've discovered?"

"I would trust the truth." Drevik spread his hands. "Whatever that truth is. But I won't trust orders that come wrapped in lies, and I won't follow leaders who treat members as disposable resources."

The chamber hold its breath.

Then Elder Thornwood did something that rippled through the assembled watchers like a shockwave. She reached up and pulled away her veil.

The face beneath was older than Drevik had expected, weathered by decades of struggle he couldn't imagine. Her eyes were the same green as Lexi's, but where Lexi's sparked with defiance, these held a weariness that go down to the bone.

"You want truth?" She spoke to the room, but her eyes stayed on Drevik. "Then truth you shall have. But understand—the truth I offer you today cannot be unheard. Once you know what we protect, you become responsible for protecting it as well."

She turned to face the other Elders.

"Brothers. Sisters. The time for secrecy among our own has passed." Her voice carried an edge that hadn't been there before. "We either bring this acolyte and his faction into the full light of our purpose, or we destroy them here and now and hope the shadows take their secrets before they can spread."

Drevik felt Lexi's hand tighten on his.

Elder Vance rose from his throne. "You would break centuries of tradition? Reveal our innermost workings to children who haven't even completed their training?"

"I would prevent a war within our own ranks." Elder Thornwood's voice hardened. "Something you seem determined to provoke."

"I'm preventing catastrophe." Vance's shadows gathered around him, dark and thick. "These children have already meddled with forces beyond their understanding. They've communicated with entities that should never have been contacted, opened doors that should have remained sealed—"

"Because you opened them first." Drevik's voice cut through the gathering darkness. "The breach in the library basement. You didn't contain it—you created it. You've been pulling Shadowland entities through intentionally, testing them, trying to weaponize them."

The shadows around Vance froze.

"Where did you hear this?"

"I didn't hear it." Drevik let his own shadows rise, feeling them respond to his will in a way they never had for other acolytes. "I saw it. One of the possible futures that I'm still learning to read. You've been experimenting on sentient beings, Elder Vance. Treating entities that have thoughts and feelings and families as tools to be used and discarded."

"They are monsters." The word came out twisted, ugly. "Creatures of shadow and malice that would devour our world if given the chance."

"Would they?" Drevik stepped closer, and he felt something shift in the air—a recognition from the shadows themselves. "Or have we been fighting a war that was started by the very people supposed to prevent it?"

Vance's hand shot up, shadows coalescing around his fingers in a pattern Drevik recognized as a binding. But before he could complete the gesture, Elder Thornwood moved.

She was faster than her age suggested, shadows lashing out to intercept shadows, the collision sending a shockwave through the chamber that knocked several acolytes off their feet. Drevik grabbed Lexi, steadying her as the floor shook.

"Enough!" Elder Thornwood's voice filled the room with authority that went beyond the physical. "This Council has debated in shadow long enough. I call for a vote—here, now, before witnesses. Do we continue down the path of secrets and manipulation, or do we embrace a new way? Do we trust our members with the truth, or do we rule through fear?"

One by one, the Elders turned to face each other.

Drevik watched the invisible lines shift, alliances forming and breaking in the silence. Beside him, Ivy leaned close.

"Three will support Thornwood," she said. "Two will follow Vance. One is undecided."

"Which means—"

"Whoever controls the undecided controls the Order."

He found the uncertain Elder at the end of the row—an older man whose face remained hidden, whose body language screamed discomfort. Elder Zhao, if he remembered correctly. The one who'd always taught history with a focus on the costs of secrecy.

Drevik moved before he could second-guess himself.

He crossed to Elder Zhao's throne, aware of every eye tracking his movement, aware of Lexi positioning herself to intercept if Vance tried anything. He stopped at the base of the older man's seat and looked up.

"Elder Zhao. You taught my introductory history course. You spoke about the Purification of 1847, when the Order nearly destroyed itself from within because different factions couldn't trust each other with the truth."

The Elder didn't respond.

"You said that the worst atrocities in our history came from leaders who believed they were protecting their followers by keeping them ignorant." Drevik's voice dropped. "Is this that moment? Are we standing at the edge of another Purification?"

Slowly, Elder Zhao reached up and lowered his veil.

His face was weathered, tired, but his eyes—his eyes held something Drevik recognized from his own mirror. The desperate desire to do the right thing, tangled with the fear of making things worse.

"I remember your paper on the Shadowland treaties," Zhao said. "You argued that the old agreements weren't failures—they were simply incomplete. That entities we call monsters might have been trying to communicate rather than invade."

"I still believe that."

"Then you understand why I've remained silent." Zhao's gaze drifted to where Vance stood surrounded by his shadows. "When you see something wrong, but speaking would tear apart the only organization keeping the world safe from what lies beyond... you learn to live with compromise."

"Compromise is different from complicity." Drevik kept his voice low. "There's a point where staying silent becomes the same as supporting the thing you hate. The question is whether you've reached that point."

Zhao was quiet for a long moment.

Then he stood.

"I cast my vote with Elder Thornwood," he said, his voice carrying through the chamber. "And I further move that Elder Vance be suspended from Council duties pending investigation of the allegations presented."

The chaos that followed was immediate.

Vance's shadows exploded outward, but Elder Thornwood was faster, her power slamming into his like waves against a cliff face. Drevik grabbed Lexi and dove behind one of the stone pillars, pulling Ivy with them. Around them, the chamber erupted into motion—acolytes scattering, seekers drawing weapons, the air itself seeming to tear apart with the force of the Elder's clash.

"Stay down!" Lexi shouted, but Drevik was already moving.

His shadows weren't as strong as the Elders', but he'd learned things in the past months that they hadn't. He'd learned to listen to the darkness rather than forcing it to obey. And right now, the darkness was angry.

He reached for the shadows gathering around Vance's feet—not to control them, but to understand them. And what he found there made his blood run cold.

The shadows weren't natural. They'd been bound, twisted, forced into service through methods that left them screaming at the edges of his perception. These weren't the cooperative shadows he'd learned to work with—they were slaves.

"Stop!" He launched himself forward, placing himself between the fighting Elders. "Both of you, stop!"

A bolt of shadow struck him in the chest.

He felt it like ice spreading through his ribs, cold that burned, darkness that tried to take hold. But his own shadows rose to meet it, wrapping around the intrusion and—instead of fighting—simply absorbing it.

The chamber went silent.

Drevik stood in the center of the chaos, shadows wreathing his arms, his chest, his face. He felt them like extensions of his own will, feel the bound shadows Vance had enslaved reaching toward him with something that might have been hope.

"You've been torturing them." His voice came out strange, layered with echoes. "Binding them against their will. Using pain to force obedience."

Vance's face contorted. "They are tools—"

"They are living beings." Drevik felt the shadows pulse with agreement. "And you've been treating them like property."

He reached out—not with power, but with intention. The shadows surrounding Vance hesitated, then drift away, moving toward Drevik like iron filings to a magnet. Not because he forced them. Because they wanted to.

Vance staggered as his shadows abandoned him, the sudden loss leaving him pale and suddenly ordinary.

Elder Thornwood lowered her hand, the fight going out of her posture. Around them, the other Elders watched with expressions ranging from horror to wonder.

"What are you?" Vance said.

"I'm an acolyte who listens." Drevik let the shadows settle around him like a cloak. "Who asks before he takes. Who understands that the power we wield comes from somewhere, and that somewhere deserves respect."

He turned to face the Council.

"I came here to confront you about your lies. But I'm starting to think the bigger problem is that you've forgotten what we're supposed to be." His voice steadied. "The Order of the Eclipsed Veil doesn't exist to control the Shadowlands. We exist to maintain the balance. To protect both worlds from those who would exploit either one."

Elder Thornwood studied him with an expression he couldn't read.

"You speak of balance while standing in the center of our sacred chamber, wielding power that should be beyond any acolyte, surrounded by shadows that answer to you rather than any Elder." Her voice was neutral, but something flickered in her eyes. "What would you have us do, Drevik Markinsoul? The threats we face are real. The sacrifices we've made have kept this world safe for centuries."

"Then let us help you face them." He gestured to Lexi, to Ivy, to Mira and the others who had gathered behind him. "We're not children. We're not resources to be managed. We're members of this Order who deserve to know what we're fighting for."

The silence held.

Then Elder Thornwood did something that made every person in the chamber freeze.

She knelt.

Not to Drevik—to the shadows that surrounded him, the ones that had been bound and were now free, the ones that had been used and were now heard. She knelt to them in acknowledgment of what had been done wrong.

"Your words will be taken to the full Council," she said, rising. "All of them. Elder Vance will face judgment for his actions, and the truth of our purpose will be shared with every member who wishes to hear it." Her eyes found Drevik's. "But this does not end here. The threats we face are real, and now you've made yourself visible to them. The entities that seek to breach the Veil will know your name. The powers that move in shadow will see you as either an obstacle or an opportunity."

"I know."

"And you still choose to stand in the light?"

Drevik looked at Lexi, her hand finding his again. At Ivy, whose careful mask had cracked to show something vulnerable underneath. At Mira, who had crossed an ocean and lost everything to stand in this chamber.

"I choose to stand with them," he said. "Whatever light or shadow that means."

Elder Thornwood nodded slowly. Then she turned to Elder Zhao, who had moved to stand beside her, the lines of alliance clearly drawn.

"The Council is dissolved for tonight," she announced. "Elder Vance will be confined to quarters. All evidence presented will be examined by a tribunal of neutral parties. And you"—she fixed her gaze on Drevik—"will return tomorrow. There is much to discuss about your future in this Order."

The dismissal was clear. Drevik felt the tension drain from his shoulders as the chamber empty, acolytes and seekers filing out in whispers and glances. Lexi stayed at his side, her grip on his hand almost painful.

"That was insane," she muttered as they moved toward the doors. "You stood between two Elders and absorbed shadow energy that should have killed you."

"Should it have?"

"Theoretical consensus says yes."

He smiled slightly. "Then I guess theory needs updating."

They emerged into the corridor that led back to the surface, the stone walls giving way to the familiar architecture of the university's basement level. Behind them, Ivy and Mira fell into step, the four of them moving together in a silence that felt heavy with unspoken words.

It was Ivy who finally broke it.

"Your grandmother just lost significant political standing," she said to Lexi. "And she's not the type to forgive easily."

"I know."

"And you publicly chose Drevik over your family's legacy."

"I know that too." Lexi's voice was steady, but Drevik felt the tremor in her hand. "I chose correctly."

They reached the stairs leading up to the main building, the late afternoon light filtering down through the windows above. Drevik stopped, turning to face the three women who had risked everything to stand with him.

"I need you to know—" he started.

"No." Lexi cut him off, stepping close enough that he saw the flecks of gold in her green eyes. "Whatever you're about to say about gratitude or obligation or how we didn't have to come—stop. We came because we chose to. Because we believe in what you're doing." Her hand came up to rest against his cheek. "Because you're worth following."

Drevik felt something shift in his chest—not shadows this time, but something warmer. Something that had been building for months and had finally found its moment.

"I've been terrified this whole time," he admitted quietly. "Every step, every confrontation, every time I saw a possible future where things went wrong. I kept waiting for someone to tell me I was making a mistake."

"You were making a mistake," Mira said from behind them. When Drevik turned, she was smiling slightly. "You should have let us help sooner. You should have trusted that we could handle the truth. You should have known that you don't have to carry everything alone."

"I'm learning."

"Good." Ivy's voice was dry. "Learn faster. We have an Order to rebuild."

The afternoon sun hit them as they emerged from the building, casting long shadows across the quad. Students walked past, oblivious to the ancient conflict that had nearly erupted beneath their feet. A group of freshmen laughed near the fountain. A professor hurried toward the science building with papers clutched to his chest.

Normal life. The world continuing while everything had changed.

"Drevik."

He turned at Elder Zhao's voice, finding the older man standing in the doorway, his veil gone entirely now, his face exposed to the fading light.

"A word before you go."

Drevik gestured for the others to continue, then moved back toward the entrance. Zhao's expression was unreadable, but there was something in his eyes—something that made Drevik's shadows stir in anticipation.

"I told you that I remembered your paper," Zhao said. "What I didn't mention is that I also remember your parents."

The world stop.

"They were members of this Order," Zhao continued. "Brilliant researchers who saw things in the Shadowlands that no one else saw. They believed, as you do, that the entities beyond the Veil were capable of communication. Cooperation. They spent years building bridges, learning to understand the shadow beings that we had always treated as enemies."

Drevik's voice came out hoarse. "What happened to them?"

"They discovered something." Zhao's voice dropped further. "A truth about the Veil itself that certain parties wanted buried. They tried to bring their findings to the Council, but not every Elder was interested in the truth. Some were invested in maintaining the status quo, in keeping the Shadowlands as enemies rather than allies."

"Vance."

"Vance was among them, yes. But there were others. Others who are still on this Council, still making decisions, still hiding what they know." Zhao's hand came up to rest on Drevik's shoulder. "Your parents were killed in what was described as a research accident. But I've never believed that. And I've spent eighteen years waiting for someone who might finally uncover what they died to protect."

The words landed like stones in still water.

"Why tell me this now?"

"Because tomorrow, the Council will offer you advancement." Zhao's eyes held something that might have been hope. "They'll make you a Seeker—the youngest in Order history. They'll give you access to archives and resources and training that you've earned through today's events." He squeezed Drevik's shoulder. "But that's not why you should accept."

"Then why?"

"Because your parents left something for you. A message, hidden in the archives, that only a Seeker can access. A message that I've been guarding for eighteen years, waiting for the right moment to reveal." Zhao's voice dropped to barely a whisper. "Your parents didn't just discover a truth about the Veil. They discovered the source of the corruption that's been eating this Order from the inside. And they died to keep that knowledge from the people who would use it to destroy everything."

Drevik felt his shadows surge, felt them wrap around him in a protective embrace that he hadn't consciously summoned.

"Who killed them?"

"That's not for me to say." Zhao stepped back. "But the answer is in that message. And once you have it..." His expression hardened. "Be careful who you trust, Drevik Markinsoul. Not everyone who stands with you today will remain an ally when the truth comes out."

He turned and disappeared back into the building before Drevik could respond.

The walk across campus felt longer than usual, shadows lengthening as the sun continued its descent. Drevik's mind raced through everything he'd learned, everything that had changed, everything that still lay hidden.

Tomorrow he would become a Seeker. Tomorrow he would have access to his parents' final message. Tomorrow the real work would begin.

But tonight—

Tonight, he found Lexi waiting for him at the edge of the quad, her auburn hair catching the last of the golden light, her expression soft in a way she rarely allowed in public.

"Zhao told you something," she said. Not a question.

"He told me enough to know that this is just beginning."

"It always is." She reached out and took his hand, her fingers intertwining with his in a gesture that had become natural over months of practice. "The Order doesn't do simple. The Shadowlands don't do safe. And us..." She trailed off, something vulnerable flickering in her eyes.

"Us?"

"Us is the one thing I'm sure of." Her voice dropped. "Whatever comes next, whatever truths you uncover, whatever enemies you make—I'm with you. Not because of duty or family or Order politics. Because I choose you."

Drevik felt his heart stutter in his chest.

"I've been seeing futures," he said. "Fragments of what might be. And in almost every one, you're there. Standing beside me. Fighting with me. Building something that's better than what came before."

"That's not prophecy." Lexi's smile was small but genuine. "That's intention."

He pulled her close, letting his forehead rest against hers, letting the moment stretch in the fading light. Behind them, he sensed Ivy and Mira keeping watch, giving them this small piece of privacy in a world that offered very little.

"I'm going to need all of you," he said. "Whatever's coming—it's bigger than I thought. Bigger than the Order. Maybe bigger than the Veil itself."

"Then we'll face it together." Lexi's hand came up to rest over his heart. "That's what this is, right? Not just a cult. Not just duty. Family. The kind you choose."

"The kind you choose," he agreed.

The sun dipped below the horizon, and the shadows came alive around them, reaching, listening, waiting for what would come next.

Drevik Markinsoul had entered the Order as an acolyte, uncertain and alone.

Tomorrow, he would rise as a Seeker.

And somewhere in the archives, a message from the dead waited to tell him why he'd been chosen for this path all along.

The chapter ends here, but the story continues in Book 3: The Seeker's Shadow, where Drevik will uncover the truth about his parents' fate, confront the full scope of the Order's corruption, and discover that the entities beyond the Veil have plans for him that no one could have predicted.

The shadows are listening. The Veil is thinning. And the real war is just beginning.

Chapter 21: New Bonds

Chapter 21: New Bonds

The morning after felt different.

Drevik stood at the window of his new quarters—Seeker's quarters, he reminded himself—and watched the sun climb over the university campus. From here, on the third floor of the compound's eastern wing, he saw students crossing the quad, carrying coffee cups and textbooks, utterly unaware of what lurked beneath their feet.

Three days had passed since the Shadowlands. Three days of recovery, of debriefings, of Elder Thorne's pointed questions about exactly how he'd managed to stabilize a collapsing dimensional breach while simultaneously anchoring four other people to the mortal realm.

He still didn't have a good answer for that.

A knock at the door interrupted his thoughts.

"Enter," he said, turning.

The door swung open to reveal Lexi.
