Her auburn hair was pulled back in a loose ponytail, and she wore comfortable clothes—sweats and a fitted t-shirt—rather than the formal robes most members wore within the compound. She looked human. Real. Not like the fierce warrior who had fought beside him in the darkness.

"Privacy wards?" she asked, lifting an eyebrow.

"Already active."

She nodded and stepped inside, letting the door close behind her. For a moment, she simply stood there, studying him with those green eyes that missed nothing.

"You look different," she said.

"I feel different."

"Good different or bad different?"

Drevik considered the question. His body had healed from the Shadowlands—the Order's healers saw to that—but something inside him had shifted. He felt the connections now. Four threads of light, invisible but tangible, stretching from his chest to somewhere beyond the walls of this room.

"Stronger," he said finally. "More... certain."

Lexi's mouth curved into a small smile. "That's the promotion talking. You're a Seeker now. That comes with certain privileges." She moved closer, stopping just inside his personal space. "And certain responsibilities."

"Like what?"

"Like not dying." Her voice was light, but her eyes were serious. "Like acknowledging what you've built. Who you've bound yourself to."

Drevik's breath held. They hadn't spoken about what had happened in the Shadowlands—not directly. They'd been too focused on survival, on escape. But he remembered the moment when she'd reached for him in the darkness, her hand finding his, their bond flaring bright enough to push back the void.

"Lexi, I—"

"Don't." She pressed two fingers to his lips. "Don't apologize. Don't explain. Not yet." Her hand dropped to his chest, palm flat over his heart. "I need you to understand something first."

He waited.

"I've known about the Order my entire life. I was raised to be perfect—to follow the path my mother laid out for me, to become an Elder someday, to serve the balance." Her voice was steady, but he felt the slight tremor in her fingertips. "I never questioned it. Never wanted anything else. Until you."

"Lexi..."

"Let me finish." She stepped even closer, and now he could smell the faint vanilla of her shampoo, saw the gold flecks in her irises. "I didn't expect you. I didn't want you. You were an outsider, a scholarship kid who stumbled into our world by accident. You shouldn't have mattered."

Her other hand came up to cup his jaw.

"But you did. You do. And I'm not going to pretend otherwise anymore."

Drevik's hand found her hip, pulling her against him. "I never asked you to pretend."

"No. You didn't." She smiled, and it was softer than anything he'd seen from her before. "That's why this works. That's why I'm not going anywhere."

She kissed him then—not the desperate, frantic kiss of the Shadowlands, but something slower. Deliberate. A promise rather than a claim.

When they parted, Drevik's heart was racing.

"I need to tell you something," he said. "About the others."

Lexi's expression shifted, but she didn't pull away. "I know."

"You know?"

"I felt them." Her hand pressed harder against his chest. "When we were in the Shadowlands, when our bond solidified—I felt the other threads. Mira. Ivy." She paused. "Seraphina."

Drevik tensed, waiting for anger, for jealousy. But Lexi just sighed.

"I can't say I'm thrilled about sharing you," she admitted. "But I'm not stupid enough to pretend I don't understand what happened. You anchored all of us. We're all connected now, whether we like it or not."

"Do you like it?"

The question came out rougher than he intended. Lexi considered it, her thumb tracing small circles against his jaw.

"I like that you're powerful enough to hold multiple bonds," she said finally. "I like that you didn't abandon them, even when it would have been easier. I like that you're the kind of man who can't let go of people who need him." Her smile turned wry. "I don't love the practical complications. But I can live with them."

Drevik exhaled slowly. "You're remarkable."

"I know." She stepped back, smoothing her shirt. "Which is why you need to get dressed. The ceremony is in two hours, and Elder Thorne specifically requested formal attire."

"The ceremony?"

"Your promotion, idiot." She rolled her eyes. "Try to keep up. You're about to become the youngest Seeker in three decades. That requires pomp and circumstance."

"Wonderful," Drevik muttered.

Lexi laughed, and the sound was genuine. "I'll send the others to you. Individually. You have things to discuss with each of them." She moved toward the door, then paused. "For what it's worth, Drevik—I'm proud of you. What you did in the Shadowlands... no one could have predicted that. You've earned your place here."

She slipped out before he could respond.

Ivy arrived first.

She knocked softly, and when he opened the door, she stood there with her hands clasped in front of her, looking smaller than he remembered. Her dark hair was loose around her shoulders, and she'd traded her usual practical clothing for a simple blue dress that matched her eyes.

"Can I come in?"

"Always."

She stepped inside, her gaze darting around the room before settling on him. The privacy wards hummed with his renewed power—Seeker status brought stronger magic—and Ivy's eyes widened slightly.

"You feel different," she said. "Stronger."

"The promotion has some benefits."

She nodded, but didn't move closer. Drevik saw the tension in her shoulders, the way she held herself like she was bracing for impact.

"Ivy." He crossed the room and stopped in front of her. "What's wrong?"

"I'm not..." She swallowed hard. "I'm not like the others, Drevik. I'm not a warrior like Lexi, or mysterious like Seraphina, or even..." She trailed off.

"Or even what?"

"I don't know what Mira is to you. But I know what I am." Her voice dropped to barely a whisper. "I'm just me. Quiet. Forgettable. The one who handles the books and the research and stays in the background while everyone else does the important work."

Drevik tilted her chin up, forcing her to meet his eyes. "You think you're forgettable?"

"I know I am."

"Then you're wrong."

He kissed her before she could argue—softly at first, then deeper when she melted against him. Her hands found his chest, gripping his shirt, and he felt the bond between them glowing brighter.

When he pulled back, her eyes were wet.

"I don't want to be left behind," she said. "I know you have others now. Stronger, more impressive women. But I've been with you from the beginning. I was the first person in this whole damn Order who looked at you like you were worth something. And I can't—"

"Ivy." He held her face in both hands. "You were the first person who saw me. Not the scholarship student. Not the unexpected initiate. Me. You looked past all the barriers I'd built and found the person underneath." His thumb brushed away a tear. "I'm not leaving you behind. I'm not forgetting you. You're part of this now."

"Part of what?"

"Us. All of us." He smiled slightly. "I don't fully understand it yet. But I know that when I was holding everyone together in the Shadowlands, your thread was just as bright as anyone else's. Just as important."

She sniffled, then laughed softly. "You're really not getting rid of me?"

"I wouldn't dream of it."

She pulled him into a hug, burying her face in his chest. He held her close, feeling the slight tremble in her shoulders, and pressed a kiss to the top of her head.

"Thank you," she mumbled against his shirt.

"For what?"

"For meaning it."

Mira came next.

She didn't knock—she simply appeared in the middle of his room, stepping out of a shadow like it was the most natural thing in the world. Drevik jumped, his pulse quickening, and she laughed.

"Sorry." She didn't look sorry at all. "Old habits."

"You could use the door like a normal person."

"Where's the fun in that?" She prowled closer, and there was no other word for it—Mira moved like a predator, all grace and danger. Her silver hair was braided back from her face, and she wore black leather that hugged her curves.

Drevik's mouth went dry.

"Like what you see?" She smiled, showing teeth.

"I—"

"Relax, Seeker." She stopped in front of him, close enough to touch. "I'm not here to seduce you. Well." She tilted her head. "Not primarily."

"Mira."

"Drevik." She mimicked his tone. "We need to talk about what happened in the Shadowlands."

"I know."

"I wasn't supposed to survive." Her voice was matter-of-fact, stripped of emotion. "When the Order sent me to observe you, I expected to write a report and leave. Maybe interfere slightly if things got interesting. I didn't expect..."

"Didn't expect what?"

"To care." The word dropped between them like a stone. "I didn't expect to want to stay."

Drevik studied her face, looking for the trick, the angle. That was the thing about Mira—she was always playing a game. But her silver eyes held steady, and he felt the truth of her words through their bond.

"Are you staying?" he asked.

"That depends." She stepped closer, her hand lifting to rest on his chest. "I have options, you know. The Order isn't the only game in town. I could take what I learned and disappear. Find somewhere else to be useful."

"But?"

"But I find I don't want to." Her fingers curled into his shirt. "I like being connected to you. I like the way our bond feels—like fire and ice, constantly pushing and pulling. I like that you don't trust me completely."

"You admit that?"

"Of course." She smiled, and it was genuine this time. "Trust is earned, Seeker. And you're smart enough to know that I'm still dangerous. Still unpredictable. Still not entirely on anyone's side but my own."

"Then why stay?"

"Because you're worth the risk." The answer came without hesitation. "And because I think you're going to need someone who plays by different rules. Lexi is bound by duty. Ivy is bound by loyalty. Seraphina is..." Mira's nose wrinkled. "Something else entirely. But me? I'm bound by choice. I choose to stay. I choose you."

Drevik's hand came up to cup her face. "I don't know what we are, Mira. I don't know what this becomes."

"Neither do I." She leaned into his touch. "But I'd like to find out."

He kissed her then, and she responded with a fierce hunger that made his head spin. Her hands pulled him closer, her body pressing against his, and he felt the heat of her magic mixing with his own.

When they finally parted, both breathing hard, Mira's smile was sharp.

"Good," she said. "We have chemistry. That's important."

"You're impossible."

"I'm essential." She stepped back, smoothing her leathers. "Don't forget it." She moved toward the shadows in the corner of the room. "I'll see you at the ceremony, Seeker. Try not to embarrass yourself."

Then she was gone, slipping into darkness like she'd never been there at all.

Seraphina arrived last.

She didn't knock either, but unlike Mira, she simply opened the door and stepped inside. Her dark hair cascaded over her shoulders, and she wore robes of deep purple that made her pale skin glow. Her violet eyes found his immediately.

"Drevik."

"Seraphina."

She closed the door behind her and activated her own privacy wards, layering them over his. The air shimmered with combined power.

"We need to talk," she said.

"So I've been told. Several times today."

A smile flickered across her face. "Yes, I imagine so. But our conversation is different."

She moved to sit on the edge of his bed, her movements graceful and deliberate. Drevik remained standing, not trusting himself to be that close to her.

"In the Shadowlands," Seraphina said, "something happened. Something beyond the normal bonding process."

"I felt it."

"Then you know what I'm about to say." She looked up at him, her expression serious. "Our connection isn't like the others. It's deeper. Older. It transcends the normal categories the Order uses for Heart Bonds."

"What does that mean?"

"It means I'm not just connected to you, Drevik. I'm part of you. And you're part of me." She touched her chest. "I can feel your thoughts sometimes. Your emotions. Your dreams."

Drevik's breath held. He'd felt it too—moments where Seraphina's presence in his mind was so strong it was like she was standing beside him, even when she was rooms away.

"Is that... normal?"

"Nothing about you is normal." Her smile was sad. "You know that. The Order knows that. Why do you think Elder Thorne has been watching you so closely?"

"Because I'm an outsider."

"Because you're something new." She stood, moving toward him. "I don't know what you're becoming, Drevik. But I know I want to be part of it. Whatever that means."

He reached for her hand. "You're sure?"

"I've never been more sure of anything." Her fingers intertwined with his. "I've spent my whole life searching for purpose. For meaning. I thought I'd found it in the Order, but that was just preparation. You're the real thing."

"Seraphina..."

"Don't." She stepped closer, her free hand rising to touch his face. "Don't question this. Don't doubt it. Just accept it."

She kissed him, and it was unlike anything he'd felt before—not just physical, but spiritual. Her power merged with his, their bond blazing bright enough to see even with closed eyes. He felt her soul touching his, memories and emotions passing between them.

When they parted, he was shaking.

"What was that?"

"That was us." Her voice was soft. "No barriers. No walls. Just connection."

He pulled her close, resting his forehead against hers. "I don't deserve this. Any of it."

"No," she agreed. "You deserve more. You just haven't realized it yet."

The ceremony was held in the compound's central chamber.

Drevik stood at the center of a circle of candlelight, surrounded by robed figures. Elder Thorne presided, her silver hair gleaming in the flickering glow. Behind her stood the other Elders, their faces unreadable.

"Drevik Markinsoul," Thorne intoned, "you have been called before this council to receive acknowledgment for your service. You entered the Shadowlands as an acolyte, but you return as something more."

She gestured, and two other Elders stepped forward, bearing a folded robe of dark blue.

"The rank of Seeker is not given lightly. It is earned through sacrifice, through dedication, through proof of character. You have demonstrated all these qualities." Thorne's eyes met his. "Rise, Drevik Markinsoul. Accept your new station."

He knelt, and they placed the robe across his shoulders. Power rushed through him—not new power, but power recognized, channeled, made official. The compound itself hum in response.

"I accept," he said. "I will serve the balance. I will protect the innocent. I will honor the Order."

Thorne smiled, and for once, it reached her eyes. "Welcome, Seeker. Your journey continues."

The assembled members said their approval, and Drevik rose to his feet. He caught sight of Lexi in the crowd, her arms crossed, a proud smile on her face. Beside her stood Ivy, wiping her eyes. Mira lounged against a pillar, looking bored, but her silver eyes were fixed on him with something that might have been approval.

And in the back, barely visible in the shadows, Seraphina watched with her violet gaze.

He had done it. He had proven himself.

The ceremony dissolved into conversation, into congratulations, into the business of the Order moving forward. Drevik accepted handshakes and nods, answered questions about the Shadowlands, tried to process the reality of his new position.

It was exhausting.

When he finally slipped away, heading for the quiet of his quarters, he felt wrung out. The emotional conversations of the morning, combined with the formality of the ceremony, left him wanting nothing more than sleep.

But when he opened his door, someone was waiting.

She sat in the chair by his window, a figure in shadows that seemed deeper than the dim light could account for. As he stepped inside, she rose—and he realized she was tall, taller than him, with dark skin that absorb the light and eyes that held no color at all.

"Zara Nightshade," he said, the name coming to him unbidden.

"You've heard of me." Her voice was soft, but it carried a weight that made the air feel heavy.

"The Veiled Ones are spoken of. In whispers."

"Good. We prefer it that way." She moved closer, and Drevik felt the immense pressure of her power—not threatening, but vast. Like standing next to an ocean. "I've been watching you, Drevik Markinsoul."

"Since when?"

"Since the day you were born."

He froze. "What?"

"Your parentage is not what you've been led to believe. Your grandmother raised you, yes—but she wasn't just a kindly old woman. She was one of us. One of the Veiled."

"That's not—"

"Possible?" Zara smiled, and it was neither warm nor cold. "You've learned by now that many things are possible. The Order keeps secrets. The Veiled Ones keep more." She stopped in front of him, close enough to touch. "You're becoming something, Drevik. Something that hasn't existed in centuries. The bonds you've formed, the power you've accessed, the choices you've made—they're all leading somewhere."

"Where?"

"That's what we need to discuss." Her colorless eyes held his. "You have enemies, Seeker. Not just in the Shadowlands, but here. In the Order. In the world you think you know. People who will try to use you, control you, or destroy you."

"Why tell me this?"

"Because I believe in choice." Her hand reached out, hovering near his face without touching. "Your grandmother believed in it too. She hid you away to give you that choice—to let you decide your own path. But now that path is becoming clear, and others will try to force you down it."

"And you won't?"

"I'll give you the truth. What you do with it is your decision." She stepped back, shadows swirling around her. "Sleep tonight, Seeker. Dream. And when you wake, come to the eastern tower at midnight. We have much to discuss."

"Wait—"

But she was already gone, melting into the darkness like she'd never been there at all.

Drevik stood alone in his room, his heart pounding. The promotion, the bonds, the ceremony—all of it suddenly seemed like preamble. Like the first act of a play he hadn't realized was larger than he'd imagined.

He touched his chest, where four threads of light connected him to the women who had become part of him. Whatever came next, he wouldn't face it alone.

That was the only certainty he had.

And as he lay down to sleep, the shadows in his room whisper of things to come—enemies within the Order, secrets in his bloodline, a destiny that was only beginning to unfold.

The eastern tower at midnight.

He closed his eyes, and for the first time in weeks, he dreamed without nightmares.

The dream was of light.

Four strands of it, woven together, stretching toward something vast and unknowable. And beyond them, in the darkness, eyes watched. Waited.

Zara's voice echoed through the void:

"You're becoming something new, Drevik Markinsoul. And the world isn't ready for what that means."

He woke to moonlight streaming through his window and the certainty that everything was about to change.

Again.

Chapter 22: Ascendant

Chapter 22: Ascendant

The weight of the Seeker's mantle settled across Drevik's shoulders like armor made of midnight itself.

He stood in the central chamber of the Order's compound, surrounded by the faces of those who'd shaped his journey—from skeptical initiate to whatever he was becoming now. The ceremonial robes shifted against his skin, warm despite their shadowy appearance, and power hummed beneath his sternum like a second heartbeat.

"Kneel," Elder Thornwood commanded.

Drevik dropped to one knee, never breaking eye contact with the man who'd once threatened to end him for touching his daughter. Some things you couldn't forget. Some things you couldn't forgive, either, but the Order didn't require forgiveness. It required utility.

Lexi's father pressed his palm against Drevik's forehead. Words poured from his mouth in a language that predated human speech—guttural, melodic, wrong in ways that made Drevik's hindbrain scream at him to run. He held his ground.

Power flooded him.

Not the gentle trickle he'd felt as an acolyte. This was a tidal wave, drowning out everything except the sensation of shadows wrapping around his bones, filling spaces he hadn't known existed. His Veil Sight sharpened until he saw the threads connecting everyone in the room—silver bonds of loyalty, gold threads of love, black strings of hidden enmity.

And beneath it all, the Shadowlands pressing against reality like a bruise through silk.

"Rise, Seeker Markinsoul."

He rose.

The chamber erupted in applause, but Drevik barely heard it. His attention fixed on four women standing together against the far wall—Lexi with her arms crossed and fierce pride radiating from every line of her body, Seraphina mysterious and unreadable as always, and the others he'd collected along the way. Mira, whose healing hands had saved his life twice now. Katryn, whose information networks had proven invaluable during the crisis they'd just survived.

Four women. Four bonds he felt pulsing in his chest like echoes of his own heart.

What the hell was he supposed to do with that?

The ceremony's aftermath blurred into handshakes and said congratulations, most of which Drevik dismissed with appropriate humility while his mind raced ahead. Seeker. He'd made it. One step closer to the truth about his parents, one step further into a world that seemed determined to kill him.

"You're brooding already." Lexi appeared at his elbow, her familiar presence a balm against the chaos. "Can't you enjoy five minutes of victory before planning the next campaign?"

"I'm not brooding. I'm strategizing."

"Same thing with you." She bumped his shoulder with hers, casual in a way that would have seemed impossible months ago. "My father's watching."

"Let him watch."

"He's trying to decide if you're worth the headache of having you in the family."

Drevik turned to face her fully, taking in the controlled tension in her jaw, the way her hands wanted to clench but wouldn't allow it. "What do you think?"

"I think—" She stopped, something vulnerable flickering across her features before her familiar armor slammed back into place. "I think we need to talk. All of us."

"All of us?"

She gestured toward the other three women, who'd drifted closer during their conversation. "This isn't going to work if we're all pretending the others don't exist. Whatever this is—" she waved at the space between them, encompassing bonds that defied easy explanation "—we need to figure it out before it blows up in our faces."

He'd never loved her more than in that moment. Practical, fierce, unwilling to let awkwardness fester into something worse.

"Tonight," he said. "My place. The Order's given me new quarters, and I'm told Seekers rate actual privacy."

A smile cracked through her composure. "You're moving up in the world, Markinsoul."

"Don't I know it."

Seeker quarters were a significant upgrade from the acolyte cells Drevik had called home for the past months. A sitting room with actual furniture, a bedroom spacious enough to pace in, a small kitchen, and—most importantly—wards so thick he could taste them on his tongue. Shadowland entities would find no easy entry here.

He stood in the center of his new domain, still processing the reality of his promotion. The crisis that had nearly destroyed the Order's local chapter had been resolved, but at costs he was only beginning to understand. Three acolytes dead. Elder Vance revealed as a traitor and subsequently vanished into the Shadowlands, his fate unknown. And Drevik himself...

He touched his chest where the power now lived, vast and strange. The ascension ritual had changed something fundamental. He felt possibilities branching from every choice, futures flickering at the edge of his vision like heat distortion.

A knock interrupted his contemplation.

Lexi entered first, of course. She'd never wait for permission when she could simply take it. The others followed—Seraphina gliding like smoke, Mira with her ever-present medical bag, Katryn bringing up the rear with a tablet already in hand.

"Nice place," Katryn said, her tone carefully neutral. "Bigger than my apartment."

"Perks of nearly dying repeatedly." Drevik gestured at the seating. "Make yourselves comfortable."

Nobody moved.

The air thickened with unsaid things. Drevik looked at each woman in turn, seeing clearly what he'd been avoiding for weeks. Lexi, whose feelings had grown from rivalry to respect to something deeper. Seraphina, whose mysterious connection to the Shadowlands matched his own in ways he didn't understand. Mira, whose healing touch carried intimate knowledge of his body. Katryn, whose sharp mind had saved him more times than he could count.

Four women. Four bonds. No clear path forward that didn't end with someone getting hurt.

"So," Lexi said into the silence. "This is awkward."

"Brilliant observation." Seraphina's voice carried its usual sardonic edge. "Shall we state more obvious truths? The sky is blue. Water is wet. We've all fallen for the same man."

"Not the same." Mira spoke quietly, but her words carried weight. "Different facets. Different needs."

"Still a problem," Katryn added. "Unless someone's planning to bow out."

Nobody did.

Drevik let out a breath he hadn't realized he was holding. "I don't... this isn't something I planned. Any of it. I came here looking for answers about my parents, and instead I found—" He gestured helplessly. "This. You. All of you."

"Life rarely follows plans." Seraphina moved to the window, her silhouette cutting sharp lines against the fading daylight. "The bonds we form aren't always convenient. Sometimes they're not even logical. But they are what they are."

"And what are they?" Lexi demanded. "Because I didn't sign up for a harem arrangement, and I'm pretty sure neither did anyone else."

"Would it be so terrible?" Mira's question hung in the air. "The Order has traditions—"

"The Order has a lot of traditions I don't give a damn about." Lexi's hands clenched. "I care about what's real. What's true. And what's true is that I—" She stopped, jaw tight.

Drevik crossed to her, taking her hands in his despite her obvious desire to pull away. "You."

Her green eyes met his, fierce and vulnerable in equal measure. "I love you, you idiot. I've loved you since you nearly got yourself killed saving those initiates. I've loved you through every stupid risk, every secret you kept, every moment you pushed me away because you thought you weren't worth the trouble."

His heart clenched. "Lexi—"

"Let me finish." She pulled one hand free to press against his chest. "I love you, and I know I'm not the only one. I see how you look at Seraphina. I see how Mira touches you when she thinks no one's watching. I see how you and Katryn finish each other's sentences. And I can either fight battles I'll lose, or I can figure out how to live with reality."

"It's not losing," Seraphina said. "It's expanding. There's a difference."

"Is there?"

The question hung between them, heavy with genuine uncertainty. Drevik looked around at the women who'd somehow become essential to his existence, trying to find words for feelings that defied language.

"I can't choose," he finally said. "I won't. Not because I'm greedy or indecisive, but because each of you holds something the others don't, and losing any of you would leave me incomplete in ways I can't survive." He met each of their eyes in turn. "If that makes me selfish, then I'm selfish. But I'd rather be selfish and honest than noble and lying to everyone, including myself."

silence held. Then Katryn laughed, the sound breaking tension like a stone through glass.

"Finally. Someone said it." She tucked her tablet away. "For the record, I've run the numbers on this kind of arrangement. Success rates are higher than monogamous partnerships within the Order—something about distributed emotional load and multiple perspectives on problem-solving."

"Romantic," Lexi muttered, but a smile tugged at her lips.

"I'm a data person. Sue me."

Mira moved to Drevik's side, her hand finding his. "We'll figure it out. Together. That's what matters."

Seraphina remained by the window, her expression unreadable. "There will be complications. Jealousy. Misunderstandings. Times when one of us needs something the others can't provide."

"Name a relationship without those things," Lexi countered.

"Point taken."

The evening light caught Seraphina's features, illuminating something beneath her usual composure. Drevik crossed to her, conscious of the others watching.

"What do you need?" he asked quietly.

"Something I'm not sure you can give." Her fingers found his, cool and certain. "But I'm willing to wait and see."

"That's not an answer."

"No. It's not." She pressed closer, her breath warm against his ear. "But this is."

Her kiss was different from Lexi's fire—deeper, slower, carrying the weight of shadows and secrets. Drevik felt the Shadowlands respond, power stirring in recognition of something he couldn't name. When they separated, her smile held genuine warmth beneath its mystery.

"Later," she promised. "When we have time to do this properly."

Night fell in earnest, and with it came the responsibilities Drevik had been promoted to bear.

A Seeker's duties, he discovered, were less glamorous than the title suggested. Reports to review, patrol schedules to coordinate, the endless administrative machinery that kept the Order functioning. He bent over his desk in the small hours, trying to make sense of supply requisition forms, when a presence stirred at the edge of his awareness.

Not hostile. Familiar.

"You should be resting." Mira's voice came from the doorway, soft with concern. "The ascension takes a toll."

"So does paperwork, apparently."

She crossed to his desk, her healer's hands pressing against his temples. Cool relief spread through his skull, easing a headache he hadn't consciously registered.

"You're pushing too hard."

"There's a lot to push through."

"Always will be." Her fingers traced down to his shoulders, working knots with professional efficiency. "But you're no good to anyone if you burn out in your first week."

He caught her hands, pulling her around to face him. In the dim light, her features held the same quiet strength that had drawn him to her from the beginning—steady, grounding, asking nothing while giving everything.

"Mira."

"Drevik." Her smile held gentle amusement. "If you're about to apologize for something, don't. I chose this. All of it."

"Did you? Choose?"

"I walked into that warehouse knowing I might die. I stayed when I could have transferred to another chapter. I'm standing here now, knowing you have feelings for three other women, and I'm not walking away." She freed one hand to cup his cheek. "That's choice. Every single moment of it."

He turned his head to press his lips against her palm. "You deserve better than being one of four."

"Probably." Her thumb traced his cheekbone. "But deserving and wanting rarely align. I want you. The rest is logistics."

"Logistics." A laugh escaped him, surprised by his own lightness. "Is that what we're calling it?"

"What would you call it?"

He pulled her into his lap, her weight settling against him with easy familiarity. "I'd call it the most complicated thing I've ever attempted. I'd call it terrifying and wonderful and probably doomed to spectacular failure."

"Romantic."

"I'd call it—" He stopped, searching for words. "I'd call it worth the risk."

Her kiss tasted like healing herbs and quiet devotion. She moved against him with the easy confidence of someone who knew his body intimately, and he let himself sink into the sensation, paperwork forgotten, responsibilities fading to background noise.

"Bed," she said against his mouth. "You need actual rest, and I'm not having you fall asleep at that desk."

"Is that a medical opinion?"

"It's a demand from someone who's seen what sleep deprivation does to newly ascended Seekers." She stood, pulling him with her. "Come on. I'll stay. Make sure you actually sleep."

"Just sleep?"

Her smile turned wicked. "Maybe not just sleep. But sleep eventually."

Dawn found him tangled with Mira in sheets that smelled like her herbs, her steady breathing a counterpoint to his own thoughts. He slipped from bed without waking her, padding to the sitting room in search of coffee.

Lexi was already there, mug in hand, reading something on her phone.

"Early," she said without looking up.

"Couldn't sleep."

"Liar. You slept fine until you snuck out." She finally met his eyes, amusement warring with something deeper. "Mira looks peaceful when she's unconscious. Less worried."

"She carries a lot."

"We all do." She set her phone aside. "Katryn wants to set up a household management system. Color-coded calendars, designated nights, the whole bureaucratic nightmare."

"That sounds... practical."

"It sounds like Katryn." Lexi stood, crossing to him with purpose. "But before we turn this into a committee meeting, I need something else."

"What?"

"This."

Her kiss hit like a thunderclap, all the fire he associated with her packed into a single moment. She pressed him against the kitchen counter, hands everywhere, claiming what she'd staked out with unmistakable clarity.

"Mine," she said against his lips. "Regardless of who else you're with. Some part of you is mine."

"Lexi—"

"Don't argue. Just accept it." She pulled back, eyes fierce. "I'm not good at sharing. I'm making an exception because you're worth the exception. Don't make me regret it."

"I won't."

"See that you don't." She kissed him once more, hard and demanding, then stepped back as if nothing had happened. "Now. Coffee's getting cold and Katryn will be here in twenty minutes with charts."

The day unfolded in a blur of orientation and duty. Drevik's first official Seeker assignment: investigating a Shadowlands breach in the university's library, minor enough to be handled alone but significant enough to prove his new status. He walked through the stacks, power humming through him, and found the thin place between worlds with an ease that would have been impossible weeks ago.

The entity that slipped through was small—barely a nuisance—but he dispatched it with efficiency that surprised even him. New strength, new senses, new responsibilities.

He returned to find his quarters transformed. Someone—probably Katryn—had reorganized the space into something more livable. Someone else—Mira's touch, he suspected—had added plants and soft textiles. Lexi's weapons rack now occupied one corner, her presence declared through steel and leather.

Four women, making their marks on his space. His life.

The thought should have terrified him. Instead, it was like coming home.

Night fell again, and with it came the message that would change everything.

Drevik stood at his window, watching shadows shift in ways only he could perceive, when the air itself whisper. A presence pressed against his wards—not hostile, but familiar in a way that made his chest ache.

The voice came from everywhere and nowhere.

"My son."

He froze. Recognition flooded him, bone-deep and impossible.

"Mother."

"They're coming for what you carry. The truth you seek is buried deeper than you know. Your father and I hid you for a reason—hid what you are."

"What am I?"

"The key. The bridge. The final piece of a war that started before your birth." A pause, weighted with sorrow. "Trust no one with this truth—not even the women you love. Some secrets must remain hidden until the final ascension."

"Where are you? Let me help you—"

"I'm beyond your help. Beyond everyone's help. But you—you have a chance to finish what we started. To end the shadow war before it consumes everything."

The presence fade.

"Wait! Tell me more!"

"Find the Veiled One's journal. It holds the answers you need. And Drevik—"

The voice dropped to a whisper.

"The heart bonds you've formed are real. Don't let the Order's politics cheapen them. But remember—anyone can be turned. Anyone can be broken. The only person you can trust completely is yourself."

Silence. The presence vanished entirely, leaving Drevik alone with shadows that suddenly seemed darker, deeper, more threatening than before.

He stood motionless for a long moment, processing. His mother—alive, or something like it. A war he'd never known about. A truth about himself that others would kill to possess.

The door opened behind him. Lexi's voice, sharp with concern.

"Drevik? What's wrong?"

He turned to face her, and the others gathering in the doorway. Four women who'd become essential to his survival, his sanity, his heart. Four potential weaknesses, according to his mother's warning.

But also four strengths. Four anchors. Four reasons to keep fighting.

"Nothing," he said, and the lie tasted like ash. "Just adjusting to the new normal."

She studied him, clearly unconvinced, but let it pass. "Katryn made dinner. Try to eat something before you collapse."

He nodded, following them to the small kitchen table, taking his place among them as they argued over seating arrangements and schedules and all the mundane logistics of building a life together.

His mother's warning echoed in his mind.

Trust no one.

But he looked at Lexi's fierce protection, Seraphina's mysterious devotion, Mira's quiet strength, Katryn's brilliant mind—and knew he couldn't follow that advice. Wouldn't. Some risks were worth taking.

He just hoped he wouldn't live to regret it.

The shadows outside his window deepened, and somewhere in the distance, something howled.

The next chapter of his ascent was about to begin.

End of Book Two
